# 20. Scaling, team and processes

## Readiness to scale and scaling priorities

### Gate 1: Working, Profitable Live Store

- Begin scaling only after the system works. The store must be live, attracting traffic, generating sales and producing real income. [L0190.001]
- Before scaling, check that the business is still tied to a diagnosis. Revenue that swings from month to month without a clear cause, and sales that stall around $1 million per year, are typical signs of inconsistent and disconnected marketing. These symptoms point to a missing system rather than a problem with the market alone. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0414.051]
- Use this procedure to set up the business at its current stage:
  1. Set up the business for success at its current stage (grow phase).
  2. Read your numbers regularly and understand what they show.
  3. Use those numbers to decide how to scale the business. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0449.013]
- Scaling has three focus areas: increasing order volume, improving efficiency and maximizing what already works. [L0190.002]
- Scaling does not mean restarting operations. Layer new strategies on top of your existing traffic generation, customer communication, order processing and supplier relationships. [L0190.003]
- Consistency matters more than aggressive scaling. Do not scale daily spend beyond 2x until 2-3 weeks of proven profitability are observed, even if day one shows profit. Aggressive scaling requires supporting systems (payment gateways, fulfillment, supplier capacity) that may not be in place. [L0321.029]
- Scale slowly rather than pushing volume hard on the front end. A weak backend, such as unresolved account problems or risky operations, can collapse a business that is scaling fast. Check the backend before each scaling step. [L0906.021]
- Keep improving a store that makes only a few thousand dollars net profit per month before starting another store. A struggling store can be made profitable by repeating proven steps and looking for missed items. (Older advice, 2021.) [L0962.134]
- Gross sales are not profit. Tens of thousands of dollars in monthly sales means gross sales, not net profit. Stores at $5,000 to $15,000 a month should focus on the main growth goals rather than on other channels. (Older advice, 2019.) [L0357.058]

### Gate 2: Core Setup Running at Full Potential

- Pursue advanced scaling tactics only after all of the following are true:
  1. Core strategies are fully implemented.
  2. The store is live with consistent traffic and sales.
  3. Performance limits have been reached with current channels: supplier expansion, Google Shopping Ads and remarketing. [L0194.002]
- Begin refining, tweaking and expanding strategies only once suppliers and traffic are truly maxed out. Advanced tactics belong in later stages, after the fundamentals are exhausted. [L0195.002]
- Add advanced levers only after the core setup runs in sequence at its full potential and results are already where you want them. Extra levers squeeze out the last gains; they do not fix a weak base. [L0544.001]
- Keep the core scaling levers maxed out before adding advanced strategies. Layering on extra tactics too early tends to overwhelm the operation without adding necessary results. [L0512.002]
- Advanced techniques are not part of the basic program. Discovery and Demand Gen (formerly Discovery for lead generation), and search and video ads, are added once a store has suppliers and steady sales. [L0472.066]

### Gate 3: Operations Hold at Current Volume

- Problems scale with sales. If customer service, shipping, back orders, refunds, chargebacks or cash flow already struggle at current volume, multiplying sales will multiply those problems too. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0632.010]
- Fix existing operational problems before pushing for more sales. Scaling marketing and sales alone is not enough, because operations, team and finances must grow with it. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0632.011]
- Scaling only marketing and sales while leaving operations, team and finances unchanged can make customers angry and eventually end the business. Success itself can become the cause of failure. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0632.012]
- Scaling multiplies problems as well as sales. Ten times the revenue brings ten times the cash flow strain, customer service load and chargebacks. Chargeback volume that grows too high can get a merchant account cancelled. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L1458.005]
- Maintenance comes before growth. Maintenance work keeps current sales and fulfillment running: customer service, website upkeep and product management. Growth work increases sales through marketing and team building, and it should only be added once maintenance runs reliably. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L1334.043]
- Answer every pre-launch checklist item with Yes or No. Any No means that item is unfinished, so fix it before switching on paid traffic. The store is ready for ads and real visitors only when every item is a Yes. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L1145.052]

### Supplier Choice Risk

- A battery-operated product was bought from a supplier in China for 46 US dollars plus 30 US dollars shipping, bringing the total to 76 US dollars. The buyer then received an import bill of about 38 euros or pounds from FedEx, which made the product too expensive to sell. The example shows why Chinese dropshipping leads to surprise costs, delays and unhappy customers. [L0359.103]

### Team and Outsourcing Timing

- Do not outsource or automate business processes until the business is profitable. Outsourcing and automation before that point add cost and complexity that an unproven store cannot carry. [L0555.007]
- The outsourcing and automation module is for stores that already get traffic and sales and want to hand off as much manual work as possible. Stores that are not yet running should not prioritize it. [L0373.023]
- Growth requires new skills in stages: first product research, copywriting, video editing and Facebook ads, then hiring and managing people. Plan for team building as part of scaling. [L1128.025]
- Revenue growth and team expansion reinforce each other: more revenue enables hiring, and a larger team drives more revenue. This cycle cannot reverse except through deliberate downsizing. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0107.005]
- Once revenue grows past six figures, the tactics that worked before stop working. The business needs a team and automation to reach seven figures. Processes must be repeatable so the business runs whether a given person is present or not. [L0337.016]
- Scaling requires always tackling a bigger problem than the last one, since bigger problems bring bigger rewards when solved. Each level of growth needs new tools and new help. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L1516.016]

### Lever 1: Suppliers and Products

- Focus on two levers first: securing as many suppliers as possible in your niche, and scaling your traffic. Together these are the main drivers of revenue growth at this stage. [L0512.001]
- These two levers alone are sufficient to reach five-figure monthly revenue, and then to scale toward six figures and beyond. [L0195.001]
- Supplier minimum to launch: 2 suppliers with at least 20 products between them. Continue getting approvals in the background while running early ads. (Older advice, 2022.) [L0249.083]
- Keep approving more brands in the background, even after launch, while the store runs on its first suppliers. (Older advice, 2022.) [L1113.082]
- Growth comes from continuously adding new deals and working with more suppliers. Store maintenance is ongoing, while adding suppliers and products is the main growth engine. (Older advice, 2019.) [L1068.078]
- Once a store runs, products are continuously added as new suppliers are signed. This is ongoing work, not a one-time setup. [L0472.065]
- Once the store has real products and live traffic, re-contact suppliers who declined earlier. Their previous "not right now" answer may have changed now that you can show results. [L0938.011]
- Running a store that already sells well without changing it usually caps its growth. Keep testing new suppliers, products, offers and traffic sources, because adding a few strong suppliers can sharply raise sales. [L0413.054]
- Methods for finding suppliers outside standard criteria should only be used after the niche verification and supplier research steps of the blueprint are complete, typically at module four or beyond. Use them when you need more suppliers, more products or less competitive products within a niche that is already working. [L1125.019]

### Lever 2: Traffic

- Early-phase focus order:
  1. Decide which products to upload first.
  2. Upload the top 20 percent of best sellers.
  3. Finish the product page optimizations of the optimization module.
  4. Then start the paid campaigns. [L0856.107]
- Spend most time on paid traffic early, around 70 to 75 percent, because it gives fast traffic and sales turnaround. SEO is a longer-term investment. [L0856.106]
- Product SEO comes first. Product page SEO has the easiest early wins and should be done before blog or backlink work. Ranking product pages does not require backlinks, though relevant backlinks help. [L0856.102]
- Growth marketing covers these areas as a continuous marketing system after launch (older advice, 2021-22):
  1. Search engine optimization (SEO).
  2. Content marketing.
  3. Backlink building.
  4. Email marketing.
  5. Retargeting.
  6. Pay-per-click advertising on Google Ads and Bing Ads.
  7. Copywriting, including product copy that sells.
  8. Affiliates and the Dream 100 outreach method.
  9. Customer multiplier funnels.
  10. Financial management and knowing your numbers. [L0912.011]
- The growth phase starts at launch and continues for as long as the business runs. It is a continuous marketing system, not a one-time task. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0912.010]
- A dropshipping business does not need to complete every growth marketing area before it can earn sales. These can be worked through at the owner's own pace, and adding new tactics as the business grows helps prevent early growth plateaus. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0912.014]
- Set the first ad budget by what you are comfortable testing, not by a click target. Typical launch budgets are $10 to $30 per day. About 10% of total ad spend goes to remarketing. Example: $20 per day gives a $600 monthly budget, with $60 going to remarketing. [L0971.056]
- Scaling ads means increasing spend gradually once a store is profitable. Fear of spending more on ads is the main thing that holds back growth from about 100,000 USD to a million USD per month. (Older advice, 2020.) [L1277.115]
- A $1M annual revenue goal at 10x ROAS needs about $100,000 total ad spend in the worst case. That spend is built up gradually over 12 months, not paid upfront. [L0808.035]

### Lever 3: Efficiency and Order Value

- Grow revenue without losing margin in three steps:
  1. Add profitable products or suppliers.
  2. Expand into additional traffic sources or streams.
  3. Raise average order value or customer lifetime value. [L0858.009]
- Build the offer so that ROAS can fall and the campaign still stays profitable. The margin cushion decides whether a drop in performance is survivable. Products with a healthy margin can ride out seasonal dips, while thin margins turn the same dip into a loss. [L0999.081]
- Growing from 10K to 50K monthly revenue at a $1,000 average order value means lifting sales from 10 to 50 orders per month. [L0934.083, L0934.117]
- Choose the lever by diagnosis, not by habit. [L0934.083, L0934.117]

| Diagnosis | Lever |
|---|---|
| Few suppliers, but traffic already optimized | Add suppliers [L0934.083, L0934.117] |
| Many products, but weak ad performance or unclear ad profitability | Optimize ads and product pages while raising budget [L0934.083, L0934.117] |

- Scaling ads and efficiency work comes after the core levers are maxed out (see Gate 2). [L0195.002, L0544.001]

### Revenue Milestone Ladder (Source A)

- Progressive revenue targets: five figures per month as the first milestone, six figures per month as the second, with membership in the 100K Club as the next achievement level. [L0195.005]
- Common revenue milestones for dropshipping businesses are five figures per month, then six figures per month, then beyond. Reaching six figures per month is the point at which a business is commonly considered to have entered the 100K club. [L0512.004]

| Step | Monthly revenue | Next requirement |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Five figures | Maxed-out suppliers and traffic before advanced tactics [L0195.001, L0195.002] |
| 2 | Six figures (100K Club) | Team and repeatable processes [L0337.016] |
| 3 | Beyond six figures | Team and automation to reach seven figures [L0337.016] |

- Coaching and product development show that a store needs a strong early product base before scaling. [L0472.065]

### Milestone Ladder: Step-by-Step Growth (Source D)

- Scale in steps: first earn around 1 USD a day, then 100 USD a day, then 1,000 USD a day, before scaling up further. Jumping straight to around 10,000 USD in a single step is risky because the operator has not yet learned how to manage that money and those processes. [L0631.009]
- Getting from zero to roughly $500 to $1,000 in daily revenue is the hardest phase of scaling. After the first roughly $1,000 day, growth mostly comes from raising the ad budget and adding more ads to proven products. [L1454.018]
- Focus by revenue stage:

| Stage | Main focus |
|---|---|
| Beginner (no sales yet) | Foundation building, product research, store setup |
| First sale achieved | Consistency, fixing metrics, understanding data |
| $500/day | Scaling, creatives, A/B testing, angles |
| $10k/month and up | Operations, hiring, systems, LTV, building, brand |
| $100k+/month | Pure scaling strategy, backend systems, exit potential |

[L1323.009]

- Advice and strategy should match the revenue stage a store is at. A beginner needs foundation work, while a store doing several hundred dollars a day needs scaling and testing. Mixing stage-specific tactics across levels slows progress. [L1323.010]
- Phase 6, Optimization & Scaling (Days 75-90), targets consistent monthly revenue between $5,000 and $20,000 from a single proven product. [L0199.014]
- To scale a winning product in Phase 6 (Days 75-90), execute these steps in order:
  1. Identify and fix funnel bottlenecks.
  2. Produce new creative variations every week to maintain ad freshness.
  3. Double down on the winning advertising angle by allocating more budget to it.
  4. Launch retargeting ads to recover abandoned carts and engage repeat visitors.
  5. Add upsells and bundles to increase average order value.
  6. Introduce subscription or recurring revenue products (MOO) to build predictable monthly income. [L0199.015]
- Week-by-week roadmap for a new branded dropshipping store:
  1. Weeks 1-2: foundations and store setup.
  2. Weeks 3-4: product research and validation.
  3. Weeks 5-8: ads and testing frameworks.
  4. Weeks 7-8: scaling with systems (MRR and retention).
  5. Week 9 onward: advanced strategies, automation and long-term growth. [L0914.017]

### Milestone Ladder: Brand Targets (Source C)

- The revenue goal for the brand this year is 100,000 in revenue, with growth in following years through better brand deals and supplier terms. [L0492.061]
- The speaker names 30,000 to 40,000 USD of sales per day as the scale that successful dropshippers aim for. At that level, doing the work alone is very difficult. [L1128.024]

### Launch Preconditions Before Paid Traffic

- Procedure to prepare a store for launch:
  1. Set a launch date and communicate it to all suppliers.
  2. Email each supplier to request product information, pricing and product images before the launch date.
  3. Upload products for all approved suppliers, and keep adding products after launch.
  4. Start ads once the first two suppliers' products are uploaded and the site is ready. [L0972.090]
- Early days of store launch may take 30 minutes. Later days with more complex tasks may take 2-3 hours. Following the 21-day launch plan targets a store live with traffic within 21 days. (Older advice, 2021.) [L0270.044]
- Done-for-You store builds take between 2 and 3 weeks. Speed may vary with the queue. Assume 2-3 weeks, but builds may complete faster if there is no backlog. [L0278.031]
- Pre-launch checklist: see Gate 3. [L1145.052]

### When Not to Add Advanced Tactics

- The self-contained program is designed to build a profitable business without additional programs. Advanced strategies are optional rather than necessary. [L0194.001]
- Do not add advanced strategies if the store is still in build or stabilization phase, is currently scaling profitably with existing methods, or is already delivering the target results. [L0194.004]
- Do not rush into advanced strategies too early or overwhelm yourself with tactics you do not yet need. Based on extensive field experience, this premature escalation is a recurring error. [L0195.003]
- Do not add advanced tactics before the core is maxed out (see Gate 2). [L0512.002, L0544.001]
- Do not outsource or automate before the business is profitable (see Team and Outsourcing Timing). [L0555.007, L0373.023]
- Where sources disagree on timing:
  - Source D times advanced strategies by calendar, starting at week 9 onward, and includes subscription products in Phase 6 (Days 75-90). The calendar is a reference point; check the conditions in Gate 2 before starting. [L0914.017, L0199.015]
  - Source A and Source C time advanced tactics by conditions: a live store with consistent sales, and maxed-out suppliers and traffic. The conditions decide, not the calendar. [L0194.002, L0195.002]
- Growth marketing advice from 2021-22 says to keep adding new marketing tactics as the business grows, to prevent plateaus. This is older advice and applies to growth marketing areas, not to advanced tactics before the core is maxed out. [L0912.014]

## Owner time, task audit and what to delegate

### Why owner time is the scarce resource

- A business that depends on the owner's daily involvement stops when the owner is unavailable. The goal is a business that keeps running without the owner in day-to-day operations, which is the main reason to document and delegate early. [L0337.001]
- Build systems so the business keeps running when the owner is not working in it. A business that depends on constant personal effort does not scale. [L0631.007]
- Scale by building backend systems for customer retention, so the business keeps running without the owner doing daily work. [L0914.016]
- *Older advice (2021-22):* Scaling is easier and more fulfilling than remaining solo, because the owner can focus on work they enjoy rather than handling every task, including those they dislike. [L0107.006]
- *Older advice (2021-22):* To perform higher-value work, you must build a team. Scaling emerges naturally when you focus on meaningful work rather than pursuing it as a direct goal. [L0107.004]
- Running the business alone is possible for years. Outsourcing repetitive daily tasks is a lifestyle choice rather than a requirement. [L0968.102]
- Build the business as a lifestyle business. A single store can run part-time with about 15 to 20 minutes of work per day, and full-time work on one store suggests tasks should be outsourced. [L1111.162]
- Recurring daily tasks are what consume the owner's time, so they are the first candidates for automation or delegation. The owner's time is better spent on growth, finding new opportunities and other priorities. [L0592.003]
- Busy work that does not contribute to results should be removed from the business, not just performed more efficiently. Eliminating it is one of the fastest ways to stand out from people who have struggled for years. [L1039.021]
- Growth stalls when the owner keeps senior-level tasks that are not worth their time or energy. Sustainable growth needs a team of people who outperform the owner in specific areas of the business. [L1129.001]
- Income grows through leverage, which comes from working less in the business, not working longer hours. Leverage is built by hiring staff and learning to manage them. A business earning around 250,000 dollars a year cannot grow if the owner does every task, so delegation frees time for new ideas and new businesses. [L1513.010]
- Parkinson's law says work expands to fill the time available for it. Owners who work 40 to 60 hours a week can test this by deliberately cutting their working time, for example to 10 hours in one week, and often find the same output gets done. Use the test to find unnecessary work and tighten deadlines. [L1513.011]
- *Older advice (2021-22):* Outsourcing and personal effectiveness are closely linked for an owner-operator, because delegating tasks frees time and focus for the work only the owner can do. [L0802.009]
- *Older advice (2021-22):* Owner-level preparation, such as time management, prioritization and focus, comes before scaling tactics, because a business cannot grow faster than its leader's capacity to execute. [L0802.010]
- The owner's role shifts from running the business to building it. [L0337.041]

### Owner time and hourly value

- Calculate your hourly time value and multiply it by the weekly hours you spend in your current role. If an agency costs less than this weekly opportunity cost and frees you to work on higher-impact activities, the financial math improves. [L0217.011]
- Example: if you spend 10 hours weekly managing ads at five hundred dollars per hour, your time costs five thousand dollars weekly. Outsourcing frees that value to redirect toward higher-impact work such as supplier negotiations or product development. [L0217.012]
- Estimate how long a task should take by doing it yourself and then allowing for the staff member's experience. A product upload may take five to ten minutes, and experienced staff can be faster than the owner. Use a reasonable average and reject unreasonable estimates, such as a 500-word article in half an hour. [L1396.053]
- Outsourcing a task costs money, but the freed time usually lets the business do more growth work, so the extra activity can repay the cost over time. Refusing to outsource to protect every dollar of profit can limit growth and reduce total profit in the long run. [L1247.024]
- Outsourcing early can speed up progress considerably, even when the outsourcing cost is hard to carry and leaves little profit in the short term, because the business reaches a better position sooner than it would by doing everything itself. [L0499.002]

### Task audit: list, measure and test

#### Step 1: map the work

- Decide what you actually need before anything else. The four-step method to free up working time is applied in this order: [L0827.001]
  1. Definition: decide what you actually need.
  2. Elimination: remove everything you can get rid of.
  3. Automation: automate what can run without you.
  4. Liberation: outsource what remains and cannot be automated.
- Work time is drained by three groups of tasks: minimize expenses, material items, responsibilities, accounting and daily routine tasks. Automate lead generation, ad management, FAQs, email responders, call tracking numbers, order emails and passive income. Liberate by handing work to virtual assistants, phone support, a bookkeeper, partners, or by selling the store and living on investment income. [L0827.003]
- Build a task chart before hiring: [L0337.002]
  1. List the main business areas (in the example: operations, customer service, sales, product support, sales and marketing, accounting).
  2. Break each area into small, specific tasks, e.g. "answer the phone" instead of "customer support".
  3. Color-code who does each task: tasks the owner does are blue; tasks the owner does 100% alone get a heavy dark red outline.
  4. When a task is handed to someone else, recolor it green so the owner can see what has left their plate.
- To choose which tasks to outsource, work through these steps in order: [L1122.028]
  1. List every task you perform to run the business day to day.
  2. Record how many hours per week each task takes.
  3. Focus on the pain points, which in dropshipping are usually repetitive tasks such as product uploads during setup, or order placement and payment processing when sales are high.
  4. Outsource the simplest repetitive tasks first.
- Review the task lists of each priority in the 180-day plan (up to five lists). Ask how much of the work the owner should really be doing, and which tasks could be handed to someone else. [L1247.019] *(Older advice, 2021-22.)*
- Plan a delegation: [L1514.016] *(Older advice, 2021-22.)*
  1. Identify the task and ask whether only you can do it, not whether you are the best person for it.
  2. If not, plan when it can be handed off.
  3. Identify where and how to find someone to do it and the options available.
  4. Create SOPs and training materials.
  5. Hand the task over when the timing is right, or set a date if it is not possible immediately.

#### Step 2: test each task against the criteria

- Test each task on the task list against seven criteria: [L1247.008] *(Older advice, 2021-22.)*
  1. The task is simple and repeated.
  2. The task is urgent but not important.
  3. The task is neither urgent nor important.
  4. The task is time consuming for small returns.
  5. The owner is not good at or does not enjoy the task.
  6. The task is unpredictable and disruptive.
  7. The task is affordable to outsource.

  A task only needs to meet ONE of these criteria to be a candidate for outsourcing.
- Delegate any recurring task that takes more than one hour per day of the owner's time and does not need the owner specifically. The owner's top priority as a business leader is delegation, not doing everything. [L1514.014] *(Older advice, 2021-22.)*
- Never delegate a task you would not do yourself. Only hand off work whose results you can judge. [L1515.030]
- Outsource tasks as early as possible when you lack natural strengths for them or do not enjoy them, and create space for others to contribute. Keep the work that fits your strengths for yourself. [L0701.033]
- Work that does not come naturally to you or that drains you should be handed to someone else. Every time work must be converted into a form that another person has to handle, value is lost, so fewer conversions give a better return. [L0701.031]
- A task the owner keeps postponing or dreads is a sign it should be outsourced, even when it is important to the business. [L1247.012] *(Older advice, 2021-22.)*
- Tasks the owner dislikes or is weak at tend to be avoided and never completed, so they should be outsourced even if they are important, such as content writing for SEO or managing ads. [L1247.011] *(Older advice, 2021-22.)*
- Do not outsource a task only because you dislike it. Some tasks the owner must personally keep, especially those that build key relationships. [L1122.010]
- Unpredictable tasks that pop up and need quick handling, such as phone calls and online chats, should be outsourced to protect the owner's focus. [L1247.013] *(Older advice, 2021-22.)*
- Small optimizations such as product data, product descriptions and SEO metadata help but are not big growth levers, so they are good outsourcing candidates for time-consuming, low-return work. [L1247.010] *(Older advice, 2021-22.)*
- Customer service is urgent but usually not important, and it does not grow a store significantly on its own. The owner should keep focus on tasks that drive large gains, such as SEO, email marketing and ads management. [L1247.003] *(Older advice, 2021-22.)*
- Growth increases customer service volume. An owner who handles it personally loses time for growth work, creating a vicious cycle that threatens the business in the long run. [L1247.004] *(Older advice, 2021-22.)*
- Tasks that are neither urgent nor important should not be done by the owner at all. If they are still wanted, someone else should do them, and the owner's focus should stay only on important tasks. [L1247.009] *(Older advice, 2021-22.)*
- When a task needs research, the owner should define exactly what must be found and then hand the research to someone else. Doing the research personally keeps the owner stuck in manual work. [L1247.023] *(Older advice, 2021-22.)*
- Consider a virtual assistant when work-life balance suffers because the business takes more screen time than personal time. Delegation can restore free time for family and friends. [L0765.005]
- Routine work that keeps getting too little time can be protected: move it into the first two work hours after the daily business and community tasks. Ad creation then gets protected focus time instead of leftover slots. [L1181.027]

#### Outsourcing candidates (reference list)

- The course lists these as outsourcing candidates. [L1247.015] *(Older advice, 2021-22.)*

| # | Task |
|---|---|
| 1 | Logo design and creation |
| 2 | Website design |
| 3 | Banner image design and creation |
| 4 | Product image resizing |
| 5 | Unique product description writing |
| 6 | Supplier and competitor product page scraping |
| 7 | Research (almost any topic) |
| 8 | Product CSV building and upload |
| 9 | Product page formatting and optimisation tasks |
| 10 | Custom webpage coding |
| 11 | Content creation (blog posts, ebooks, lead magnets, social posts) |
| 12 | Review gathering |
| 13 | Video production |
| 14 | Customer service |
| 15 | Order processing |
| 16 | Accounting |
| 17 | Social media management |
| 18 | Pay-per-click advertising management |
| 19 | Keyword research |
| 20 | Search engine optimisation |
| 21 | Direct sales |
| 22 | B2B lead generation |

  [L1247.015]

#### Priority and sequencing

- Outsource the most outsourceable tasks first, build up gradually as the business grows, and keep the tasks that only the owner can do or that carry the highest return and complexity. [L1247.018] *(Older advice, 2021-22.)*
- Outsource the repetitive, low-skill tasks that eat owner time first, such as loading products, order placement and payment processing. Keep in-house the tasks you do best, and any task that depends on supplier relationships or on understanding paid traffic. [L1122.002]
- Outsource customer service once order volume exceeds what the owner can answer alone. Keep supplier relationships in-house, since those are central to the business and hard to hand over. [L1279.101]
- Prioritize outsourcing customer service within the next six months as a primary candidate for delegation, if you have not already done so. [L0223.011] *(Older advice, 2021-22.)*
- Owners who already outsource should review how they do it, not only look for new tasks to hand off, because the working process around outsourcing determines much of its value. [L0499.003] *(Older advice, 2021-22.)*

### Before you delegate: do the task yourself first

#### The rule

- Do a task yourself before you outsource it, so you know what good results look like. [L0413.012]
- Learn each task yourself before you delegate it, so you can judge the finished work. Only outsource fully once you have the skills and can check the quality. [L0519.013]
- Before delegating any task, the owner should first build the skill personally and find out what works. A team member can only follow a method the owner already knows, otherwise they will produce generic output without a clear target. [L1360.002]
- Only delegate a task after you have learned to do it yourself. Knowing the method lets you judge the output, teach the next person, and cover the work if a team member leaves or raises prices. [L1358.002]
- Learning a skill yourself first protects the business if a team member leaves or suddenly raises rates. Once you know the method, you can run the task yourself for a short time or teach the next hire. [L1360.003]
- Perform each day-to-day task yourself until you fully understand it, and only then outsource it. This applies to all routine business tasks, such as customer contact and order handling. [L1447.003]
- Outsourcing only works when the owner already knows the correct way to do a task. Without first doing the process yourself, you cannot write clear instructions or judge whether the outside person is doing it right. [L0592.007]
- Delegating a process you have never run yourself is a mistake, because you cannot explain it properly or verify the results. Start with hands-on work and only outsource after the process is proven. [L0592.008]
- Never outsource paid ads, web design, content, SEO or social posts to outside providers too early. Learn each process first, document it for each supplier, then share the documents with a future team. [L0856.099] *(Dated 2024, not older advice.)*

#### The steps

- Three steps to working less and delegating: [L0230.008]
  1. Build a documented business system with processes and tools.
  2. Find and hire the right person.
  3. Train thoroughly and let go of day-to-day control.
- Order for scaling a business with a team: [L1359.040]
  1. Do the task yourself until you know the steps work and how long it really takes.
  2. Confirm that the task makes money before you scale it.
  3. Document it as an SOP with short, simple steps.
  4. Decide whether the role needs a contractor or an employee, and whether a virtual assistant or an apprentice fits the work.
  5. Train the new person with the skill transfer process so that efficiency is not lost.
  6. Repeat the cycle for each new process as the business grows.
- Outsourcing sequence: [L0827.004]
  1. Do the task yourself first.
  2. Eliminate tasks that are not necessary.
  3. Automate what you can, then hire for the rest.
  4. Record your workflow as a screencast so someone else can follow it.
  5. After hiring, follow up with the hire every week.
- Before outsourcing a task: [L1515.027]
  1. Do the task yourself until you know exactly how it works.
  2. Remove every unnecessary step, spreadsheet column or report.
  3. Automate or buy software for what remains.
  4. Hire a person only for tasks that cannot be removed or automated, and train them with a screencast of you doing the task.
- Perform each task yourself inside the business until you know how it works and what the correct result looks like. Then: [L0592.006]
  1. Document every step you take, creating a user guide that works as the instruction manual for the business.
  2. Fill the ready-made templates with your own business information to complete the user guide.
  3. Hand the completed user guide to the person you bring in, so they can run the task based on what already works.
  4. Once the task is delegated, focus your own time on growth or other priorities.

#### What the owner must understand

- You cannot train someone for a task you have not done yourself. Before handing off customer support or order processing, you must be able to explain the likely customer questions, how to respond to them in different situations, and the supplier-specific steps, including who to contact and what to do when an order goes to a different supplier. [L1447.005]
- Without a basic understanding of what an outsourced person does, you cannot tell whether they are succeeding or doing good work. This applies equally to outsourcing store building or traffic setup, so a minimum base-level understanding of every area is needed before hiring. [L1447.006]
- A business does not need to be profitable before you start, but the owner must understand what is happening in it. For example, the owner should be able to explain CPM and conversion results when the assistant reports them. [L0519.017]
- Handing over the whole dropshipping business to a virtual assistant without understanding it means you cannot tell whether store design, emails or ad results are good. Always keep enough knowledge to evaluate the work. [L0519.016]
- A beginner should not outsource store creation entirely, because learning to build the store yourself lets you check and teach the assistant later. [L1432.002]
- Early in a business one person may need to wear all roles, including supplier calls and uploads. Once a process is working, hand specific tasks to others. [L0832.037]

### What to delegate first: the order

#### General order

- Recommended delegation order for a running dropshipping business: [L1358.003]
  1. Product research
  2. Store build
  3. Traffic and ads
  4. Order fulfillment
  5. Customer support
- Delegate in this order as the business grows: [L1360.024]
  1. Product research: build your own skill first, then hand over the search with your criteria and tools.
  2. Store development: learn store building yourself, then have someone build the store to your structure once you have a product to test.
  3. Traffic and ads: learn the Facebook or TikTok ad strategy yourself, then let a team member prepare creatives and set up campaigns under your monitoring.
  4. Order fulfillment: outsource early, since it is time-consuming and not highly skilled work.
  5. Customer support: outsource early as well, covering email, live chat and social media messages.
- Virtual assistants can take over store setup, Facebook ad management, customer emails and order fulfilment, while the owner checks the finished results. Outsourcing is recommended when there is not enough time to finish tasks on schedule. [L0519.014]
- As a store grows, outsource tasks such as customer service, email marketing and Google Ads so the founder can focus on the highest-impact work, such as Facebook ads and creatives. Founders need to leverage their time once the business reaches a size where doing everything personally no longer scales. [L0639.040]
- Outsourcing ads, email and creator scripts to an agency and hiring a virtual assistant can free up the founder's time for other work. This makes sense when customer service and operations are taking many hours per day. [L0694.035]
- Outsource repetitive roles such as customer service once they consume daily time. When one person covers customer service, ad creation, finances and fulfillment at the same time, a structured calendar becomes essential to get anything finished. [L1181.034]
- Early on, when the owner has limited hours, delegate the most time-consuming tasks first. [L1358.001]

#### Beginner sequence (as described in the course)

- Typical beginner delegation sequence as described by the speaker: [L1432.005]
  1. Do the product research yourself and pick the product to test.
  2. Let the assistant build the store and write product descriptions while you learn the store setup.
  3. Have the assistant install apps such as Klaviyo and SMS Bump and implement them, while you understand what each does.
  4. Let the assistant search for product images and videos on Instagram and Facebook.
  5. Once orders arrive, have the assistant learn CJ Dropshipping and handle order fulfillment.
  6. Create a separate support email address and give the assistant direct access to it. For tricky emails, the assistant sends you a screenshot and you dictate the reply, while the assistant sends it.

- Note on sequence: this beginner order keeps product research with the owner, while the general order above delegates research once the owner has built the skill. Both apply at different stages.

#### Early-stage and repetitive-first sequence

- Early-stage sequence for when to outsource: [L1447.007]
  1. Follow the blueprint.
  2. Start processing orders yourself.
  3. Once you are comfortable with the business and feel qualified to operate it, begin outsourcing.
- Start outsourcing early and in small steps, but only when the business can afford the cost. Build up gradually instead of handing over everything at once. [L1122.003]
- Assistants can also take over the owner's personal admin, such as drafting emails or researching documents for a task. Handing these tasks off frees time for the business. [L1431.067]
- At the business-owner stage, delegate personal and university or study tasks as well as business tasks, and hire missing skills such as Google Ads or email marketing into the company rather than relying on outside help. [L1432.035]

### The first hire and the first assistant

#### Timing

- Hire help once a store is live, running ads and making sales, and you need time for product research, ads and offers. Early on, when the owner has limited hours, delegate the most time-consuming tasks first. [L1358.001]
- There are two entry points for building a team: once a store is live, sales are coming in and the owner needs help to free time; or early on, when the founder lacks the time to run everything and needs help with the most time-consuming tasks. Both lead to the same goal of delegating work so the owner can focus on product research, running ads and creating offers. [L1360.001]
- Delegation frees time to focus on the highest-value tasks, such as product research, ads and offers. Outsourcing tasks like order fulfillment or customer support early is a common way to achieve this. [L1360.004]
- Outsource as early as possible once the business is up, running and profitable, meaning it makes money. Hire help, adjust your schedule and free time from day-to-day tasks at that point. [L1447.008]
- Waiting until you can no longer cope with the workload before outsourcing is a mistake. Once the business is profitable, delegating routine tasks should begin rather than being postponed until you are overwhelmed. [L1447.009]
- Hiring the first VA earlier rather than later is recommended, since the sales gap after hiring was large. [L1437.022]
- At a low volume of high-ticket sales, around 20 sales a month, the owner can handle calls and emails personally, so a virtual assistant is not yet necessary. Add support once revenue and workload clearly justify it. [L1472.057]
- Many owners delay hiring a virtual assistant because they feel the business depends on them personally. Delegating only works once the owner accepts that the assistant will be the face of the business and must be trained to represent it. [L1000.022]
- Many owners delay hiring because they believe nobody else can run the business and because the project feels overwhelming with no clear starting point. Breaking the work into a system, a hire and training steps makes it manageable. [L1473.033]
- Once the early hustle phase is over (about six to twelve months at most), hand off tasks that fall outside your strengths and values. Keep only the work that sits in your sweet spot. [L0701.010]
- Founders who personally run websites, supplier contact, ads, email and phone for years end up with slow response times and neglected ad optimization. Build a team and systems within the first few months once sales start to take off. [L0852.003] *(Older advice, 2020.)*
- Outsourcing is useful for bringing in experience you lack. For example, a hired customer service specialist with 14 years of experience improved service processes and customer engagement. [L1122.011]

#### Hiring a virtual assistant

- To hire a virtual assistant: [L0671.085, L0671.222] *(Older advice, 2021; the two units are identical and merged here.)*
  1. Run the store yourself when it goes live and let orders and emails come in.
  2. Learn how to handle each supplier's orders and the most frequently asked customer questions.
  3. Write these steps in a Google Doc as an instruction manual for your business.
  4. Hire the assistant and give them that document.
- Outsourcing means hiring a person to take over tasks that still require human interaction, while tasks that repeat identically are automated. Early on, the typical outsourced worker is a virtual assistant hired remotely from anywhere in the world, not an employee on payroll in an office or warehouse. [L1447.002]
- A virtual assistant makes sense when you want to spend your time on scaling and growth rather than repetitive operational tasks. Delegating routine work frees the owner for decisions that move the business forward. [L0765.003]
- A virtual assistant buys back time, which is the scarcest resource for a business owner, by taking over day-to-day tasks such as answering phones and managing email. The owner can then focus on growth, travel or rest. [L1000.021]
- Delegating daily tasks to a virtual assistant freed the owner to focus on the big picture. Hiring the first VA earlier rather than later is recommended, since the sales gap after hiring was large. [L1437.022]
- Handing over daily operations, including customer service, to the team let the owner stop working day to day and focus on the bigger picture. A team that treats the business as its own can run it while the owner is away. [L1437.033]
- When an employee is given responsibility and trust, their performance improves noticeably and they ask fewer questions. Letting go of tasks early is the main leverage for the owner. [L1473.030]
- Training and letting go: Train thoroughly and let go of day-to-day control. [L0230.008]
- Hire and train with the screencast and SOP: after hiring, follow up with the hire every week. [L0827.004]

#### Accountability

- Without someone to hold you accountable, tasks get postponed indefinitely. Hiring help is an investment that also trains leadership and team management skills. [L0519.018]

### Owner role as the business scales

#### Core role

- The business owner should own strategy: find where the valuable traffic is, design the funnel and ladder, and then hire specialists to run ads and optimize tactics. Trying to do every task personally slows the business down. Division of labor makes growth possible. [L0523.091]
- At scale the owner should act as the strategic leader who sets the vision, makes sure work gets done and puts the right people in the right roles. Daily tasks such as supplier calls, uploading product pages and optimizing the site should be handed to others. [L0413.008]
- Partnerships, branding and the X factor are the three most effective ways to raise revenue, so the owner's time should go there. Optimization, product uploads and research can be outsourced to a team. [L0413.015]
- Once the team handles day-to-day operations and escalations, the owner should keep only sales, some marketing and accounting, which can be done from anywhere. The owner's role shifts from running the business to building it. [L0337.041]
- Hand over any task that someone else can do, including code-level changes you are not able to make yourself. A strong business leader delegates tasks rather than doing everything personally. [L1300.031] *(Older advice, 2021-22.)*
- Outsourcing frees time for growth work, such as finding new businesses, new ideas and new opportunities. Using time on repetitive order handling leaves no room for this. [L1122.012]
- The purpose of outsourcing routine tasks is to free time for growth, because a business cannot grow while the owner only handles day-to-day operations. Delegating these tasks is the step that allows the business to expand. [L1447.010]
- Outsourcing is a major lever for e-commerce owners across marketing, growth and day-to-day operations, and it is worth starting early rather than waiting until the business is large. [L0499.001] *(Older advice, 2021-22.)*

#### Future role

- The owner's future role can be to step back from the day-to-day, for example by managing a team or a network of businesses. Decide in the vision whether the owner stays in the weeds or moves to an advisory or management position. [L0439.012] *(Older advice, 2021-22.)*
- An owner moves up the ownership ladder as early revenue produces free cash flow to pay the first hire. Each new hire takes one more task off the owner, from doing the work, to managing those who do it, to managing those who work on the business. [L1334.044] *(Older advice, 2021-22.)*
- Scaling works best when the owner only reviews what changed: a supplier that stopped responding, a backordered product, or a stock report with low inventory. Routine work is delegated. [L0468.175] *(Older advice, 2020.)*
- Before leaving the country or taking a long break, set up the store and hire someone to handle phone calls and customer messages. [L0468.174] *(Older advice, 2020.)*
- Early in a business one person may need to wear all roles, including supplier calls and uploads. Once a process is working, hand specific tasks to others. [L0832.037]

#### Owner-level example

- A dropshipping owner with a niche store at about 40,000 USD monthly sales had plateaued. Customer feedback showed the range was too narrow, so he planned to add about 700 products. He hired freelancers for image editing, product descriptions and uploads to Shopify, and finished in about three weeks. Within two months monthly sales rose from 40,000 USD to 80,000 USD. Afterwards his own time grew to 20 to 30 hours per week until he moved the tasks to a team, and he cut his weekly time from over 30 hours to under 5 hours per week while keeping pay-per-click management himself. [L1122.006]
- Starting point: a single-owner store that had run since 2016, with the owner still in a corporate job and working around 40 hours per week on the business in January. The owner set a goal of $2 million revenue for 2021 and $1 million by June 30; 2020 revenue was $1.4 million with a team of three. The owner then joined a state COVID vaccination response team in early January 2021 and worked about seven days a week, 12-hour days, for six months, coordinating 121 nurses and 70 National Guard members who administered 250,000 vaccinations. [L0337.017]
  - What was done: the business was run with help from a team and was reduced to about 10 owner hours per week by June while revenue kept growing.
  - Result: $1 million was reached in May, one month ahead of the June goal, and about $1.29 to $1.3 million was reached over the six months. [L0337.017]
- A store owner in one case ran a store from 2014 and handled all operations for four years while struggling with trust issues and overwhelm. In 2018, after having a child, she hired a virtual assistant who saved the business and her well-being. Key insight: building systems before a crisis forces you to delegate is better than waiting until life events demand it. [L0230.001]

### The 30-minute-a-day target and time budgets

Sources give different time targets. Each value applies to the model stated.

| Source | Time figure | Model it applies to | Units |
|---|---|---|---|
| Source A | 15 to 30 minutes per day | Goal for a dropshipping business to be managed | [L0506.011] |
| Source A | 15 to 20 minutes per day | One store run part-time as a lifestyle business | [L1111.162] |
| Source A | 30 minutes a day | Combined approach: automation for repeatable work plus delegation of day-to-day tasks | [L0592.002] |
| Source A (2019, older) | About 30 minutes of business work per day, of which 15 minutes go to contacting new suppliers | Daily work split | [L1086.003] |
| Source B (2021-22, older) | About one hour a day during setup, then a couple of hours a week once sales are made | High-ticket store run as a side business | [L0458.055] |
| Source B (2021-22, older) | Many owners manage a store with about one hour per day; some with only a couple of hours per week | Store with sales running | [L0771.053] |
| Source A (case) | Owner time over 30 hours per week cut to under 5 hours per week | Niche store that moved tasks to a team, owner keeps pay-per-click | [L1122.006] |

- Running the business at 30 minutes a day combines two approaches: automation through apps for repeatable work, and delegation of day-to-day tasks to people. Automation covers what software can handle, and delegation covers what still needs a human. [L0592.002]
- The usual order is to set up automation apps for repetitive work first, then delegate the day-to-day tasks that remain to people. The two approaches are combined to keep the owner's daily time low, not used in isolation. [L0592.014]
- Routine execution work that moves the business forward, in order: [L1042.014] *(Dated 2026, source the source's own site
  1. Launch new creatives.
  2. Improve operations.
  3. Fulfill orders.
  4. Talk to customers.
- Priorities for a busy store owner, in order: [L1281.086] *(Older advice, 2022.)*
  1. Make sure every order ships on time.
  2. Make sure every customer receives tracking information.
  3. Ask customers for reviews once they are happy.
  4. Check ads at least once a week for tweaks.
  5. Track profit per order to see what really makes money.
- When the owner is overloaded, give the team a clear list of daily priorities. Keep shipping, customer tracking and reviews first, then ad monitoring and profit tracking. [L1281.085] *(Older advice, 2022.)*

### What must stay in-house

- Keep supplier contact in-house, because supplier relationships drive bulk rates and discounts that compound as the business grows. Do not hand this task to someone else, even when it is unpleasant. [L1122.009]
- Keep supplier relationships in-house, since those are central to the business and hard to hand over. [L1279.101]
- Keep the tasks you do best, and any task that depends on supplier relationships or on understanding paid traffic, in-house. [L1122.002]
- The owner should keep only sales, some marketing and accounting, which can be done from anywhere. [L0337.041]
- Owner keeps strategy and key relationships: Partnerships, branding and the X factor are the three most effective ways to raise revenue, so the owner's time should go there. [L0413.015]
- Focus yourself on ads, supplier relationships, offers and big-picture decisions. Outsource your weaknesses and keep your strengths. Give tasks like web design and customer service to others. [L0852.004] *(Older advice, 2020.)*
- Outsource your weaknesses and keep your strengths: give tasks like web design and customer service to others, and focus yourself on ads, supplier relationships, offers and big-picture decisions. [L0852.004]
- Keep the work that fits your strengths for yourself, while setting the framework or strategy. Outsourcing works best when you define the end result you want from a task and let someone else run the execution. You can keep the part you enjoy, such as setting the framework or strategy, while a team member manages the day-to-day work. [L0701.032]
- Ads: Owners who are weak at ads or dislike them should hand off ad management. Other sources advise the owner to keep paid traffic understanding in-house and to keep paid ads management for themselves in one case. These differ and both apply by case: Dislike or weakness: outsource. [L1247.011] Paid traffic understanding: keep in-house. [L1122.002] Owner kept pay-per-click after moving tasks to a team in the case. [L1122.006] Too early: do not outsource paid ads before learning and documenting the process. [L0856.099]
- Source A's test for what must stay: a task the owner does personally and must keep is one where the owner's own knowledge is required for relationships or results. [L1122.009, L1122.010]

#### Mistakes to avoid

- Delegating a process you have never run yourself is a mistake. [L0592.008]
- Handing over the whole business to a virtual assistant without understanding it is a mistake. [L0519.016]
- Outsourcing a task costs money, but the freed time usually lets the business do more growth work. [L1247.024]
- Never outsource a task at a loss: if outsourcing would push the business into the red, wait until the business can afford it, though owners may push closer to break-even because outsourcing enables faster growth. [L1247.014] *(Older advice, 2021-22.)*
- Busy work that does not contribute to results should be removed from the business, not just performed more efficiently. [L1039.021]
- Many owners delay hiring a virtual assistant because they feel the business depends on them personally. [L1000.022]
- Many owners delay hiring because they believe nobody else can run the business. [L1473.033]
- Waiting until you can no longer cope with the workload before outsourcing is a mistake. [L1447.009]
- Refusing to outsource to protect every dollar of profit can limit growth. [L1247.024]
- Do not outsource paid ads, web design, content, SEO or social posts to outside providers too early. [L0856.099]
- Outsourcing a task only because you dislike it is a mistake. Some tasks the owner must personally keep. [L1122.010]

### Sources on timing and affordability

Sources differ on when to start outsourcing. Each rule applies to the condition stated.

| Condition | Advice | Units |
|---|---|---|
| Business is up, running and profitable | Outsource as early as possible | [L1447.008] |
| Business can afford the cost | Start early, in small steps | [L1122.003] |
| Outsourcing would push the business into the red | Wait until the business can afford it | [L1247.014] |
| Low volume (around 20 high-ticket sales a month) | Owner handles calls and emails; no VA yet | [L1472.057] |
| Order volume exceeds owner capacity | Outsource customer service | [L1279.101] |
| Paid ads, web design, content, SEO or social posts, early in the business | Do not outsource too early; learn and document first | [L0856.099] |
| Owner already outsources | Review how the process works | [L0499.003] |

- Outsourcing is useful for bringing in experience you lack, for example a hired customer service specialist with 14 years of experience who improved service processes and customer engagement. [L1122.011]
- Outsourcing works best when you define the end result you want from a task and let someone else run the execution. [L0701.032]

Note: the timing sources are not all the same age; the older (2019-2022) entries are marked above.

#### Further details

- Delegate in small, concrete tasks, such as 'can someone answer the phone for me', rather than in broad functions like 'I need customer support'. Only a specific task can be handed off cleanly and checked afterwards. [L0337.003]

- The operator first outsourced his own job (writing blog posts and managing a social media page) to a virtual assistant, which freed time to build his own dropshipping business. Tasks he had no time for, such as replying to customer emails and processing orders daily, were then also delegated. [L0519.015]

## Documenting processes: SOPs, checklists and training videos

### Why document before you delegate

- Build documented business systems and processes before you need to delegate. This lets you hire without disrupting the business and gives you a repeatable roadmap for onboarding [L0230.002].
- A documented business system provides three benefits: a smooth transition when delegating, less stress for the owner, and higher business value [L0230.019].
- Create a video of every process from the very beginning, even before hiring anyone, because someone else will eventually need the knowledge. A business where all knowledge sits in the owner's head ends when the owner is away, sick or unavailable [L0337.007].
- Running a business with all processes stored in the owner's head makes it impossible to hand over work. Write down processes and build a system before hiring anyone [L1000.046].
- Do not wait for a major life event to force you to build systems. Start documenting while you still run the operation yourself, so a hire can take over when revenue allows [L1473.032].
- When an ecommerce brand grows quickly, operations tend to become unstable, data ends up spread across disconnected systems, and priorities lose clarity. Build standardized systems and processes during growth so that scale stays sustainable [L0828.001].
- Outsourcing requires standard operating procedures and training. This feels like a step backwards at first but frees time later; expect several months of stress before the benefit appears. *(Older advice, 2021.)* [L0834.103]
- Staff who are still learning need documented processes more than experienced staff do. Without them they miss steps that are routine for the owner. *(Older advice, 2021-22.)* [L0949.038]

### What to document first

- Document only processes that already make money. Scaling an unprofitable task adds inefficiency and bloats a small business [L1359.004].
- Confirm that the process is profitable and document it before you hire anyone for it. Hiring for an unproven process means paying someone to learn something you have not yet proven works [L1359.010].
- Start with the repetitive, time-consuming tasks that eat your hours [L1359.006].
- Do customer service and daily operations yourself at the start, even if it means very long weeks, so you can write down how the store works. Outsource only after you have documented processes to hand over. *(Older advice, 2019.)* [L0467.008]
- Learn every task yourself before delegating it, including customer reply templates, comment moderation and assigning a page moderator. Then record your screen with a tool like Loom and give the videos to new hires for training [L1431.022].
- Answer customer questions yourself for the first period of the business [L1287.009].
- Systemize repeatable tasks so they can be done efficiently and consistently, reverse engineered, outsourced and automated. Systemizing is the basis for scaling the business to the next level [L1043.008].
- Each supplier should have its own project and documentation, because a team member working on one supplier's uploads will not know the workflow of another supplier [L0359.102].
- Pair every launch step with a downloadable checklist and a ready-to-use template. A defined output format lets the operator complete the step by following the list instead of working out the method each time, which reduces delays [L0555.009].
- Build a standard operating procedure for repeatable tasks that are not business specific, such as SEO, Instagram or Facebook work. Share and store these procedures so the team can reuse them. *(Older advice, 2021.)* [L0801.157]
- Write standard operating procedures for every recurring task you hand over, so team members know exactly how to do the work. Templates and SOPs can be reused, because most businesses repeat the same tasks [L0413.013].

### Setting up the documentation workspace

Getting started with business systems:

1. Create a Google Chrome workspace at https://bit.ly/2rlrFyK [L0230.009].
2. Set up Reamaze and Asana for operations [L0230.009].
3. Document everything in Google Drive: SOPs, FAQs, response templates, passwords, supplier and customer contact information [L0230.009].

### Central business spreadsheet

The primary spreadsheet has one tab per area, all in one place [L1000.048]:

| Tab | Contents |
|---|---|
| Supplier sheet | Current, potential and rejected suppliers; order-forwarding script for each supplier; shipping details such as pickup times, warehouse hours, pickup contact, and which carriers collect from the area; wholesale rates for bulk discounts; supplier logins where ordering is done online |
| Prospect sheet | Every person who calls, emails or contacts the store without ordering immediately; contact method codes such as PH for phone and EM for email; notes on the product sought and the reason for not buying |
| Accounts and logins | Shipping company accounts; returns addresses; bank transfer details for customers who pay that way; app and live chat logins; social media logins |
| Discount calculator | Simple formulas for quickly calculating percentage discounts up or down |
| Approved coupon codes | Codes the assistant may apply without asking permission |
| Returned stock | Returned items that are unsold and can be offered to customers at a lower price |
| SOP links | Links to standard operating procedures stored in Google Drive or YouTube |
| Brand details | Brand hex colours for designers making social media graphics |
| Shipping dimensions | Accurate product sizes so that shipping costs stay low |

[L1000.048]

### Recording tools and video

- Loom is a free Google Chrome extension that records screen video, microphone audio and webcam simultaneously. Use it to record an SOP video of your product upload process, aiming for at least 20 to 30 seconds, then send it to a freelancer hired to complete the task. *(Older advice, 2019.)* [L0243.017]
- Loom records screen and camera video quickly. Use it to document processes and train team members or contractors without long meetings [L0675.013].
- Loom (the free version is enough) records screen, webcam and microphone at the same time and gives a shareable video URL [L0971.008].
- Loom is a screen and webcam recording tool that creates shareable video links. Use it to send walkthroughs and explanations to team members or clients instead of long written instructions [L1007.009].
- Loom videos are quick to produce, so you can record five to ten follow-up videos in a row and send them in one session. Batching them keeps the time cost per lead low [L1049.015].
- A screen recording of you doing a booking yourself can be quicker to make and easier to follow than a written instruction [L1291.015].
- SweetProcess is a paid tool for modular, visual SOPs. Each step is numbered automatically, can carry directions, images and video, and the worker ticks off steps as a checklist. A Google Doc can serve as a free alternative [L1359.008].

### SOP definition and principles

- A standard operating procedure (SOP) is a written workflow or manual that tells a person how to handle a specific situation. Its purpose is to make sure tasks always run the same way and as efficiently as possible [L1340.001].
- A SOP is a written step-by-step list for a recurring task. Create SOPs for everyday tasks as the business grows, so the work can be handed to a team member without one-on-one training [L0359.100].
- Written SOPs let any new person do the same job the same way, whether a long-term employee, a newly hired stranger or a first-time virtual assistant. A new person can read the SOP and manage situations without prior training from the owner [L1340.002].
- Write SOPs simpler than seems necessary. What is obvious to the owner after many repetitions can confuse a new person. Visual steps help people who read the language less fluently [L1359.009].
- Break long processes into short chunks so a team member can finish one part in a sitting and know exactly where to resume. A process that must be finished in one go is harder to delegate [L1359.005].
- Keep SOPs light on words and heavy on screenshots or video. A five-minute task should not need a ten-page document. *(Older advice, 2020.)* [L1088.071]
- Writing an SOP for a task you have never performed yourself leads to wrong time estimates and missing steps. Someone else then fails at the task or takes far longer than expected [L1359.007].

### Writing an SOP: the procedures

Steps to build an SOP:

1. Confirm that the task makes money before you document it [L1359.006].
2. Do the task yourself at least a couple of times, and time it, so you know how long it really takes and that the steps work [L1359.006].
3. Start with the repetitive, time-consuming tasks that eat your hours [L1359.006].
4. Write the steps at an entry-level, almost basic level, as if a fast-food employee had to follow them [L1359.006].
5. Split complex tasks into small chunks, and when a sub-task is complicated, link it to its own separate process [L1359.006].
6. Add screenshots or short videos to each step, especially for team members who are not native speakers [L1359.006].
7. Test the SOP with a peer or another entrepreneur who does not know your business, and fix every step they cannot follow [L1359.006].

Systemizing a repeatable task:

1. Look for inefficiency, especially in tasks that repeat often [L1043.009].
2. List what currently works for each task and what wastes time or effort [L1043.009].
3. Combine the best elements into one written procedure for the task, which can then be handed to others or automated [L1043.009].

Writing a simple SOP with Loom *(older advice, 2020)*:

1. Perform the task once while recording it with Loom [L1088.070].
2. Write a short bullet list of the steps, using few words [L1088.070].
3. Embed the recording next to each step, so the video shows exactly how each step is done [L1088.070].
4. Keep all SOPs in a shared Google Drive so the team can access and edit them in real time [L1088.070].

Documenting a repeatable process before handing it over:

1. Write down how the task is done and the rules that apply [L1122.022].
2. Prefer a screen recording of you completing the task, for example from order collection in Shopify through the supplier order and fulfilment [L1122.022].
3. Store the documentation so new hires can use it, and it will also ease a future business handover to a buyer [L1122.022].

### SOP format: text, video and checklist *(older advice, 2021-22, unless stated)*

- Video walkthroughs of the task performed on screen, with narration, are often the easiest SOP format because the person hears and sees each step. Not every task suits video, though. Many operators provide both a video and a text document for the same SOP, since people learn in different ways [L1339.004].
- Text SOPs become easier to follow when screenshots of the relevant screen are added, so the person can confirm they are in the right place [L1339.005].
- Record the SOP as a screen share, with voice and screen but no need for your face on camera. Add screenshots or video to each step [L0263.019, L1340.005].
- Structure every SOP in this order [L1339.006]:
  1. Use text or video as required.
  2. Always start by explaining the purpose of the task.
  3. Break the task into a step-by-step sequence.
  4. Finish the SOP with a section on what NOT to do (no-nos or reminders).
  5. Create a single-page checklist for the SOP.
- The purpose section should explain what the task helps the business achieve and how it supports the wider business. For example, uploading products to a Shopify store widens the product range, which engages more customers and increases revenue and profit. Knowing their role fosters a sense of inclusion, which improves engagement and the quality of work [L1339.007].
- Break the task into as many specific numbered steps as possible (Step 1, Step 2, etc.). In a video, state each step and demonstrate it. In a text document, outline each step with its details and add screenshots where a particular page or screen needs to be recognised [L1339.008].
- Example: for a Shopify product upload SOP, Step 1 is to copy the product title into the title field, Step 2 is to paste the product description into the description field, and further steps cover every other product data field. Each step is one concrete action, so the person can follow it without guessing [L1339.018].
- A no-nos section lists what the person must not do, such as copying content from other sites. Specifying prohibited actions is as important as the steps, because it removes doubt about expectations and clarifies whether a contractor should be kept if they repeat a forbidden action. If someone repeats an action after being told not to do it, that is grounds for immediate dismissal [L1339.009].
- Example: for a task where a contractor writes unique product descriptions with research, copied content from another site is a clear no-no, and product descriptions must contain no duplicated content [L1339.010].
- Every SOP must end with a checklist that fits on a single page. The checklist is a quick reference the person ticks off after completing the task, while the full SOP is used for learning rather than for memory checks [L1339.011].
- Checklists reduce errors in industries that use them, as shown in aviation, where accidents fell after pilots began running through pre-flight checklists. Checklists reduce time spent fixing and checking errors, and lower the need for later performance management of staff [L1339.012].
- The book *The Checklist Manifesto* (a short book of a few hundred pages, available from major bookstores and Amazon) covers why checklists support business processes, safety and quality [L1339.013].
- Keep every SOP simple by starting from one minimal template that already contains all required parts: purpose, numbered steps, a no-nos section and a single-page checklist. Adapt the template for each new task instead of designing a new layout each time [L1339.017].
- Record screencasts of recurring tasks without using the employee's name, so the same video can train every future hire, including replacements [L1515.028].

### Templates

- SOP templates are Word documents that can be opened in Microsoft Word, Apple Pages or Google Drive. They are meant to be edited so they fit your own business [L1340.003].
- SOP templates are a starting point, not a final document. Adapt each template to your own products, suppliers and policies before your team uses it [L1340.004].
- The seven SOP templates available in the set [L1340.007]:
  1. Customer Service SOP
  2. Order Management SOP
  3. Virtual Assistant Interview Template
  4. Hiring Questions
  5. Supplier Procedures SOP
  6. Website Style Guide SOP
  7. Shipping and Return Policy SOP
- Procedure for adopting the SOP templates [L1340.005]:
  1. Download all SOP templates.
  2. Open them on your computer and read through them.
  3. Look at the processes described and identify what you actually use.
  4. Modify each template so it fits your business better.
  5. Save all finished SOPs in your own Google Drive account.
  6. When you hire someone, share a Google Drive link with them instead of sending a file.
- A user guide, meaning a written instruction manual for the business, is the core document for outsourcing. It should describe each recurring process in enough detail that a new person can follow it without the owner present [L0592.009].
- Pre-built templates for the user guide let the owner plug in their own business information instead of writing each document from scratch. Using them speeds up documenting processes before delegation [L0592.010].
- Write quotes and emails from fixed parts. Keep the same opening and closing sentences in every template and only edit the middle for each customer. This removes the need to write each message from scratch and keeps all messages consistent even when different people send them. *(Older advice, 2021-22.)* [L1396.010]
- Create a quote template in Excel or Google Sheets with your logo, company colours, the date, the company email, the customer name and contact details, and fields for item number and price. Store the file in the PDF or templates section so every team member uses the same version. Staff then only replace the item and price fields instead of building the quote from scratch. *(Older advice, 2021-22.)* [L1396.011]
- Build a pre-built template for each new product page and reuse it. Once one product page works, reuse the same template and checklist for the next product [L1188.030].

### Storage and sharing

- Store all SOPs in Google Drive, using separate folders per work area (for example Shopify product uploads with attachments, website design, content creation and posting). Share a folder with a freelancer or VA to give access; updates reach them automatically without resending files. Dropbox or another online file storage works equally well, and originals should also be kept on a hard drive or backup storage. *(Older advice, 2021-22.)* [L1339.003]
- Store your SOPs in your own Google Drive and share the link with each new team member. Do not send downloaded files, because the team then works from one central, consistent version [L1340.006].
- A common mistake is to send SOP files directly to new team members, which creates several copies that drift apart over time. Sharing one Google Drive link keeps every team member on the same current version [L1340.008].
- Creating and storing SOPs:
  1. Write each SOP down or record it as a screen share [L1000.060].
  2. Store the SOP in Google Drive or on YouTube [L1000.060].
  3. Add a link to each SOP in the shared business spreadsheet, so staff can find it by clicking [L1000.060].
- Storage location: Google Drive is the default in the Source A sources. YouTube is the other option named for stored SOP videos [L1000.048, L1000.060, L1340.006]. Dropbox is named as an equal alternative in the Source B source *(older advice, 2021-22)* [L1339.003].
- Create a shared resource board for guidelines, logos, photography and key information in SharePoint or Google Drive. Set it up as a self-service repository so team members find materials without requesting them from the creative team [L0260.017].
- Share all strategy documents (brand, marketing, content, influencer and so on) in a central accessible location so any team member can reference company direction at any time [L0260.021].
- Keep one master folder per product with subfolders for thumbnails, videos, review screenshots and brand assets, and use a consistent naming pattern. This makes it fast for a team member to rebuild a product page or ad without searching the whole computer [L1188.029].
- Store important information in more than one place, such as the price list appearing in both the company information and the quotes section, because people search for things in different ways. Attach documents like price lists directly to the relevant card in Trello. Update every copy whenever a manufacturer sends a new price list. *(Older advice, 2021-22.)* [L1396.006]

### Data standards

- Standardize controlled lists in SOPs, for example approved product tags. Create an official bucket of tags that all team members must use, with no ad-hoc variations. The manager approves additions only. This ensures data consistency, enabling collections, filters and reporting across products [L0263.020].
- Create a live SOP file that team members can contribute to with comments and suggestions. If someone discovers a faster method, they add it to the SOP and the manager approves. SOPs then improve over time and everyone invests in them [L0263.019].

### Process repository and team questions

Answering recurring team questions through a process repository:

1. Whenever a team member asks how to do something, record the answer as a short screen video (or article) instead of explaining it again [L0337.009].
2. Save it in the shared repository and send the link to the team member [L0337.009].
3. When a team member asks about a process that is not in the repository, check first whether it exists [L0337.009].
4. If it does not exist, the team member records the process and adds it to the repository, then reports that the new entry is there [L0337.009].

Staff who handle customer service need the same knowledge base. Written standard procedures for returns, support, uploads and ads reduce errors as the team grows. *(Older advice, 2020.)* [L1111.164]

### Customer service playbook

Building a playbook from customer questions:

1. Answer customer questions yourself for the first period of the business [L1287.009].
2. Record every recurring question and its approved answer in one document [L1287.009].
3. Turn that document into a playbook that a future hire or assistant follows when responding to customers [L1287.009].
4. Update the playbook as new question types appear [L1287.009].

### Company information board and communication scripts *(older advice, 2021-22, unless stated)*

Building a general company information board for new team members, in this order [L1396.003]:

1. Company history.
2. Company values.
3. Shipping and return policies.
4. How the business works with vendors.
5. How customers should be addressed and what image the company projects.
6. Examples of difficult customer situations with model answers.
7. Working hours and schedule.
8. Clear expectations for the role.

Creating a standard operating procedure tab that holds all repeatable communication [L1396.008]:

1. A phone greeting script.
2. A chat opening and closing script.
3. Email templates for common cases, such as requesting quotes from manufacturers.
