4. A list of questions to ask customers before quoting.
5. Follow-up templates for quotes.

### Onboarding and training

- Onboard new team members through a video library that covers company goals, the vision and all core processes, so newcomers reach working speed without the owner. The library also makes it easier to sell the business later, since documented processes are expected by buyers [L0337.010].
- Create a training video for a complex recurring task such as product uploading [L1441.020]:
  1. Record a narrated screencast of about 30 minutes that shows exactly how to upload the product, including the tags, description format, removal of files and linking [L1441.020].
  2. Have the virtual assistant watch the video at half speed [L1441.020].
  3. Have the assistant repeat the task for each product [L1441.020].
  
  This approach saves hundreds of hours over time [L1441.020].
- Before a new hire starts, record a sample upload of about 5 to 10 products and send it to them, so they can see the correct process [L0971.008].
- Give a VA or freelancer both a written SOP and a video walkthrough of each task. Record the video after running through the task once. The written SOP is the quick reference for later use, because it is faster to navigate than a long video. *(Older advice, 2021-22.)* [L0945.007]
- A virtual assistant can book shipping by following written step-by-step instructions for the booking process [L1291.015].
- Assign the product page SEO checklist to whoever creates or uploads product pages, and have them run through it during the product data upload. Team members can also work through it as a learning tool when onboarding someone new. *(Older advice, 2021-22.)* [L1191.024]

### Hiring and outsourcing

Hiring a Business Process Outsourcing freelancer to build documentation when the owner is overloaded *(older advice, 2019)* [L0467.007]:

1. Post a job on Upwork for a BPO specialist, not a day-to-day virtual assistant [L0467.007].
2. Choose a candidate whose Upwork profile shows experience writing SOPs (standard operating procedures) [L0467.007].
3. Have them do the tasks and document each process as they go, so the owner spends only a few hours a week with them [L0467.007].
4. Once the documentation exists, bring on a customer service VA and hand over the documents [L0467.007].

A BPO freelancer costs more than a customer service VA, but the Philippines offers strong workers at good prices *(older advice, 2019)* [L0467.008].

- Write standard operating procedures before hiring a virtual assistant so they know how to run the business. Hire someone who has held jobs at other companies for long periods, since short tenures often signal instability *(older advice, 2021)* [L0853.068].
- Write the SOP before posting a job advertisement, not after hiring someone. Documenting the task first clarifies what to ask candidates in the application and interview stages and avoids pressure during onboarding *(older advice, 2021-22)* [L1339.002].
- Create a standard operating procedure for every task you delegate, regardless of how small or simple it seems. For large tasks, several SOPs covering individual components can be useful, but every outsourced task needs at least one *(older advice, 2021-22)* [L1339.001].
- Every SOP should be adapted to the specific business before it is handed to a VA. Add the business's own preferred methods, storage locations for files and any other specifics, and update the SOP whenever the business changes *(older advice, 2021-22)* [L0945.006].
- Link building outsourcing SOPs should be written first for the Skyscraper method, since it is the most time-consuming and most complicated link building approach. Its tasks are the priority for documentation *(older advice, 2021-22)* [L0945.008].
- Learn as you go and document each step, so that whoever runs the operation later can understand how every supplier works without asking the owner *(older advice, 2020)* [L0468.172].
- Build the business so that it can be handed over. Create a video of every process from the start, and keep the team's SOPs current [L0337.007, L1122.022].

### Systemizing and continuous improvement

- Build repeatable systems for every recurring task, because systems maximize efficiency and produce consistent results. A good system can be handed to an employee or virtual assistant with predictable output [L1039.016].
- Use the same proven selection criteria every time you choose a product, and the same repeatable process every time you build traffic, instead of starting from scratch each time. This makes results predictable across stores [L1039.017].
- Study competitors who already succeed in your market and reverse-engineer their systems to see what works. Then replicate it and improve on it rather than guessing [L1039.018].
- A system should work like a recipe: anyone who follows it exactly should reach the same result. This allows you to outsource or automate tasks without wondering whether they are being done correctly [L1039.019].
- If you cannot describe a task as a clear step-by-step process, you do not understand it well enough to delegate or repeat it. Document every routine as you go, including tasks like uploading a product or researching suppliers [L1039.020].
- Look for inefficiencies in repetitive tasks and improve them, since a system should be the most direct route to the result. Keep what works and build it into the process for your future employees [L1039.022].
- Build systems as an ongoing habit rather than a one-time to-do list, and keep refining them as the business changes. Treating process improvement as part of your normal way of working makes it continuous [L1039.035].
- Build a standard operating procedure for repeatable tasks that are not business specific, and share and store them so the team can reuse them *(older advice, 2021)* [L0801.157].

### Mistakes to avoid

- Running a business with all processes stored in the owner's head makes it impossible to hand over work. Write down processes and build a system before hiring anyone [L1000.046].
- Do not wait for a major life event to force you to build systems. Start documenting and building the operation while you still run it yourself [L1473.032].
- Writing an SOP for a task you have never performed yourself leads to wrong time estimates and missing steps [L1359.007].
- Sending SOP files directly to new team members creates copies that drift apart. Use one shared Google Drive link [L1340.008].
- Hiring for an unproven process means paying someone to learn something you have not yet proven works [L1359.010].

## Automation and AI workflows

### Readiness: when a task is ready to automate

- A task that repeats regularly is ready for automation without first being perfected. Repetition is the criterion, not the optimization level. [L0285.006]
- Automation fits tasks that occur day in and day out. [L0285.002]
- Automate capturing orders only once suppliers reliably have stock. Until then, capture orders manually and have a team member confirm stock on each order. *(Older advice, Source A 2021.)* [L0834.104]
- Eliminate the cause of a problem before automating the response to it, because a problem that never occurs needs no handling. Automation should follow once the root causes of calls, emails and refunds have been addressed. [L0910.042]
- Automate repeated customer-facing steps once volume grows, because manual handling becomes overwhelming. Example: quiz replies were moved to a team member for a personal touch, but the team returned to automation after about a week because the automated version was better. [L0337.038]
- Identify repetitive manual tasks documented in Standard Operating Procedures. Any task that recurs consistently can be turned into a Zapier automation, which reduces human error and frees team capacity for high-value work. [L0286.033]
- Any task that employees or team members repeat the same way multiple times is a strong candidate for automation. It saves money and frees people for higher-value work. [L0312.004]
- Automation handles rule-based, repetitive tasks (trigger X, perform Y). It does not replace human judgment for exceptions, negotiation or creative decisions. Use the time it frees for those tasks. [L0286.036]
- The aim of an automated dropshipping store is to keep daily manual work to 30 minutes or less, even when the store is very profitable. Judge every tool and automation by how much recurring manual work it removes. [L0437.011]

### Manual first, then automate

- Execute each process once manually to understand it, then transfer it to automation for ongoing execution. [L0285.002]
- In the early stage, prioritize manual, non-scalable work, such as personal email follow-ups and individual abandoned-checkout outreach. This extracts maximum conversions from existing traffic. As you scale, these tasks can be automated or delegated. The manual work also generates customer feedback that reveals optimization opportunities. [L0208.012]
- For a team, automate the order email step only after manual processing has worked smoothly for several orders. Add automation once the manual process is verified. [L0964.149]
- Supplier order emails and the Trello card move are done by hand at first. Once several orders have gone through without errors, add a Zapier automation that moves the card to "sent to supplier" when the order email goes out. Add a note on the card showing it was moved automatically. [L0964.160]
- Add an extra note to an automated card when an automation moves it, so staff can tell it was not handled manually. [L0964.150]
- Study the automations module only after the store receives paid traffic and runs operationally, because automations are most useful once you understand the baseline workflow. *(Older advice, Source A 2020.)* [L0268.076]
- Learning automation pays off over time. After you understand how one automation works, you start seeing similar opportunities across the business, such as inventory updates, customer emails, competitor monitoring and report generation. [L0287.009]

### Core automation sequence: abandoned cart, order tracking, profit tracking

**Two sequences are given. Both place abandoned cart first and profit tracking near the end. Use the five-step list for the full build-out. Use the three-step list as the shorter core set.**

Full priority sequence:

| Step | Automation | Source |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Abandoned cart automation | [L0192.001] |
| 2 | Order processing automation | [L0192.001] |
| 3 | Google Sheets automation | [L0192.001] |
| 4 | Order tracking and reviews automation | [L0192.001] |
| 5 | Profit tracking automation | [L0192.001] |

- If you maintain a blog, also create a content distribution automation. [L0192.001]
- Once the business is profitable, hire your first employee to handle operations. [L0192.001]

Three-step core set (the same list appears in two source units):

1. Set up the abandoned cart automation. [L0393.018, L0532.022]
2. Set up the order tracking automation. [L0393.018, L0532.022]
3. Set up the profit tracking automation. [L0393.018, L0532.022]

Advanced automation for outsourcing is a later step. It fits once the core processes already run and the store needs to hand work to others. [L0544.013]

### Time saved and where it goes

- Automation is meant to free time. Campaign management, bid optimization, and Google and Facebook ad bid optimization together typically take about 5 hours each. Use the saved hours for growth instead of manual adjustments. [L1033.015]
- Time saved by automation should move to higher-leverage work: product titles, product page conversion rate, cross-channel feed distribution, branding and cold social funnels. For example, five hours of bid tweaking can go to product titles or product pages instead. [L0454.040]
- Automate in steps rather than everything at once. As paid traffic becomes more consistent, shift attention to branding, conversion rate optimization, SEO, email marketing, social media, content marketing and influencer outreach. [L0454.042]
- Automation and outsourcing let order volume, sales and customer count grow without a matching increase in work, manpower or hours, which is what makes the business ready to scale. [L0506.012]
- Automation templates can be used to automate most store operations, and ready-made templates can be set up directly in the system. [L0506.013]

### Learning the stack

1. Use each tool in the stack yourself at least once to learn how it works: Shopify, Facebook page, Google Sheets, Trello, Slack and Zapier. [L0437.012]
2. Only after you understand each tool, set up the automations that connect them. [L0437.012]
3. Keep the free versions of Slack, Trello and Google Sheets unless a real limit is reached. [L0437.012]
4. Pay only for Zapier's $20 per month plan when you need the Shopify integration. [L0437.012]
- Automations work like a virtual employee that handles repeatable tasks such as order tracking, posting and sending notifications. Connect each automation through Zapier and have it report results to Slack or a shared sheet. [L0437.013]
- Automation platforms cost approximately $20 per month and reliably execute tasks, without the operational costs and risks of employees: no sick days, errors, salary increases or staff turnover. [L0236.001]
- Process automation costs $20 per month and runs 24 hours a day, 7 days a week, 365 days a year, including holidays. [L0285.004]
- Automation provides reliable execution without errors once configured, runs without interruption from illness or other human factors, eliminates requests for raises, preserves business trade secrets and continuously improves its capabilities. This frees the owner for work that needs human judgment and strategic thinking. [L0285.003]
- Automations are not free of trade-offs. Automated email sequences sometimes arrive at suboptimal times for individual customers, such as during backorders or shipping delays. This timing variance is an acceptable trade-off for operational scalability and simple systems, and it causes minimal harm overall. *(Older advice, Source B 2021-22.)* [L0203.014]
- Manual updating of order notes in Asana is the weakest operational friction point. Automation or workflow redesign would reduce this burden. [L0230.025]

### Zapier and Trello order flows

**Trello order flow (Source A):**

1. Customer places order: Zap 1 automatically creates a card in paid orders with complete order details (name, email, product, SKU, shipping address). [L0292.008]
2. When the supplier ships, mark fulfillment as shipped in Shopify. Zap 2 automatically moves the card to shipped orders. [L0292.008, L0286.013]
3. When tracking shows delivery, manually move the card to delivered and pending review. [L0292.008]
4. Contact the customer by phone, email or text using the follow-up script to request a review and feedback. [L0292.008]
5. Once the review is received, move the card to the reviewed column. [L0292.008]
6. Goal: ensure no order is forgotten and every customer receives follow-up within days of delivery. [L0292.008]

- When an order is marked as shipped in Shopify, Zapier moves its Trello card from the "Sent to Supplier" list to the "Ship Orders" list, which removes manual card movement. [L0286.013]
- **Two ways to set the delivered move are given.** When you check tracking yourself, move the card by hand on delivery (step 3 above). [L0292.008] When you automate it, set the delay between "fulfilled" and "delivered" in Trello to match the usual delivery time, for example 7 days after fulfillment. [L0359.101]
- Supplier order automation: after all configuration is complete, toggle the switch labeled "off by default" to "on". The Zap then immediately processes all new paid orders according to the rules you set. [L0283.011]
- After activating the Zap, monitor it for the first few orders. Check the Shopify admin and the BCC emails in your inbox, and confirm emails are being submitted to suppliers correctly. Verifying the initial orders confirms the setup works before you rely on it for all orders. [L0283.014]
- The Trello board is a management dashboard that needs no daily involvement. If the paid orders list holds several cards with old dates, that signals slow supplier processing or internal fulfillment gaps, so you can intervene quickly. [L0292.010]
- Order tracking and review follow-up can be self-managed or delegated to a virtual assistant. The system scales from low to high volume without manual intervention. As volume grows, systematic tracking stops orders slipping through with new or untested suppliers and keeps customer contact consistent for review collection. [L0292.012]

### Custom order automations

- This automation applies to stores that offer customized product versions (varying sizes, colors, heights or specifications), or to B2B businesses handling bulk orders of 50 or more units that require price quotes. [L0284.002]
- B2B bulk orders handled through quote forms typically involve 50 or more units per request. [L0284.014]
- Custom order forms typically capture: customer name, email address, phone number, geographic location or company, and a description of the desired product customization or specifications. [L0284.011]

Build the custom order automation in Zapier as follows:

1. Set WuFu (as written in the source) or Typeform as the trigger, selecting the "Form Completed" event. [L0284.010]
2. Add an action: send a Slack private message containing the customer name, email, phone number, location and inquiry details. [L0284.010]
3. Add a second action: insert a new row into the Google Sheets tracking sheet with all lead information. [L0284.010]
4. Both actions run automatically in sequence when the form is submitted on the website. [L0284.010]

### Customer-facing automations and thank-you notes

- Thank-you note with Scribeless (Source B, *older advice, 2021-22*):
  1. Create the thank-you note template inside Scribeless. [L0406.024]
  2. Confirm that the Scribeless and Shopify integration is active, so the note is sent automatically when an order is placed. [L0406.024]
  3. Add a step to the order-processing SOP for the virtual assistant or order processor: when an order is placed, make sure the Scribeless thank-you note is sent along with processing the order. [L0406.024]
  4. The team member only needs to check that the note has been sent, because the process is mostly automated. [L0406.024]

### Blog and content distribution

- Manually sharing a blog post to social media takes 5-15 minutes per post when a person does it. [L0312.002]
- Setting up the complete WordPress or Shopify blog automation in Zapier takes approximately 10 minutes. [L0312.005]
- Blog automation increases the visibility of content already being created, keeps social media pages active and current, maintains the perception of an active business, and saves team time on repetitive distribution tasks. [L0312.008]

### Ad account and ad launch automation

- *Older advice, Source A 2019:* Set up ad account automation once by giving Google, Facebook, Bing and Pinterest a fixed set of rules they follow on their own. Once the rules are active, campaigns run without daily manual management or an agency. [L1086.001]
- *Older advice, Source A 2019:* Automate the repetitive operational tasks staff used to do every day, then move those people toward supplier relationships, customer experience and building unique offers. The aim is to shift human time to higher-value work, not to cut people for their own sake. [L1086.002]
- Example rule: every 7 days, check Meta ad creatives performing below $35 CPA and automatically launch those winning creatives on TikTok. Apply the same automation across several platforms (Meta, TikTok, Snapchat, Pinterest) while you manage budget increases and campaign optimization by hand. [L0207.009]
- Ad Manage.ai saves 20-40 hours per month through automations, integrated analytics and one-click ad launches. It removes the need to switch between multiple platforms. [L0207.011]

### Automation versus outsourcing

- Outsourcing means hiring humans to perform business tasks. Automation means using tools, apps and bots to execute processes automatically. [L0285.001]
- Outsourcing means buying a service from a third party in place of an in-house resource, and it adds capabilities the owner lacks. It is not the same as automation, which removes the human step entirely. The two can be combined, but they solve different problems. [L1122.001]
- Example: setting up automated email sequences that nurture leads and close sales is automation, because the repeated manual work of writing and sending disappears. Paying someone to write and send those emails by hand would be outsourcing, which is a different thing. [L1122.030]
- Compare the two on cost and reliability: automation runs for about $20 per month with no sick days or turnover, while staff bring the costs and risks listed under readiness and cost above. [L0236.001, L0285.003, L0285.004]

### AI workflows, AI agents and their levels

**Automations progress through three levels.** Start with AI Workflows. [L0287.001, L0287.012]

| Level | What it does | Example | Current status | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Workflows | Linear, triggered by time or events, without AI | Daily ad reports to Slack; competitor ads to Google Drive | Reliable | [L0287.001] |
| AI Workflows | Chain multiple AI steps in a predetermined order | Scrape forum → summarize → generate angles → write scripts → save to spreadsheet | Start here | [L0287.001, L0287.012] |
| AI Agents | The system decides what to do dynamically | Email agent that chooses between responding, notifying or scheduling | Unreliable, fails frequently | [L0287.001] |

- AI Agents: current technology is unreliable and fails frequently. Do not prioritize building agents now. Agents often make unpredictable decisions or misinterpret instructions. Most tasks that seem suited to agents work more reliably as AI Workflows, which use predetermined steps with AI at each stage. Plan to revisit agents in a few months as the technology matures. [L0287.001, L0287.012]
- Example of an email agent: it can notify you in Slack about an incoming email, compose a reply using Claude or another AI model, send the reply directly to the sender, read your previous emails for context, check your calendar for availability, or create a Google Calendar event for a proposed meeting. It makes these decisions dynamically, without a predetermined sequence. [L0287.010]
- AI org charts: a hierarchy of agents where a CEO agent delegates to specialized agents such as a CMO, COO or CFO agent. Each specialized agent can manage its own sub-agents. For example, a CMO agent might supervise copywriter, competitor research, data analysis and landing page builder agents. This models how human teams scale and allows automation of complex multi-team workflows. [L0287.011]

### AI creative workflows: setup

**Source D setup (Steps 0-8 once, then Steps 9-10 for each new ad script):**

- Steps 0-8 build five reference files in a Claude or ChatGPT project: Voice Guide, Structure Library, Banned List, Avatar and Psychology Lens. Once these five files live in the project, Steps 9-10 create a new ad in a fresh chat every time. The project reads the five files automatically, so you supply only the product, winning scripts, research and chosen angle. [L0039.001]
- Set up Steps 0-8 once and upload the five reference files to your project. After that, run only Steps 9-10 for new ads. Every script session is a new chat in the same project, so the five files stay constant and you change only the angle. [L0039.027]

Procedure:

1. Gather 5-10 winning ad scripts from Kalodata or the free Meta Ad Library. Any ad running for months is a proven winner. Copy them into one document and keep it. [L0039.002]
2. Open Reddit Answers and ask six research questions, one at a time. Copy each complete answer into one research document:
   - (1) What's so hard about [SITUATION]?
   - (2) Why do people struggle with [PROBLEM]?
   - (3) What have people tried for [PROBLEM] that didn't work?
   - (4) What finally worked for people with [PROBLEM]?
   - (5) Why do people give up on [PROBLEM]?
   - (6) What do people dislike about [CATEGORY]?

   Copy everything. Don't pick and choose, because the next prompts sort it. [L0039.002]
3. Run the Step 3 prompt to reverse-engineer the concepts in your winning scripts and identify concept families and gaps. [L0039.002]
4. to 8. Run the Step 4-8 prompts to generate and download five files (Voice Guide, Structure Library, Banned List, Avatar, Psychology Lens), then upload each into your project knowledge. [L0039.002]
9. Run the Step 9 prompt in a new project chat to get a ranked angle bank. [L0039.002]
10. Run the Step 10 prompt in the same chat, pick one angle and get your script. [L0039.002]

**Conflict hierarchy (Step 10).** The Source D source names two files differently: "Voice Guide" in the setup and "Style Guide" in the rule, and "Banned List" in the setup and "Banned Blacklist" in the rule. Use the names as they appear in your project. [L0039.001, L0039.011]

Use the five-file conflict hierarchy exactly as written:

1. Style Guide governs voice first. [L0039.011]
2. Structure Library governs beats second. [L0039.011]
3. Banned Blacklist supplies hard negative constraints third. [L0039.011]
4. Avatar & Context tells you who to write to fourth. [L0039.011]
5. Psychology Lens informs but never overrides voice, fifth. [L0039.011]

Flag any brief element that contradicts a reference file instead of overriding it silently. [L0039.011]

Bottom-of-funnel ad production (Source D procedure):

1. Ask Claude: "Make me four [concept type] bottom-of-funnel ad prompts for ChatGPT." [L0316.030]
2. Copy each prompt to ChatGPT and generate image descriptions or ad text. [L0316.030]
3. Upload each generated image to Canva. [L0316.030]
4. Re-download from Canva to remove AI metadata. [L0316.030]
5. Upload all images to Meta. [L0316.030]
6. Create one ad set per concept (e.g., testimonial, free gift, benefit) with 4-8 ads inside each. [L0316.030]
7. Set the budget to $50 per day (or $15-20 AUD minimum) and test for 2-3 days. [L0316.030]

Prompt (Source E-style system brief; reproduce unchanged):

```
Set up a streamlined AI system to help our brand produce ads that perform better using data and scale more quickly. Core capabilities: 1. Getting to know customers: go through survey results, persona profiles and behavior data. Work out the real pain points, desires, objections and buying motives. Watch for emerging patterns across our segments. 2. Market and competitor research: look into rivals and the direction of the industry. Surface gaps and openings where our brand can differentiate and strengthen its positioning. 3. Creative planning and ideation: come up with new ad angles, hooks and creative concepts drawn from customer insight. Strengthen current ideas by basing them on data and proven marketing frameworks. 4. Scriptwriting and copywriting: produce persuasive, high-converting ad scripts and marketing copy. Revise copy so it matches the emotions, language and motivations of the audience. 5. Performance feedback loop: examine ROAS, CTR, hook rate, watch time, CPC and comparable metrics. Clarify why particular ads win and others fail. Turn these findings into concrete guidance for the next round of creatives. 6. Knowledge base integration: rely on all uploaded files and research as the main knowledge source. Apply insights and Breakthrough Advertising principles automatically to everything produced. Keep every answer anchored in real audience data. Benefits for the founder: converts customer feedback into clear, actionable insight; recommends creative angles and strategies matched to the audience; helps produce better ads faster from real data; supports ongoing improvement by evaluating ad results and closing the creative feedback loop.
```
[L0613.071]

### AI creative workflows: the reference-file build

- Assemble the reference files a brand's custom AI assistant needs, and keep them inside the assistant's project so its research follows the brand's own process:
  1. A brand or product overview. [L0485.031]
  2. A strategy knowledge file describing the creative method. [L0485.031]
  3. A copy quality rules file. [L0485.031]
  4. A team process overview. [L0485.031]
  5. A discovery call template. [L0485.031]
  6. Production documents for static ads and for video ads. [L0485.031]
- Use AI to analyze what customers say. A weekly review scraper, for example, can turn customer language into ad ideas. [L0485.014]
- AI is often seen only as an idea generator, script writer or visual generator. In practice, almost any task that can be written down can be completed, automated or assisted by AI. [L0485.014]
- Tools, templates and software built with AI can assist current team members or replace some roles entirely. This reduces slow manual work in creative production. [L0485.015]
- A coded workflow connects AI tools and software so each step passes its output to the next. A typical chain:
  1. The custom AI assistant creates a prompt-based output. [L0485.025]
  2. The workflow sends it to a platform such as Arcads to create the ad. [L0485.025]
  3. The result is saved to a spreadsheet. [L0485.025]
  4. A human is notified on Slack to review it. [L0485.025]
- Workflows save admin time and speed up the process, but a custom assistant or pre-built platform already speeds up work without them. Understanding how to build and use the assistant and the platforms comes first. [L0485.028]

### Creative team roles and the human-AI balance

- A traditional creative team with fixed roles (strategist, copywriter, editor, designer) can be replaced by a breakdown into processes and tasks. Each task can be done by a person or an AI agent, as long as the process is predictable and can be written as step-by-step instructions. [L0464.001]
- Break every team role into processes, then into individual tasks from start to finish. Check which tasks can be automated or handed to AI. Any task that can be described as step-by-step instructions can in principle be completed by AI. [L0464.002]
- Any task a person can be trained to do can in principle be trained into an AI agent. The AI can run the same task up to 100 times faster than a human. [L0464.003]
- Projected capability (a forward-looking estimate, not a measured result): once the software and workflow are set up, AI could return creative test projects within about 10 seconds, and a full creative test cycle could be completed in about 20 minutes. [L0464.004]
- Measured experience: the first AI-produced videos took about one hour each while the creator was learning the tools. Once the workflow is familiar, the same production time drops to about 20 to 30 minutes per video. [L0888.028]
- Some brands can run with one creative team member who oversees a group of AI agents. This works only with a well-defined, reliable workflow and a human who reviews the output before it goes live. [L0464.005]
- The right balance of humans and AI is a decision for each brand, based on its budget, niche, existing team and required approvals. Work out this balance before choosing tools or team size. [L0464.008]
- A creative strategist's typical tasks:
  - Analyzing what customers say. [L0464.009]
  - Identifying likely buyers and their motivations. [L0464.009]
  - Extracting core ideas for ads. [L0464.009]
  - Finding ideas and styles not yet tested. [L0464.009]
  - Running product, competitor and market analysis. [L0464.009]
  - Briefing concepts for ad assets. [L0464.009]
  - Reviewing the team's work. [L0464.009]
- Creative systems can be organized into stages covering conception, planning, production and management of ads. Each stage can use its own AI tools and written SOPs or instructions. [L0464.010]
- One AI prompt can set creative KPIs and check each team member's launch output per product. This is a simple way to confirm the creative team is delivering against its targets. [L0885.022]

### Production: batches, images and copy

- Run a small test batch of 2 or 3 images before a full run of 50, because each image and each retry costs credits. Scale up only after the test outputs look right. [L0794.048]
- Batch rollout order:
  1. Write one collection page with the prompt. Check the format, style and length against your template. [L0883.025]
  2. Refine the prompt until the output matches what you want. [L0883.025]
  3. Run all collection keywords together as an asynchronous batch, for example 20 collections in one run. [L0883.025]
  4. Write one blog post the same way, and refine the prompt until its format, style and length are right. [L0883.025]
  5. Run the remaining blog posts with more automation, relying more on the AI. [L0883.025]
  6. Have a team member or VA edit and fact-check every output. [L0883.025]
  7. Publish the edited copy to the site. [L0883.025]
- One batch covered 20 collection pages for one brand in a single asynchronous run. The operator started it, left the computer and came back to finished drafts ready for editing. [L0883.026]
- AI can take over work that used to require expensive freelancers or employees: drafting supplier approval messages, checking store pages for compliance before ads go live, and converting supplier spreadsheets. This saves weeks or months on large product catalogs. [L1097.041]
- Photoshop batch automation for product images (Source B, *older advice, 2021-22*): a freelancer found on OnlineJobs.ph can build a folder-based automation that resizes images, places them on a white background if needed, and compresses them. It then runs unattended at about $10 per 100 images. [L1456.044]
- Outreach automation tools (Source C):
  - Lemlist and Instantly are email automation tools for outreach. Import a list with first name, last name and email address, set up a sequence of follow-up emails, and the automation stops as soon as the recipient replies. [L0789.010]
  - Instantly allows 25 sending email addresses, each able to send 50 emails per day. Use several sending inboxes to scale outreach volume in a country without overloading one address. [L0789.011]

### Limits of AI workflows and creative automation

- Keep a human review step in any AI creative workflow. AI still makes mistakes, such as errors in content or visuals, so the review checks output before ads are published. [L0464.006]
- Have a human edit and fact-check every AI output before it goes live, even when the prompt is well tuned. The first draft can contain wrong product claims or inaccuracies. [L0883.021]
- Identify the tasks AI cannot yet do well, such as tools that do not exist for a combination of tasks, or tasks that are hard to explain in writing. For those tasks, find a workaround or keep a human, and use this list to cut costs. [L0464.007]
- The practical limits of an AI creative workflow are:
  - the number of separate software tools involved; [L0464.025]
  - the connections needed between platforms; [L0464.025]
  - whether the workflow is set up reliably enough to give predictable output. [L0464.025]

  Design the workflow so these three points are solved before adding more volume. [L0464.025]
- Automation handles rule-based tasks, not exceptions, negotiation or creative decisions. Use the time it frees for those. [L0286.036]
- AI agents are unreliable for now, so use AI Workflows with predetermined steps. [L0287.012]

**Summary of limits by approach**

| Approach | Strength | Limit | Source |
|---|---|---|---|
| Rule-based automation (Zapier, Trello) | Reliable once configured, no sick days or turnover | Only rule-based tasks; exceptions still need a human | [L0285.003, L0286.036] |
| AI Workflows | Predictable, step by step | Each AI output needs human review and fact-checking | [L0287.012, L0883.021] |
| AI Agents | Dynamic decisions | Unreliable, fails frequently, unpredictable decisions | [L0287.001, L0287.012] |
| AI creative production | Speed; projected 10-second tests and 20-minute cycles, measured 20-30 minutes per video | Human review, tool count, connections and reliability limit scale | [L0464.004, L0888.028, L0464.006, L0464.025] |

## Choosing a hiring model and sequencing hires

### Start from the problem, not the provider

- Asking someone who profits from your purchase whether you need their service creates inherent bias, because the vendor gains from a yes regardless of your actual needs. Set your requirements independently before talking to any provider. [L0217.001]
- Define the exact problem before choosing a solution. Document which capabilities are missing, what jobs need doing, and what success looks like before engaging any service provider. [L0217.003]
- Five hiring models exist to solve business problems: freelancer, agency, full-time employee, part-time employee, and contractor. Choose the model that fits your specific problem and business stage, not the reverse. [L0217.002]
- Most outsourcing problems come from weak hiring and weak ongoing management, not from outsourcing itself. A bad outcome usually means the wrong person was selected or was not managed effectively. [L1055.001]

#### Outsourcing preparation (Source B, 2021-22, older)

Procedure:
1. Identify which tasks from your 180-day plan could be outsourced. [L0223.012]
2. Create a Standard Operating Procedure (SOP) for each task to be outsourced, documenting exactly how it should be done. [L0223.012]
3. Write job postings or advertisements for those positions. [L0223.012]
4. Conduct interviews with candidates using provided template questions. [L0223.012]
5. Test by conducting interviews via message format for efficiency. [L0223.012]
6. Hire team members as positions are filled and bring them into your management system. [L0223.012]

#### Hiring process

Procedure (Source A), repeat for every new role to keep consistency and speed:
1. Know who to hire: job roles and organizational structure. [L0263.008]
2. Create and post the job, and design the filtering process. [L0263.008]
3. Run interviews: a short call and a final call. [L0263.008]
4. Onboard systematically. [L0263.008]

Four hiring stages, in this order (Source B, 2021-22, older):
1. Post the job so candidates can find it. [L1055.002]
2. Shortlist the applicants. [L1055.002]
3. Interview the shortlisted applicants. [L1055.002]
4. Trial the successful candidates. The trial overlaps with onboarding and is the last point where you decide whether to keep someone long term. [L1055.002]

- Hire for one clear job at a time rather than a generalist, because one person rarely does sales, uploads, writing and support well. Test each candidate with a paid trial task and check how quickly they finish it. Hire part-time first and convert to full time when results are good (Source B, 2021-22, older). [L1396.048]
- Breakdown example (Source B, 2021-22, older): a one-year goal of growing a customer service team by three people is split into three rocks, one new hire per quarter. Each hiring rock is broken into tasks: write the job, create the job advertisement, post it, shortlist, interview, make the hiring decision, and provide training if needed. These tasks are spread across weekly to-do lists over the 90 days. [L0679.015]

### Economics of each hiring model

#### Agency versus in-house

- For businesses under $10 million annual revenue, hiring a specialized agency is usually mathematically cheaper than hiring full-time specialists in-house. [L0217.013] (For reference, $10 million a year is about $833,000 per month on average. This figure is derived, not from the source.)
- In-house senior positions in media buying, growth strategy or paid ads cost $30,000 to $40,000 per month in additional fixed expenses. Equivalent agency services cost $12,000 to $15,000 per month. [L0217.014]
- Example: a creative agency charging $3,000 per month for 5 to 7 pieces of creative, or 10 creatives per month, is often more economical than a junior in-house designer at $5,000 per month (equal to a $60,000 annual salary). [L0217.015]
- Once annual revenue exceeds $10 million, hiring specialized in-house roles becomes economically viable because the workload justifies full-time dedication and better efficiency. [L0217.016]
- Where the sources differ: Source E sets the in-house break-even for specialist roles at $10 million annual revenue. Source C uses monthly revenue phases instead (see the revenue stages below) and moves to agencies for social platforms the store does not advertise on at $500,000 to $1 million+ per month. Use the Source E test for specialist roles and the Source C phases for sequencing. [L0217.013, L0217.016, L1129.002]
- Hire an agency or outside help for ongoing ad management before building an in-house team, unless the threshold above is met. [L0217.013, L0217.014]

#### Benchmark monthly costs

| Role or service | Cost stated in source | Source ids |
|---|---|---|
| Video editor | $20 for three videos per day | L0637.046 |
| Customer success agents | $200 to $300 per month | L0637.046 |
| Email marketing agency | About $2,000 per month | L0637.046, L0637.070 |
| Google Ads management | About $1,500 per month | L0637.046, L0637.070 |
| Influencer outreach manager (finds and contacts creators) | $300 to $500 per month | L0637.070 |
| Virtual assistant for order fulfillment and customer support | $300 to $500 per month, varies with tasks | L0765.009 |
| First assistant for fulfillment and support | $300 to $400 per month | L1431.017 |
| Long-tenured team manager who handled everything | About $1,500 per month | L1431.017 |
| Assistants paid around $100 per month (poor quality) | About $100 per month | L1431.018 |
| Typical VA salary, paid from store profit | $300 to $800 per month | L1090.134 |
| Customer service VA (Source B, 2021-22, older) | About $1,000 per month | L1334.028 |
| Order processing and supplier coordination (Source B, 2021-22, older) | About $600 per month | L1334.028 |
| Catalog manager (Source B, 2021-22, older) | About $1,200 per month | L1334.028 |
| Customer service manager (Source B, 2021-22, older) | About $1,500 per month | L1334.028 |
| SEO person (Source B, 2021-22, older) | About $800 per month | L1334.028 |
| Sales rep (Source B, 2021-22, older) | About $1,000 per month | L1334.028 |
| Product uploader (Source B, 2021-22, older) | About $500 per month | L1334.028 |

- Role costs are not universal. Check them on freelance marketplaces and online job boards. The lesson's example estimates assume Filipino virtual assistants. [L1334.026]
- Cost benchmarks for team planning: use the figures above as a starting point. [L0637.070]
- Where VA costs differ across sources: $300 to $500 per month applies to fulfillment and support combined [L0765.009]. $300 to $800 per month is the salary range to fund from profit [L1090.134]. Source E's example uses $400 per month for one low-cost offshore worker, which is the level it says cannot cover a broad skill set [L1359.017]. The $1,000 customer service figure is Source B's estimate, which assumes Filipino VAs [L1334.028, L1334.026].

#### Hourly rates

- A VA for product research or customer service can be hired on Upwork, which was preferred over Fiverr. A VA paid around $3 to $5 per hour was described as a reasonable rate. [L1127.032]
- Hourly rates by phase (Source C): overseas customer service $2 to $6 per hour; project manager $8 to $12 per hour; supply chain manager $6 to $15 per hour; payment processing agent $8 to $12 per hour. [L1129.002]
- Hire only when the new person's hourly cost is lower than your own hourly value, so the difference is margin. Example: if you are worth $20 per hour, look for help at $10 per hour. [L1359.011]
- Pay the best person you can afford. Low pay tends to produce low-quality results, and paying a higher rate to skilled people is usually cheaper than fixing poor work. [L1000.023]
- Cheap assistants can be expensive. Assistants paid around $100 per month delivered poor quality work, while freelancers on platforms such as Upwork gave better results. [L1431.018]

#### Efficiency-adjusted cost

Formula:

Efficiency-adjusted hourly cost = hourly wage ÷ share of output the worker actually achieves [L1359.020]

Example (derived from the lesson's figures, for illustration): $5 per hour at 20 percent efficiency costs $25 per hour of full-output work, which is more than $15 per hour at 100 percent efficiency. [L1359.020]

#### Offshore versus US hiring

- A Filipino VA working full-time at about AUD 26,000 per year, all costs included, was compared with an Australian hire of the same qualifications at about AUD 75,000 per year with on-costs. Overseas staffing is the cost-effective option for small businesses. [L1122.016]
- Five US staff costing about $20,000 per month can be replaced by Philippine staff costing about $5,000 to $6,000 per month, but only with the right processes and people in place. [L1359.019]
- Hire all team members internationally, not from the US. Build from the Philippines, Bangladesh and South America via onlinejobs.ph, because of the lower cost base, strong customer service experience in those regions (BPO industry), cultural fit, and alignment on work commitment. [L0263.047]
- Filipino VAs are a common choice for email, live chat and order processing because their written English is strong and they have experience with outsourced support work. Their accent makes them a poor fit for phone calls where it could hurt conversion. [L1430.008]
- If the task needs knowledge of a specific culture, market or sport, such as a regional audience or a local sport, hire someone from that market. An offshore assistant can only handle basic tasks that need no deep cultural knowledge. [L1359.016]

### Choosing the model for each job

| Model | Use it for | Source ids |
|---|---|---|
| Freelancer | Task-based work with one-off deliverables, such as a batch of product descriptions, image editing or design | L1122.013 |
| Gig (paid per job) | One-off tasks such as product uploads, on Upwork or StoreTasker | L0360.080 |
| Task-based freelancer | Early stores: specific jobs like product page SEO, product uploads, or CSV work, rather than retainers | L0856.100 |
| Virtual assistant (VA) | Ongoing work such as customer service or social media | L1122.013 |
| Managed VA service | Ongoing VA work where a company employs the assistant and handles management | L1122.029 |
| Full-time employee (remote) | Repeatable, long-term work that continues on future sites | L1359.013, L263.003 |
| Contractor | One-off projects that will not repeat, professional services you cannot afford full-time (for example a developer at $60 per hour), and highly technical work outside your core competence, part-time | L1359.013 |

- Call team members "team members" or "employees", never "virtual assistants" or "VAs". This framing determines whether you build a scalable team or stay limited to two or three assistants. [L0263.002]
- Hire remote full-time employees, not freelancers. Freelancers juggle multiple clients and will not invest in your systems, training and growth. Define this in job postings and screening to filter for people seeking stable employment who will commit fully to your business. [L0263.003]
- Use freelancers for task-based work, and virtual assistants for ongoing work. A VA can be a freelancer, but a freelancer is not always a VA. [L1122.013]
- Virtual Assistant Finder runs the full recruitment process for a fee: you describe the need and the agency finds candidates. 123 Employee is a managed VA provider for US businesses that offers Filipino VAs at 10, 30 or 40 hours per week, which suits owners not ready for a full-time hire. [L1122.015]
- For an ongoing VA, you can hire a freelancer directly or use a managed VA service. A managed service usually gives more consistent results. With a freelancer, the whole selection process, from vetting to testing, is your responsibility. [L1122.029]
- Choose a VA for work with objective, process-based decisions. Choose an apprentice for work that needs judgment, adaptation when the roadmap runs out, or subjective calls. VAs can handle more adapting work, but it is harder. [L1359.015]
- Hire for one skill set per role, and keep the core skill of the worker matched to the task. A worker hired as a developer should not also be asked to write blog content. [L1359.018]
- Hiring for advertising and content roles is harder than for customer service and product listing. Fewer qualified candidates exist on onlinejobs.ph for these skills. Expect a longer process and fewer applicants. Build your own email marketer internally and train them rather than relying on agencies. [L0263.040]

### Specialists and generalists

#### Strengths and weaknesses

- Specialists in specific skills (for example video editors) deliver high-quality output quickly, because they understand their domain deeply and have practiced it extensively. [L0260.005]
- Avoid staffing entirely with specialists. They work in silos, lack versatility outside their skill set, may require larger teams to scale, and typically cannot also manage other team members. [L0260.006]
- Generalists handle multiple varied tasks with agility, adapt to changing roles, are cost-effective for smaller teams, tend to have stronger leadership potential, and bring fresh ideas from outside a single domain. [L0260.007]
- A team of only generalists lacks depth in specialized outputs. Their energy and priorities can disperse across many deliverables, making them vulnerable to conflicting demands unless priorities are explicitly aligned. [L0260.008]
- Maintain a healthy balance of specialists and generalists to gain speed from deep expertise, fresh thinking from diverse perspectives, and leadership capacity for growth. [L0260.009]
- Hire hybrid roles where specialists extend beyond their core skill set, for example a video editor who also handles animation and graphic design. These rare talents give the team valuable flexibility. [L0260.010]

#### Avoid the Super VA myth

- Expecting one low-cost offshore worker to cover unrelated skill sets, such as coding, customer calls, writing and podcast editing, is a mistake. A $100,000 USD job cannot realistically be pushed onto one worker paid $400 per month. [L1359.017]

#### Generalists trained on your method

- Training a generalist assistant on your own tested ad method worked better than hiring a specialist who only claimed Facebook ads experience. Teach the assistant your tested method first, then let them run their own variations. [L1431.065]

### Virtual assistant scope and first tasks

#### What a VA takes over

Typical tasks a virtual assistant can take over:

| Area | Tasks |
|---|---|
| Order fulfillment | Make sure orders are fulfilled every day and tracking numbers are sent to customers |
| Customer support | Answer emails, DMs, social media comments and live chat questions from customers |
| Store management | Create the store, do product research, manage PayPal and Stripe cases, stay in touch with the supplier, set up social media pages, run ads |
| Random tasks | Any task the assistant is qualified for, e.g. managing finances |

[L0765.006]

- Build a VA team through a VA agency. Hand off store building, customer support and ordering to the team while the owner supervises. Keep order fulfillment in-house as long as it is manageable. [L0492.059]
- A first VA works best on a set of closely related tasks, such as customer service across phone, live chat, email, social comments and messages, rather than one all-purpose person. Starting at about four hours per day and growing toward eight as the business scales is a practical pattern. [L0360.079]
- Hire a VA to fill skill gaps such as supplier negotiation, customer support quality or store graphics. The assistant can cover areas where the owner lacks experience. [L0765.004]
- A new store's team can be built from a VA who learns the SEO guide and makes small improvements to product pages each week (2023). [L0855.170]
- One-off tasks such as product uploads are better handled as fixed gigs on Upwork or StoreTasker, paid per job, than by a dedicated VA on set hours. [L0360.080]
- A VA is especially useful for beginners in dropshipping who feel overwhelmed. They can help build the store, perform product research, and answer questions when the owner is unsure. [L1418.010]
- Beginners who feel overwhelmed can benefit from a VA for store building, product research, and answering questions when they are unsure. Support of this kind can speed up early progress. [L1433.010]
- Customer service and product research: a VA for either can be hired on Upwork. [L1127.032]

#### Order-volume and margin gates

- Hire a VA only once sales are consistent, meaning at least 5 to 10 orders per day. Before that point, the workload is usually too small to justify the cost. [L0765.001]
- Hire a VA once you consistently get about 5 to 10 orders per day for at least three weeks and can no longer keep up with emails and comments. Record a Loom video of the process so the VA can start work quickly. [L1218.023]
- Hire a VA when your profit margin is high enough to pay for the assistant and still leave extra income. If margin cannot cover the cost, hiring will drain the business. [L0765.002]
- Hire a VA for order processing, email, live chat and turning visitors into shoppers only when business profits can pay for outsourcing, and only after you know each process yourself. [L0941.096]
- Do not hire virtual assistants until the store is profitable and you have run it yourself for about a month. Document each process as a playbook so a new hire can take over tasks with clear instructions. [L0941.095]
- Handle customer questions yourself first so you learn what people ask and how to answer them. Do not hire staff or outsource work until the business's own profit pays for it. [L1287.008]
- Before hiring a VA, check that the business can pay their monthly wage. Do not hire someone and let them go a month later, because they depend on that income. [L1437.020]
- Hire a VA once the business can pay for them from profit. A typical VA salary is roughly $300 to $800 per month, which should come from store profit before you hire. [L1090.134]
- Outsource to a team of VAs, based in the Philippines and hired through Online Jobs PH, once the store is making sales and getting traffic. Hire to offload day-to-day tasks and keep yourself in an oversight role (2021). [L1090.136]
- Start outsourcing when the store is profitable and the owner feels overwhelmed by daily work. The first hire is usually a VA (2019, older). [L1275.051]

### Revenue and profit stages

#### Monthly net profit thresholds (Source A)

- The first hire should be customer service, made once the store nets $2,000 of profit per month. Hiring is tied to actual monthly net profit. [L1446.024]
- The second hire should be a person to upload and optimize products and fix existing pages, made once the store nets $3,500 of profit per month. [L1446.025]
- Marketing should be outsourced once the store nets $5,000 of profit per month. This is the third stage, after customer service and product upload. [L1446.026]
- Hiring should follow profit stages rather than the hope of a successful launch that makes many hires possible. Tying each role to monthly net profit keeps the team funded by real earnings. [L1446.028]

Procedure:
1. Draw the long-term organization of the company as a flowchart or on paper. [L1446.027]
2. List the roles you want in the future. [L1446.027]
3. Assign each role a monthly net profit threshold. [L1446.027]
4. Hire each role only when its threshold is reached, in order. [L1446.027]

#### Monthly revenue phases (Source C)

| Phase | Monthly revenue | Roles to add | Hourly rate mentioned |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | $0-20k | Customer service agent(s); fulfillment agent(s) if dropshipping; influencer outreach agent | Overseas customer service $2-6/hr; other roles not priced |
| 2 | $20-100k | Project manager; email marketing (optional); media buyer (optional, first hire recommended in this phase) | Project manager $8-12/hr; email via referral or Upwork |
| 3 | $100k-500k | Supply chain manager; payment processing agent | Supply chain manager $6-15/hr; payment processing agent $8-12/hr |
| 4 | $500k-1M+ | Agencies for social platforms the store does not advertise on | Not priced |

[L1129.002]

- Below $20k per month, the only priority is traction. Make only one or two essential hires to free the owner's time for growth. Those hires should take over work that does not need the owner. [L1129.003]
- The first media buyer should be hired in phase 2 ($20-100k per month), once customer service, influencer outreach and project management are covered. At that point the owner is mainly left with paid ads. [L1129.011]
- Media buyers usually match their price, so a cheaply hired entry-level buyer will need training time before performing well. Budget that training time or pay for a more experienced media buyer, with Upwork named as a source. [L1129.012]
- Above $100k per month, new problems appear that the owner did not expect earlier. More specialist roles are needed to keep the store running at that scale. [L1129.013]

#### Three stages by revenue or profit (Source C)

Build a VA team in three stages matched to revenue:
- Beginners (roughly $5,000 to $50,000 monthly revenue) keep store building and product descriptions partly in their own hands. [L1432.001]
- Advanced sellers (roughly $5,000 to $20,000 monthly profit) delegate order fulfillment and customer support first. [L1432.001]
- Business owners (around $20,000 plus monthly profit) run the business almost fully on autopilot, with a team handling fulfillment, support, live chat, finances and ad management. [L1432.001]

- Where the thresholds differ: the Source C phases are set by monthly revenue ($20,000 and $100,000 breakpoints), while Source A's thresholds are set by monthly net profit ($2,000, $3,500 and $5,000). Use profit thresholds to decide whether a hire is affordable, and revenue phases to anticipate which roles the business will need. [L1446.024, L1446.025, L1446.026, L1129.002, L1432.001]

#### Build the team when the need appears

- Build a team when you realise you need other people. Identify the skills the business needs and search for people who have them. This is one of the first signs of real business growth. [L1438.051]
- As the business grows, you can build a team by hiring employees or VAs to take over work. In the beginning, the owner does most of the work themselves. [L1480.030]
- Low-ticket dropshipping at scale requires a large team of VAs and freelancers to handle orders, shipping follow-up and customer service. High-ticket dropshipping needs far fewer orders, and so far fewer staff. [L1481.023]

### Recommended hire order

#### Sequence from the sources

Procedure (Source A), each function scaled before adding complexity:
1. Start with product listing specialists. Why: they enable unlimited brands, because you manage suppliers. [L0263.006]
2. Add a supplier relations specialist, who manages supplier outreach and partnerships. [L0263.006]
3. Add a customer service team, with pre-purchase and post-purchase roles. [L0263.006]
4. Add an operations manager, who oversees fulfillment and is the biggest team, in the middle of the organization. [L0263.006]
5. Add a bookkeeper, who tracks costs, reconciles, and finds unprofitable orders. [L0263.006]
6. Add advertising, content and email specialists. [L0263.006]
7. Build each store with the same structure. [L0263.006]

Procedure (Source A, 2021, older):
1. Hire one person to handle support (live chat, phone and email) and order processing. [L0834.102]
2. Add a second person when inbound volume grows. [L0834.102]
3. Hire part-time (around 20 hours a month) for social media and website content once repetitive tasks are offloaded. [L0834.102]
4. Test content yourself first, and hand it off once you know what works. [L0834.102]

Procedure (Source B, 2021-22, older), typical hiring order for a drop shipping operation (costs per month are estimates):
1. Hire a customer service VA first, at about $1,000, since customer communication usually takes most of the owner's time. [L1334.028]
2. When one VA is overloaded, split the role into customer service and order processing, the latter at about $600 for order management and supplier coordination. [L1334.028]
3. Hire a catalog manager at about $1,200 to keep product information, listings and stock current. [L1334.028]
4. Hire a customer service manager at about $1,500 so the owner no longer answers the team's questions and only hears about resolved problems. [L1334.028]
5. Once the operation is stable, hire growth roles one at a time: an SEO person at about $800, a PPC person for paid ads, and a content writer for blog posts and emails, all under a marketing manager. [L1334.028, L1334.024]
6. When the business gets busy, hire a sales rep at about $1,000 and split sales off from customer service. [L1334.028]
7. Add a product uploader at about $500 for data entry when product volume requires it. [L1334.028]
8. Add a finance and admin role for bookkeeping, Profit First planning and financial reporting, and outsource tax work to an accountant. [L1334.028]

Procedure (Source C):
1. Hire first for order fulfillment and customer support. [L1431.016]
2. Next hire someone to build stores. [L1431.016]
3. Then hire someone to run ads, because each role needs different skills. [L1431.016]

- Outsource customer service first, then order fulfillment (if no agent or supplier already handles it), and then agencies that run ads. VAs for fulfillment are not needed when an agent or supplier already does the work. [L0780.020]
- For a product business, hire a dedicated operations or supply-chain lead before the founders are overloaded. A hire with prior operations experience at a large company can take over supply-chain work the founders were handling themselves. [L0334.061]
- Hire the first helper quickly, even part-time and for a single task, instead of waiting until the full team is planned. Each task moved off the owner's plate frees time for growth work. [L0337.004]
- Hire one full-time employee responsible for both customer service (email and chat) and order management (status updates and supplier coordination). [L0230.010]
- Build an in-house team through a VA agency, and keep order fulfillment in-house as long as it is manageable. [L0492.059]

#### Comparison of first, second and third hires

| Source | First hire | Second hire | Third hire |
|---|---|---|---|
| Source A, profit stages | Customer service at $2,000 net profit per month | Product upload and optimization at $3,500 net profit per month | Outsourced marketing at $5,000 net profit per month |
| Source A, 2021 (older) | Support (live chat, phone, email) and order processing | Second person when inbound volume grows | Part-time social media and website content (about 20 hours per month) |
| Source B, 2021-22 (older) | Customer service VA (about $1,000 per month) | Order processing (about $600 per month) | Catalog manager (about $1,200 per month) |
| Source C | Order fulfillment and customer support | Store builder | Ads |
| Source C, revenue phases | Phase 1 ($0-20k): customer service agents, fulfillment agents if dropshipping, influencer outreach | Phase 2 ($20-100k): project manager, then first media buyer | Phase 3 ($100k-500k): supply chain manager, payment processing agent |

Sources: L1446.024-L1446.026, L0834.102, L1334.028, L1431.016, L1129.002, L1129.011

- Customer service is the first hire in most sources, and the first hire in Source A's profit stages, Source B's order, and the Source C fulfillment and support role. The second and third hires differ between sources, so use the one that matches your store's bottleneck. [L1446.024, L1334.028, L1431.016, L780.020, L852.101]
- The first VA for phone and email support frees the owner to focus on traffic and supplier relationships, and it improves customer service (2020, older). [L0852.101]
- Growth roles can be hired one by one under a marketing manager. Hiring someone to work through the training modules and implement them is a valid first step before specialists (Source B, 2021-22, older). [L1334.024]

#### Case: team build-up timeline

Store team timeline (Source A):
- 2014: A dropshipping store started on a different model. The owner learned on their own and saw it could not reach financial independence. [L0337.018]
- 2016: The owner switched to the Source A method, still working a corporate job and handling orders and customer calls in early mornings, nights and breaks. [L0337.018]
- 2017: A part-time phone helper was hired and paid per answered call. The owner still worked the full-time job and got burnt out. [L0337.018]
- 2018: A full-time virtual assistant was hired for operations. The owner left the corporate job. [L0337.018]
- 2020: A team of three, then four (including a US hire with experience and a second US hire). Revenue reached $1.4 million. The team handled operations, customer service, and an outsourced social media, ads and accounting function. [L0337.018]
- 2021: A fourth virtual assistant was hired. The team handled all day-to-day operations and escalations, so the owner only did sales, some marketing and accounting, from anywhere. [L0337.018]

#### Affordability check before each hire

- Do not hire virtual assistants until the store is profitable and you have run it yourself for about a month. [L0941.095]
- Tie each hire to the profit it can be paid from, and do not hire until the business's own profit pays for it. [L1287.008, L1437.020]

### What to hire first

- Start with the job that takes the most of your time and needs no judgment. In most sources that is customer communication. [L1334.028, L1431.016, L0780.020]
- When the business needs a skill you do not have, hire for that gap, for example supplier negotiation, customer support quality or store graphics. [L0765.004]
- Before the first hire, document each process as a playbook, so a new hire can take over tasks with clear instructions. [L0941.095]
- Hire a VA for order fulfillment and support first, then a person to build stores, then one to run ads, because each role needs different skills. [L1431.016]
- Test content yourself first and hand it off once you know what works. [L0834.102]
- Hire the first helper quickly, even part-time, and for a single task. [L0337.004]
- Hiring for advertising and content roles takes longer. Build your own email marketer internally and train them. [L0263.040]
- When a media buyer is needed, the first one should come in phase 2 of the revenue stages, once customer service, influencer outreach and project management are covered. [L1129.011]

## Sourcing candidates and writing job posts

### Choosing where to source candidates

#### Platform by role

| Role or task | Platform | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Long-term full-time or part-time VA from the Philippines | OnlineJobs.ph | L0230.011, L0801.154, L0855.164, L1473.017 |
| Short-term, project-based or contract work | Upwork | L0230.012, L0921.028 |
| Full-time customer service and order management | OnlineJobs.ph | L1473.017 |
| Product uploads (part-time or full-time staff) | OnlineJobs.ph | L0971.006 |
| Product uploads (contract work) | Upwork | L0971.006 |
| Repetitive tasks such as product uploads | Fiverr (not design work) | L0966.090 |
| Writers, editors, designers, data entry | Upwork (Odesk merged into Upwork in 2015) | L0380.018 |
| Written blog content | ProBlogger | L0379.010 |
| Posting content (uploading blog posts with images and videos) | OnlineJobs.ph, Upwork | L0379.009, L0379.010 |
| Video editing | OnlineJobs.ph, Fiverr | L0379.009, L0379.010 |
| Custom image design | Fiverr | L0379.009, L0379.010 |
| Design work | 99designs | L1122.014 |
| Developers for coding on a website | TopTool | L1122.014 |
| One-off Shopify specialist or development projects | Store Tasker | L0801.155, L1283.023 |
| Shopify bookkeeper | Upwork (search for Shopify bookkeeping) | L0855.167 |
| Klaviyo specialist | Upwork or similar sites | L0855.169 |
| Native English speakers for live call work | Upwork, Craigslist | L0910.046 |
| Back-office work | OnlineJobs.ph | L0910.046 |
| Low-cost remote staff for customer service, fulfilment, influencer outreach, project management, payment processing, supply chain | Monazar, Upwork | L1129.004 |
| Shopify dropshipping VAs pre-screened by skill | VA agencies | L1431.019 |

#### Platform notes

- OnlineJobs.ph is a platform for finding Philippine-based virtual assistants to delegate store tasks and repetitive operational work. [L0345.004]
- OnlineJobs.ph is a recruiting platform for long-term virtual assistants. [L0230.011]
- Upwork is a freelancer marketplace for VAs, designers, developers and other contractors for store tasks. Compare profiles and reviews before hiring. [L0675.012]
- Upwork is the hiring platform for VAs, where both agencies and freelancers are listed. [L0832.042, L1430.009]
- A freelancer suits a simple first hire. An agency may suit larger needs. [L0832.042]
- Upwork and Fiverr are task-based freelance marketplaces. [L1122.014]
- Freelance platforms for VAs include Upwork (formerly Odesk), Freelancer, OnlineJobs.ph, Fiverr and Craigslist. [L0910.046]
- Upwork tends to offer contract or task-based freelancers for specific projects. OnlineJobs.ph is better for finding long-term team members. The quality range is wide on both. [L0921.028]
- Fiverr suits repetitive tasks such as product uploads, but not design work. [L0966.090]
- Store Tasker is a good source for one-off development and Shopify expert projects, not for cheap bulk product uploads. [L0801.155]
- For one-off website tasks, storetasker.com suits Shopify specialists and upwork.com suits one-off jobs. [L1283.023]
- Monazar (monazar.com/vas) and Upwork are used to hire low-cost remote staff. [L1129.004]
- Common platforms for finding VAs are Upwork, Fiverr and Monazar Marketing GmbH. Other platforms exist, but these are the ones with documented experience. [L0765.007]
- VA agencies that pre-screen candidates for Shopify dropshipping skills are a hiring source. [L1431.019]
- Pakistan was named as a source for a long-term team of about 20 staff. [L1431.019]
- Outsourcing platforms for product uploads: OnlineJobs.ph for part-time or full-time staff, and Upwork for contract work. [L0971.006]
- Onlinejobs.ph is the source used to recruit the first virtual assistant. [L1437.023]
- onlinejobs.ph is one source for the first VA hires that the SOP set is designed to support. [L1340.009]
- The Empire Flippers outsourcing guide and its podcast episodes 9 and 62 are a reference for the VA hiring process. [L1441.026]

#### Platform fit by task type

- OnlineJobs.ph suits a long-term part-time or full-time team member, not a one-off project such as a single product upload. [L0855.164]
- Upwork was used to find a large number of qualified VAs for listing and data tasks. [L0801.154]
- Upwork worked only for small one-off tasks such as coding and product research and did not produce good full-time hires. [L1473.017]
- Over the years a business can build a team of around 15 from OnlineJobs.ph. It is a practical long-term source. [L0970.154]
- OnlineJobs.ph was used to hire overseas staff for product uploads and customer service. [L0671.086, L0671.223]
- For hiring VAs to post content, use OnlineJobs.ph or Upwork. [L0379.009]
- For writers, the template-based job post on ProBlogger is the specialised option. On general freelance platforms such as Upwork, many applicants claim writing skills they do not have. Sifting is slower and more error-prone there than on a specialised blog-writer board. [L0379.010, L0379.011]
- Upwork and Craigslist are better for finding native English speakers for live call work. OnlineJobs.ph is named for back-office work. [L0910.046]
- Searching for phone-specific gigs yields few results, because few qualified people want telemarketing or cold calls. Search for an all-around VA or customer service profile, then add answering calls as a side task. [L0910.047]

#### Older platform data (2018 to 2022)

- Filipino VAs usually speak English well, can handle phone support and often work reverse hours. A night shift can cover your daytime in the U.S. (older, 2018) [L1274.076]
- OnlineJobs.ph was used to hire remote customer service staff from the Philippines at low wages, for support tickets, damage claims and technical troubleshooting. (older, 2021) [L0270.022]
- OnlineJobs.ph was recommended for hiring VAs on an hourly rate basis, and the company built large teams through it over many years. (older, 2022) [L0272.049]
- For product uploads and similar tasks, look for experienced workers in the Philippines or Vietnam, which offer fair rates. (older, 2019) [L1275.052]

#### Cost of posting and platform fees

Two sources from 2019 describe OnlineJobs.ph pricing differently. Both are older. Check current terms before relying on either.

- **Source A (2019):** Upwork is free to post jobs and hire freelancers. OnlineJobs.ph charges $99 monthly (not per job) to access pre-vetted full-time Filipino employees. [L0243.018]
- **Source B (2019):** Post hiring jobs on Upwork or OnlineJobs.ph. Expect hundreds of applicants, so shortlist and test several candidates at the same time. OnlineJobs.ph charges more to post a job. [L0467.069]

#### Freelancers, agencies and trust

- Upwork and Fiverr carry a high risk of scams. A trusted assistant agency or company, or a free trial period, reduces that risk. [L1432.032]
- Fiverr carries lower hiring risk than Upwork. The buyer's payment is released to the VA only after the buyer accepts the finished job. [L1418.004]
- Fiverr lowers financial risk because you pay for a service and the freelancer receives the money only once you accept the completed job. This protects you if the work is not satisfactory. [L1433.005]
- Some agency-based VA services offer a money-back guarantee if the delivered services are not satisfactory. Read the guarantee terms before committing to any agency. [L1433.008]
- Monazar VAs is a dedicated VA recruiting service with a money-back guarantee if the client is not satisfied. [L1418.008]
- Choose Independent as the freelancer type on Upwork, not Agency. An agency is a larger company that assigns workers to you. You want freelancers who look for work directly on Upwork. [L1430.013]
- Freelancer profiles on Upwork and Fiverr vary widely in quality. Check each candidate's portfolio of emails, flows or designs before hiring. [L0855.168]
- Do not hire the cheapest VA. It saves money on paper but usually fails to deliver the required quality, and poor quality work can seriously damage the business. [L0765.010]

#### Referrals and communities

- Ask current VAs to recommend candidates near them. Well-paid and well-treated VAs stay loyal, and the team turns into a community. Hiring a partner of a current VA worked well in this case. [L1437.027]
- One strong VA often recommends other reliable VAs from their own network. Experienced VAs have often worked with many businesses over time. [L0921.020]
- A VA referred by a trusted contact can often start without training from scratch. Ask your network whether a team or agency already has a VA you can hire. [L1127.033]
- Ask your business community or forums for recommendations. [L1122.018]
- To find a VA for store management and supplier outreach, search the community's Discord for terms such as VA and freelancer and look for recommendations from other members. [L0805.065]
- Recruit freelancers and VAs through business communities for recommendations, and check reviews on the platform for recurring themes that need discussion. [L1122.018]

#### Overseas apprentices

- Apprentices are usually sharp people in their twenties who want out of conventional office work and doubt that job security exists. Offers that combine growth, adventure and a life abroad attract them, so recruit through communities of lifestyle entrepreneurs. [L1359.041]
- Describe the living and working conditions of an overseas apprentice accurately before they accept, including basic housing. An expectation gap on arrival damages the fit between employer and apprentice, so the pitch must match reality. [L1359.043]

### Writing the job post

#### Principles

- A job post should be simple and direct. State only what is needed, with no fluff or hype. Clear posts make it easier for candidates to understand the role and reduce unsuitable applications. [L1055.003]
- Expectations, pay and weekly hours should be stated clearly, so good candidates are attracted and weak ones are filtered out. [L1122.018]
- Write a moderate job description and prefer candidates with less experience. You can then train them to your own processes instead of hiring someone with fixed habits. (older, 2021 source) [L1437.024]
- State in the post that an editor will help the writer improve. Good writers value feedback, and this attracts stronger applicants. Writers choose clients who signal they will develop their skills. [L0382.055]

#### Required contents (version A, Source B)

Include the following in every job posting: [L1055.004]
1. Job title or position name.
2. Position description.
3. Proposed pay rate, which can be a starting point rather than the final agreed rate.
4. Location preference, if one is required.
5. Hours per week and any specific working hours, such as business hours in your country.
6. Whether weekend work is required.
7. Required software or tools and the existing skills needed, for example Photoshop for image editing or existing Shopify knowledge.

#### Required contents (version B, Source A)

A detailed job post should cover: [L1000.026]
1. Responsibilities, such as phone answering, email, live chat, and whether the assistant works with other assistants.
2. Required skills, such as sales ability, product knowledge, and whether the owner will train a less experienced person at a lower rate.
3. Working hours and the time zone they must work in.
4. Pay rate.
5. Work environment, such as a quiet space without background noise, for phone roles.
6. Hardware requirements, such as a working computer.
7. Availability and any fixed commitments the candidate has.
8. A link to the store, asking whether the candidate can see themselves selling these products and talking about them.
9. An instruction to reply on the first line that they have read the full job posting, which filters out applicants who skim.

A detailed post on Online Jobs PH or Upwork can be used the same way. [L1000.027]

#### Upwork job post setup

Create the Upwork job posting in this order: [L1430.010]
1. Select the category Customer Service.
2. Select the subcategory Customer Service.
3. Enter a job title and describe the work, for example handling live chat and emails.
4. List the responsibilities the assistant will carry, such as email and live chat handling.
5. Select the skills for email and live chat.
6. Enter the required working hours and the time zone.
7. Choose the payment type: hourly when first testing someone, fixed price once a good candidate is found.
8. Choose Entry-level as the experience level.
9. Answer the additional questions on expected duration (for example more than six months) and hours per week.
10. Set the freelancer preferences and screening filters described in the following rules.
11. Add the candidate questions described in the screening procedure.
12. Publish the posting.

Upwork settings for an email, live chat and order processing role:
- Experience level: Entry-level is the correct choice for a VA who answers emails, handles live chat and processes orders, because this is an entry-level role. [L1430.011]
