- Give each freelancer written guidelines for every piece: what it should include. The guideline document works as an onboarding guide and SOP, telling the freelancer what to deliver. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0379.007]

**Briefing a research or content writer (older advice, 2021-22)**

- When briefing a freelance content writer for research, give them access to your attention matrix document. For an ongoing content writer, also give them access to your content planning document so they can see what you fill in there. Both are part of the instructions you provide. [L0379.021]
- Hand the finished customer profile to anyone who writes or markets for you, including in-house staff, new hires, outsourced copywriters, ad agencies, email agencies and customer service, so they learn about your customers quickly. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0907.003]

**Writer checklist, given to every new writer and editor**

1. Know the customer from the customer profile. [L0380.022]
2. Write as if speaking to the customer face to face, in a casual tone. [L0380.022]
3. Use short words, short sentences and short paragraphs. [L0380.022]
4. Use bullets and lists where they fit. [L0380.022]
5. Give concrete examples. [L0380.022]
6. Follow the rule for how often to use each keyword. [L0380.022]
7. Follow the rule for how often to use subheadings. [L0380.022]
8. Check the readability score before handing in the article. [L0380.022]

**Pricing for specific tasks**

- Custom images, such as blog hero images, YouTube thumbnails, in-post images and infographics, can be outsourced on Fiverr. Do not spend time creating or editing images yourself. A professional custom image costs around 10 USD. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0379.014]
- A custom-designed image such as a blog hero image or a YouTube thumbnail costs around 10 USD on Fiverr. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0379.015]
- When outsourcing video editing per project, state the video's running time (for example, a 30-minute video) and list exactly what the editor must do. The editor then quotes one price for that project, and you can negotiate it. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0379.022]
- A freelancer charged 5 USD per video to translate captions into French and add a French voiceover to short TikTok-style clips. [L1427.056]
- Hiring a freelancer on Upwork to build a simple calculator page was estimated at $50 to $100 and could be completed in one day. This was used as the cost benchmark for building a tool page for a keyword. [L1120.025]
- A product-description team of three freelancers working on a project can produce about 350 unique 200-word product descriptions per week, so a complete store can be ready in about one week. [L1122.007]
- Fiverr can be used to outsource images for blog posts and content pages, while Canva can be used to make basic graphics yourself. Both keep content production affordable. [L1120.026]
- Do not pay for social media management that offers only a few posts per month, such as 1,500 dollars for four posts. At that level it does not generate meaningful returns. In the example, the owner had already spent over 4,500 dollars. (Source from 2023.) [L1283.022]

**Writer rates: sources differ**

- Upwork writers at $50 to $100 per 1,000-word article were the benchmark for a managed article team. [L0382.007]
- A freelance writer at about $30 per article was too expensive to fill 60 to 80 empty collection pages. Hiring in-house or on a part-time contract was more practical for ongoing content. Estimate the total cost of the content backlog before choosing a freelance rate. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L1396.047]
- Use the Upwork rate for article production, and use a part-time or in-house contract when the backlog is large and ongoing. Pay more for keywords with higher competition. [L0382.007, L1396.047, L0382.010]

### Production Roles and Team Structure

**Roles when systemising content marketing**

- **Writer:** drafts articles from keywords. [L0380.017]
- **Email copywriter:** writes emails and is paid more than the article writer, because the email list is the most valuable audience. [L0380.017]
- **Editor and project manager:** reviews articles and emails, and coordinates everyone. This can be a native US English speaker. [L0380.017]
- **Designer or illustrator:** creates keyword images. [L0380.017]
- **Data entry:** uploads articles to the blog and enters emails into the email provider. This is a lower-level role, estimated at about three to four hours of freelancer time. [L0380.017]
- **Quality assurance:** the owner's final review. [L0380.017]

**Reference team size**

- A content team that produced about 7 articles per week consisted of 7 writers, 3 editors, 2 graphic designers and 1 data entry assistant. The data entry assistant prepares search result research for writers. [L0382.006]

**Team size by stage (older advice, 2021-22)**

- Once consistent content has produced results for about 12 months, you can grow in three ways: publish more than 2 pieces per month, increase promotion of existing pieces, or hire an additional person. Output and promotion should keep rising rather than being held flat once results are visible. [L0383.014]
- Once consistent content has produced results for about 12 months, raise output beyond 2 pieces per month or hire an additional person to keep growing. [L0383.008]
- Use a fixed two-month review to keep the content process running. Spend at most about 30 minutes per review adding research and ideas to the planner. [L0381.015]
- Spread one content writer across several stores by working one store at a time, for about one to two months per store, until its collection pages are complete. A backlog of 60 to 80 empty collection pages was expected to take about six to eight months at that pace. Estimate the backlog size before assigning the writer to a store. [L1396.069]

**Outsourcing the pipeline (older advice, 2021-22)**

- Outsourcing keyword research and the attention matrix to a virtual assistant is acceptable and cheap. The owner does not need to do all content tasks personally. [L0381.014]
- Outsourcing techniques from Amazon Kindle eBook publishing, where creators build systems around templates and questionnaires to manage third-party writers, can be adapted to dropship store content creation. This lets you scale without expanding the core team. [L0326.003]
- A VA can take a finished Word document or draft, insert images, videos and other assets, and publish it to the website. An assistant already handling Shopify product uploads can usually learn blog posting from short instructions. [L0379.012]
- Outsource one task at a time. Perform each task yourself until you understand it, write an SOP, then delegate. [L0945.005]

### Article Production Workflow and Trackers

**Production hub**

- Use one shared Google Doc per keyword as the production hub. It holds the keyword, the article section where the writer drafts and the editor edits, the image, the article URL, the email subject line and the email body. [L0380.023]

**Per-keyword article workflow**

1. Change to the next keyword on the list. [L0380.024]
2. Writer drafts the article and hands it to the editor. [L0380.024]
3. Editor edits the draft and returns it to the writer. [L0380.024]
4. Writer rewrites the article and hands it to the editor. [L0380.024]
5. Editor finalises the article and hands it to the email writer. [L0380.024]
6. Designer delivers the initial sketch of the keyword image. [L0380.024]
7. Owner gives feedback on the sketch. [L0380.024]
8. Designer delivers the final coloured image. [L0380.024]
9. Email writer adds the article link, then writes the subject line and the email body. [L0380.024]
10. Data entry uploads the article to the blog and loads the email into the email provider. [L0380.024]
11. Quality check: confirm every step is complete, then send. [L0380.024]

**Tracker setup**

- Track every keyword in a spreadsheet with one row per keyword and one column per task, with the team member's name in each column header. Mark each completed handoff with an X. [L0380.025]
- Use the grid to find bottlenecks, for example when ten finished drafts are waiting for the editor. [L0380.025]
- Track each article in a shared spreadsheet with one column per production step and a checkmark when each step is done. This shows who is responsible for each step and where work is piling up, such as when an editor has a backlog. [L0382.012]
- Outsourcing without a tracker hides where work is stuck, such as drafts waiting for editing or articles never uploaded to the blog. Without a per-keyword overview, you only notice the problem when the output is already missing. [L0380.026]

**Operating principle**

- Set up systems and check results regularly, then revise processes when results fall short. Process, people and product are the core elements of any business, and content is mostly a process and people challenge. [L0382.050]

### Video, Creative and File Management

**Creative strategy stages (Source E 2025-26)**

- Creative strategy runs through four stages: conception, planning, production and management. An ad has to pass through all four to be produced and tested properly. [L0508.016]
- **Conception:** develop the ad concept through research before anything is made. This is where the idea and style are chosen on the basis of evidence. [L0508.017]
- **Planning:** define how the ad will be put together and which assets must be produced. A plan is needed before any filming, creator briefing or design work starts. [L0508.018]
- **Production:** the actual creation of content, such as filming, obtaining material from creators or designing assets. A plan alone produces no ads. [L0508.019]
- **Management**, in order:
  1. Upload the ads and confirm they are live. [L0508.020]
  2. Check that the media buying is set up correctly. [L0508.020]
  3. Assess the performance metrics of each ad. [L0508.020]
  4. Use the learnings to feed back into the system. [L0508.020]
  5. Repeat the cycle, iterating on the ads that worked and stopping the ones that did not. [L0508.020]
- Most brands do only conception and production, while planning and management are ignored or done poorly. Their ideas often appear sporadically and are launched after scraping material together, which makes it impossible to test the angles methodically. [L0508.021]

**Closing the creative loop**

- Measure what is launched and how it performs, use the analysis to suggest the next creatives, then brief them and contact influencers when needed. Each step should feed the next. [L0885.023]
- Build new creatives by combining hooks and testimonials from a large research list. This lets you scale testing without filming each ad from scratch. Keep the research list growing so new combinations keep coming. [L0901.016]
- When one concept proves profitable, scale it by making new scripts around it and small hook or detail changes, and keep investing in that concept. Once a fresh creative set is tested weekly or bi-weekly, the winning concept becomes the one to push. [L0902.024]
- Creating resized static ad variants can be delegated to a VA once the process is documented. The resizing step is repetitive and does not need the owner. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L1169.026]
- Brainstorm sessions should run weekly or biweekly, so creatives can ideate, test new ideas, and identify emerging trends to stay ahead of the curve. [L0260.018]
- Once one editorial page converts, reuse its structure for other products by changing only the character, the problem, the expert and the product facts. Keep the AIDA order and the 70/30 split fixed so each new page follows a tested pattern. [L0578.039]
- Once a sales letter or funnel has proven a closing structure, reuse that structure for new offers. Only the opening and the body need fresh creative work. [L0378.096]
- Use the awareness pyramid as a recurring check across traffic-based work such as content marketing, pay-per-click advertising, retargeting and email marketing, not only for conversion rate optimisation. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0481.015]

**Planning before building**

- Before building any new email sequence, retargeting campaign or content-based ad, write the plan first, covering what the message should say and when it should be sent. Repeat this step each time a new email idea, new product or new content piece is considered. The plan makes the build faster and easier to hand to outsourced staff. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0493.023]
- The target is to raise email volume to two, three or four emails per week. A founder cannot produce that volume by hand, so modular building blocks are needed to make it operationally possible. [L0610.011]
- Email marketing example: an email marketer hired from onlinejobs.ph, trained on your products and Klaviyo, creates campaigns she never did before. She becomes an expert in your business through hands-on work. This on-the-job training is often more effective than hiring an "experienced" expert who will not adapt to your specific needs. [L0263.042]

**Video file management**

1. Create one main project folder with three subfolders: video, audio and assets. [L0344.039]
2. Store raw footage and product footage in the video folder, including stock footage. [L0344.039]
3. Store music in the audio folder, and split it into sound effects and songs. [L0344.039]
4. Store graphics and photos in the assets folder, in an imagery subfolder. [L0344.039]
5. Keep the final project files in a cloud folder such as Google Drive, and keep a local synced copy, rather than only in Downloads. [L0344.039]
- Moving or deleting source files after importing them breaks the links in the project, so footage can disappear when reopened. Store all media in a fixed location, such as a cloud folder synced locally, and never rely on the Downloads folder. [L0344.040]
- Keep raw editing project files backed up in the cloud, so a project can be recovered if the local computer or drive fails. Store source assets in organised folders alongside the project file. [L0343.049]

### Link Building Delegation

**What can be delegated**

- Backlink building work, such as skyscraper outreach, can be delegated to a VA or freelancer. Written SOPs make it possible to keep building backlinks to the site on an ongoing basis. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0943.006]
- Resource page link building and broken link building can also be handed to a VA, assistant or freelancer. These processes suit delegation once the niche opportunities have been identified. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0943.007]
- Link building tasks such as spreadsheet work, email sending and follow-ups are simple and do not need advanced English or specialist skills. They can be outsourced at low cost, unlike coding, development, content writing or creative work. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0945.011]
- The outreach work is repetitive and manual but needs little specialist skill, so a general VA can usually perform it. There is no need to pay a high-cost freelancer, and the tasks can be added to an existing VA's workload. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0565.010]
- Compared with classic one-by-one skyscraper link building, this system is highly automated and scalable. After setup, the ongoing time commitment stays small, and most of the work can be outsourced. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0565.009]
- Broken link checking can be outsourced to a VA at about 5 USD per hour. The assistant runs the checker on every content page of a niche site, lists the in-content broken links with the URL where each was found, and you review the list to decide which links to pursue and which of your pages to offer. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0332.021]
- Data pulling, merging and cleaning the likely-linker list are repetitive manual tasks that a VA can complete once the topics are chosen. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L1443.018]
- Sort the backlinks column or the referring domains column from highest to lowest. Both sort keys give a useful ordering for choosing which domains to process first. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L1443.019]
- The per-domain backlink exports can be merged into one master sheet by copy-and-paste or by an automated method. The automated method depends on whether you use Mac or Windows and which spreadsheet app you use, so follow instructions that match your setup. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L1443.024]

**Resource page outreach procedure (older advice, 2021-22)**

1. Give the virtual assistant your outreach templates and written instructions on the search and filling-in steps. [L1237.020]
2. The assistant searches for resource pages using the approved search terms and builds a list of candidate sites. [L1237.020]
3. Review the list, approve or reject entries, and double-check the quality of each site. [L1237.020]
4. Once the list is approved, the assistant sends the outreach emails and manages the follow-up emails. [L1237.020]
5. Repeat the search periodically to find new resource pages. [L1237.020]

**Ownership and priority**

- If the owner keeps doing repetitive link building tasks personally, they drop down the priority list and are rarely completed. Consistent outsourcing prevents this slide. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0945.003]
- Outsource link building in this order:
  1. Perform each link building task yourself a few times until you understand it. [L0945.005]
  2. Once the work is affordable, write or record an SOP for the task. [L0945.005]
  3. Delegate the task to a VA or freelancer using that SOP. [L0945.005]
- Existing VAs with spare capacity are a good first choice for link building tasks, and they can take on part of the work immediately. This avoids hiring for a new role before the workload justifies it. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0945.012]
- For a serious link building goal in a growing business, a dedicated link building team member, full time or at least part time, is worth hiring. Once content production runs steadily, more link opportunities appear, and a dedicated person speeds up progress. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0945.013]
- Extensive outreach also builds industry relationships that can lead to business partnerships, co-promotions, guest posting, featured mentions and promotions to their audience. Over a long-term business, these relationships can pay off well beyond the link itself. (Older advice, 2021-22.) [L0565.013]

### Social Media and Influencer Outreach

**Organic social posting**

- Organic social posting shows that the brand is active and real when customers search for it. It is not a direct sales channel. [L1094.108]
- Paid social for high-ticket products often has minimal return on ad spend and a long payback, so outsourcing it is not recommended. (Source from 2023.) [L1094.108]
- A social media VA received a brand kit from the owner and had access to the website images. She made slight changes to each image so every post looked unique, then published across all accounts from Hootsuite. [L0921.024]
- A VA wrote a blog post every two weeks, which was proofread before publishing. The posts were then shared on social media. [L0921.025]
- Delegate social media engagement:
  1. Hire a virtual assistant or use automation tools. [L0226.039]
  2. Have them engage on Pinterest (pinning, inviting followers, following accounts). [L0226.039]
  3. Manage Instagram, Facebook and Twitter interactions. [L0226.039]
  4. Post blog content links to social channels. [L0226.039]
  5. Monitor engagement metrics and adjust frequency based on results. [L0226.039]

**Posting cadence: sources differ**

- Expected Instagram cadence was two to three posts per day, with Instagram as the primary platform. Creator-made content can supply much of this volume. Use this cadence only when you have creator content to fill it. [L0790.084]
- In the first phase, post occasionally, such as when a new supplier approval comes in, rather than several times a week. SEO and blogs are a longer-term play than paid traffic. Use this cadence for a new store. [L0856.104]
- Recurring weekly content formats posted on fixed weekdays build loyalty, because followers learn when to come back. Choose a format simple enough to sustain for the long term, since consistency matters more than complexity. [L0576.020]
- Examples of recurring series: a Monday myth-busting post, a Wednesday behind-the-scenes post, and a Friday weekly recap. Adjust the day and format to the brand's rhythm. [L0576.021]

**Influencer and outreach**

- Use experienced VAs to research influencer lists, because the manual work is cheap compared with the value of the list. A list of a few hundred influencers typically pays back the cost of the work. [L0790.082]
- After a campaign, record which influencer collaboration formats and messages worked best with your audience. Reuse these proven approaches in future partnerships to improve results over time. [L0787.006]
- For a technical niche where you are not an expert, find people who already have authority in niche Facebook groups or Reddit communities, and offer them a paid freelance content gig. Influencers can also be asked to contribute to your content. [L1120.027]
- Outreach can be scaled by hiring a VA who sends messages from several social accounts, funded from about 500 dollars per month of cash flow. The seller used purchased older Instagram accounts for this. That carries account-ban and platform-policy risk, which should be checked before doing the same. [L0789.058]

**Outsourcing vs. owner-led**

- Outsource posting and engagement once the brand's tone is set. The owner should still check the brand kit and approve the first posts. [L0226.039, L0921.024]

#### Further details

- Aim for at least 50 entries in the attention matrix, with no upper limit. Keep adding entries, remove those that disappear, and review the whole list about once a year. [L0478.028]

- Building the attention matrix is a simple, repeatable research task that a virtual assistant can do from written instructions. It is a good first task for a new team member, and a founder can do it personally before hiring. [L0478.029]

- Build the attention matrix in short, regular daily time blocks instead of one long session, and keep working on it until you have a solid number of entries. A virtual assistant can take on this task from written instructions, which makes it a good first onboarding task for a new team member. [L0478.034]

- Backlinks for product pages are not required to rank; start with product page SEO. Competitive niche keywords need more backlinks and time, while long-tail product pages can rank without them. [L0856.103]

## Choosing and managing agencies and ad outsourcing

### Default position: keep paid ads in-house first

- Keep hands-on control of ad setup and budgets in-house. Outsourcing ad work makes sense only once you are truly proficient in the ad platforms, so you know what to hire for and what results are possible. [L0359.099]
- Do not hire agencies for SEO, paid media or content. Agencies optimize for client retention and their own metrics, not for your niche and growth. Instead, build internal teams, or test carefully with your own money after you have proven you can execute. [L0263.007]
- Example: $3,000/month × 6 months = $18,000 spent on an SEO agency with zero results. The agency did not dig into the niche or build a real strategy; it optimized for client retention and billing, not for growth. Lesson: do not outsource core business functions to agencies before you can do them yourself and measure the results. [L0263.007, L0263.043]
- Do not outsource ad management or store building before you have a working system. Outside help can maintain and grow a working store, but it cannot build one from zero. [L0468.173]
- Do not outsource ad management until you have set up and proven profitable campaigns yourself. An agency that cannot deliver conversions at the start is a bad sign. [L0664.064]
- Run paid ads yourself and reach profitability for one to three months before outsourcing marketing. This gives you a baseline to judge an agency. Going to a marketing firm with a new store and no results usually fails. [L0467.070]
- Do not increase ad spend because Pinterest or Google account representatives push for bigger budgets. Their pressure does not lead to more sales. [L0671.098]
- Keep the management of Google and Facebook campaigns in-house rather than outsourcing it to a third-party app or agency. This was the presenter's approach at a spend of hundreds of thousands of dollars per month. *(Older advice, 2019.)* [L0663.012]
- Keep pay-per-click management in-house until you understand it well enough to judge an agency. The owner spent about 20,000 USD on outside pay-per-click agencies without getting better results than doing it himself. [L1122.008]
- Keeping ads in-house can work when the know-how is internal to the business. [L1508.020]
- Ignore the advice that a new store should never outsource: the sources below set the bar by results, not by store age. [L0801.152] *(Note: this line is a caution, not a rule of the sources; see the conditions in the sections that follow.)*

### Prerequisites: the baseline before you outsource

- Outsource paid ads only after the store has a baseline: it has impressions, clicks, abandoned carts and sales, and it is profitable. Without that baseline, a third party has nothing to beat and performance cannot be judged. [L1283.021]
- Outsourcing Google Ads only makes sense when the account is already profitable and the owner does not want to run it. The provider must commit to beating current results within a stated timeframe. Without that baseline, a new agency's underperformance cannot be judged. [L1116.002]
- Keep Google Ads in-house until the ad account is established and returns at least 10x ROAS. Only then consider an outside agency, because a new agency does not yet know the business or the traffic system. *(Source: Source A 2025.)* [L1116.001]
- Do not outsource Google Ads management until your store is already profitable and you have proven what works. Hire an ads manager for maintenance or scaling only after you have optimized the account yourself and validated the strategy. *(Older advice, 2021.)* [L0270.060]
- Do not outsource paid advertising until your store is profitable and you know what works. First establish baseline performance, prove that the store converts, and build initial momentum yourself. *(Older advice, 2022.)* [L0272.048]
- Outsourcing marketing should wait until the store has been run profitably in-house and results are clear. After that, an agency can be tested on whether it beats those results. [L0360.078]
- Prove that paid campaigns are viable before paying someone else to run them. Then outsource, so the owner can focus on other channels. [L0463.009]
- Outsource Google Ads or social media management only after those campaigns are already profitable, because an agency cannot fix unprofitable campaigns. *(Older advice, 2019.)* [L1110.046]
- Consider a Google Ads agency only after your store is already profitable and you want to accelerate what works. A store that is not yet profitable should be fixed on its own first. *(Older advice, 2019.)* [L1275.053]
- In the early stage, avoid outsourcing your advertising. Consider outsourcing only after you understand what your campaigns should achieve. [L0965.091]
- Hire marketing agencies with caution in the early phase. Without enough historic data, an agency can spend heavily without results, so monitor their spend and results closely. *(Older advice, 2021.)* [L0801.152]
- Working with a mentor or a team on Google Ads, Shopping and budget decisions is recommended as a scaling step, not an early test, once the product is proven and branded. [L0577.066]
- When you add agencies and staff, fixed monthly costs rise, and profit can shrink before results improve. Judge the return on the agency only after its work has had time to improve results. [L0694.034]
- Delegate ad management only once you know what good results look like and growth leaves you without time for it. [L1508.020]

### When outsourcing makes sense

- The main real advantage of an agency is saved time. It makes sense when you cannot find enough hours to manage your ads to the level you want. *(Older advice, 2021-22.)* [L1053.003]
- Outsourcing ads management makes the most sense for tasks you do not enjoy, that you find tedious, and that take time away from other activities that grow the business. *(Older advice, 2021-22.)* [L1053.016]
- Once a brand is established and paid ads take a lot of time, a specialist media-buying agency that manages Google and Facebook campaigns can take over that work. Owners of growing businesses often use this when running several projects at once. [L1251.049]
- When the founder is too busy with paid campaigns to work on other channels such as SEO, outsourcing full PPC management frees time for the rest of the marketing. The stated aim is to scale campaigns so the business can move from five-figure to six- and seven-figure monthly revenue. [L1262.007]
- A one-person business spreads its time across tasks and is more likely to make errors. An agency with specialists can run campaigns such as smart campaigns, Performance Max and retargeting separately. Experienced agencies also know account details that take years to learn. [L1214.055]
- At $500k-1M+ per month, an in-house media buying team should work on at most one or two platforms, such as Facebook and TikTok. Less common platforms such as Pinterest, Snapchat and sometimes Google should go to agencies. [L1129.017]
- A consultant for a focused review can be worthwhile at small budgets, where outsourcing the whole campaign management may not pay off. The consultant finds wasted spend and redirects it to better-performing areas. [L0463.014]
- A consultant who provides specific to-do items, setup feedback and campaign Q&A can save thousands of dollars in months of trial and error. The consultant can bring a campaign from a six-month timeline to a two- or three-month timeline. [L0463.015]
- Outsourcing makes sense only after you understand what your campaigns should achieve. [L0965.091]

### When to keep it in-house

- A freelancer or virtual assistant trained by you usually costs less than an agency, and gives you a person who works full time under your direct control. *(Older advice, 2021-22.)* [L1053.004]
- An agency does not automatically outperform the owner at running ads. If you apply a proven high-ticket Google Ads approach yourself, you can get results as good as an agency would deliver. *(Older advice, 2021-22.)* [L1053.002]
- Keep hands-on control of ad setup and budgets in-house. [L0359.099]
- Keep pay-per-click management in-house until you understand it well enough to judge an agency. [L1122.008]

### Scale-based split between in-house and agency

Two sources give different splits for large spend. Both are shown; apply each as stated.

| Source | Spend level | Split |
|---|---|---|
| Source C (undated) | $500k-1M+ per month | In-house media buying on at most one or two platforms, such as Facebook and TikTok; Pinterest, Snapchat and sometimes Google go to agencies. [L1129.017] |
| Source A 2019 (older) | Hundreds of thousands of dollars per month | Google and Facebook managed in-house, not by a third-party app or agency. [L0663.012] |

Note: the sources do not define a threshold below $500k per month. The rule in L1129.017 applies to that band; the 2019 approach in L0663.012 is the older statement for a comparable spend level.

### Service types and levels of involvement

| Service | Purpose |
|---|---|
| Coaching or consulting | Strategic advice and guidance while the owner keeps running the account |
| Campaign tune-up | Fixing and optimizing an existing campaign setup |
| Campaign audit | Reviewing the account to find problems and gaps |
| Store-specific feedback | Individual review of one e-commerce store's campaigns |
| Full PPC management | Outsourcing all AdWords and paid ad management to the agency |

[L1262.006]

- Before outsourcing paid ads, choose the service level that matches the stage. Start with consulting or an audit for a new or small account, and move to full management when scaling. Lighter options also let the owner learn how the account works before handing over control. [L1262.008]
- PPC agencies may offer several levels of involvement: answering questions about a course or community, private consulting on specific campaigns, or the agency running and scaling the campaigns in collaboration with the owner. Choose the level based on how much control the owner wants to keep and how much time they can spend on the account. [L1262.010]

### Agency vetting

#### Core competencies and specialisation

- Define core competencies upfront. Ask what the agency specializes in, which systems it uses for all clients versus custom setups, and which parts of the growth funnel it covers. Beware agencies claiming expertise in everything. [L0217.021]
- Choose a PPC specialist who focuses only on paid search, rather than a generalist agency that also offers design, CRO, SEO, content, logos or social media. Google Ads changes frequently, and a team that covers all of digital marketing is less likely to keep up. [L1448.015]
- When screening a PPC specialist, ask these questions in order: [L1448.016]
  1. How many hours per week do you actually work in client accounts? Compare the answer with the survey buckets: 40 hours, 10 to 39 hours, 1 to 10 hours, or less than one hour per week.
  2. Do you answer emails promptly? Check this during the screening itself.
  3. Will you schedule a call with me at least once a month?
  4. Can you share your current task list, showing which experiments, ad copy tests and bid optimizations are running?
  5. Do you focus only on paid search, or also on design, CRO, SEO, content, logos or social media?

#### Team and account manager

- The most visible person in agency sales presentations may not manage your account. Clarify who your actual point of contact will be, their experience level, and how long they have worked for the agency. [L0217.032]
- Verify whether the team assigned to your account is in-house, part-time, or outsourced to contractors. Ask how long each person has worked for the agency and whether they focus only on your account or manage multiple accounts. Big-name salespeople often do not manage accounts directly. [L0217.045]
- Read Glassdoor employee reviews for the agency to understand team dynamics and leadership behavior. Filter for patterns: whether juniors run client accounts, whether senior staff train or hand off work, and whether staff turnover is high. Employee frustration hints at problems you may inherit. [L0217.048]
- A large agency typically gives only about 5 hours of actual management time per month to one account. Any agency promising a dedicated staff member working on your account constantly is not telling the truth. *(Older advice, 2021-22.)* [L1053.005]
- Agencies that use heavy software to manage many accounts at scale usually deliver lower quality than one person manually managing your account. Take this into account when comparing them. *(Older advice, 2021-22.)* [L1053.006]

#### Position in the agency's client base

- Verify your position among the agency's client base. If you are their largest client, they pay you high attention but may lose focus if they land a bigger client. If you are small, they may deprioritize your account. [L0217.033]

#### Evaluating the agency

- Evaluate agencies on three dimensions: output (number of deliverables), integrity (brand alignment and messaging quality), and effectiveness (performance metrics in your ad account). Weight each appropriately when structuring compensation. [L0217.029]
- Money spent by an agency is not an indicator of quality work; it only shows the volume of clients served. Audit other metrics, speak to current clients, read employee reviews, and test results before hiring. [L0217.034]
- Request conversations with 2 to 3 current agency clients who have a similar business model and spend level. Case studies are handpicked and controlled. Unfiltered feedback from active clients reveals actual performance, speed of implementation, and communication quality. [L0217.047]
- Work through this procedure before hiring an agency: [L1053.007]
  1. Build a short list of agencies.
  2. Talk to each agency and ask about its experience with e-commerce businesses and who its clients are.
  3. Ask whether it has experience with dropshipping businesses and with Google Shopping ads.
  4. Ask the agency to explain how it manages campaigns.
  5. Compare its suggested approach with a proven high-ticket Google Ads approach. If it matches, the agency is reasonably likely to manage your account at an acceptable level.
  6. Ask the agency to review your ad account and suggest what it would change or improve.
- The most reliable way to find a good agency is a recommendation from a trusted e-commerce operator in your network. Someone who had a bad experience will not recommend that agency. *(Older advice, 2021-22.)* [L1053.008]
- To find a good agency or consultant, ask the platform rep for their biggest agency spender, then talk to that client about how they work. Consultancy and teaching services are often offered at a high fee. [L1214.056]
- Show the live ad account when talking to an agency, and ask them to beat the current ROAS. [L1116.003]
- Talk to several agencies before choosing one, because results and service quality vary between them. Compare what each one manages and what is included in the fee before you sign. [L1127.038]
- Check whether the agency is reachable through a support route. An agency account gives direct support contacts who reply through WhatsApp or Telegram, so response speed is a practical factor when choosing an agency. A personal ad account has no such support route, which makes its loss harder to recover from. [L0351.056]

#### Named references (older)

- Search Scientist (searchscientist.com) is a paid-search agency that works best with stores that are already profitable, because it builds on existing sales momentum. Contact it only after the store makes money, since an unprofitable store may be turned away or redirected. *(Older advice, 2019.)* [L0467.076]
- Search Scientists (searchscientists.com) is recommended for Google Ads management. The same source gives the condition that campaigns must already be profitable first. *(Older advice, 2019; note that L0467.076 spells the domain without the "s".)* [L1110.046]

#### Screening a PPC specialist: activity and reporting

- Activity is the strongest predictor of success when hiring a PPC manager. Assess how often they actually work in the account, how they communicate, and how strong their technical expertise is. [L1448.009]
- In a WordStream study, about 90% of PPC account managers said they spend at least one hour per week on an account. In practice, most do not optimize regularly as they claim. [L1448.010]
- In the same WordStream study, about 20% of account managers (1 in 5) made no changes to campaigns for an entire month. Only about 10% (1 in 10) worked on accounts every week across a full quarter. [L1448.011]
- Agencies performed slightly better than individual freelancers in the same study. More time spent in an account generally correlates with better results, because ongoing optimization compounds over time. [L1448.012]
- Check the specialist's activity with this procedure: [L1448.014]
  1. Check that the specialist answers emails promptly.
  2. Check that they invite you to a call at least once a month.
  3. Check that they share task lists showing current work, such as campaign experiments, ad copy tests and bid optimization.
  4. Check that they report openly on what they changed and why.
  5. Verify their reports instead of trusting them blindly.

### Red flags

- Do not work with an agency that contacts you unsolicited, for example one that targets everyone who opens a new Shopify store. Such agencies usually rely on churning through clients quickly, which signals weak results. Strong agencies often have a waiting list. *(Older advice, 2021-22.)* [L1053.009]
- Ignore unsolicited emails that ask you to book a call with an agency. *(Older advice, 2021-22.)* [L1053.010]
- Ignore cold emails from ad-management agencies. Many find Shopify stores with lookup tools such as builtwith, mass-email them, and churn clients instead of retaining them. [L1116.005]
- Some ad agencies find Shopify stores created in the last seven days and cold-email the owners offering ad management. They often sign up many clients without improving results. *(Older advice, 2022.)* Before engaging such a company, search its name in an owner community group to read the experiences of other store owners. [L1281.128]
- Avoid agencies that sell Google Ads management via cold calls and run templated work across hundreds of accounts. A boutique agency that understands your business and manages your account is the better choice. [L1285.059]
- Avoid agencies that cold-contact every new Shopify store. Their model is usually high-volume and short-term. [L0965.091]
- An agency charging around 200 USD per month will probably do little work on your account, or it will outsource the labour to a low-cost country where people lack significant experience in good ads management. *(Older advice, 2021-22.)* [L1053.012]
- A company that cannot deliver conversions at the start is a bad sign. [L0664.064]
- Larger marketing agencies often push a 3-month commitment and assign senior media buyers who may not understand the brand. Check the contract terms and the team that will actually work on the account before signing. [L1129.018]
- Avoid agencies that require a fixed contract, such as six months of payments regardless of results. Reputable agencies work month to month on a pay-as-you-go basis because they are confident they will deliver. *(Older advice, 2021-22.)* [L1053.014]

### Fees and compensation

Fee figures differ by model and by year. Both values are kept.

| Fee model | Figure | Source | Applies to |
|---|---|---|---|
| Percentage of ad spend | 15% to 25% of total ad spend in a healthy arrangement | Source A (undated) [L0463.012] | Ongoing paid traffic management |
| Percentage example | $500/month spend at 20% fee ≈ $100/month to the manager | Source A (undated) [L0463.013] | Calculating whether the fee buys enough optimization time |
| Fixed monthly fee | $3,000/month for a Facebook and Google ads agency, including about 20 minutes of daily account checks, product and store research and creative scripts | Source C (undated) [L1127.031] | Full Facebook and Google management |
| Minimum for a good agency | At least 700 USD/month, often considerably more | Source B 2021-22, older [L1053.011] | Agency quality threshold |
| Red-flag fee | Around 200 USD/month | Source B 2021-22, older [L1053.012] | Warning sign of little work or outsourced labour |

- The sources do not say which fee model fits which budget. The only budget-linked figure is the $500/month example, which shows how little a 20% fee buys at small spend. [L0463.013]
- Use a return test before paying any fee. Extra spend of about $1,000 per month and a ROAS improvement of about 3 points is a reasonable benchmark for a good paid traffic specialist. If that combination cannot roughly double your profit after the fee, the hire is likely not worth it. [L1448.005]
- Weight the compensation according to the three dimensions: output, integrity and effectiveness. [L0217.029]
- Before firing a specialist, compare the fee with the results over the same period. Outsourcing raises your break-even point, so the agency or new hire must beat your own prior results from the same period. Compare the agency period with the same months of the previous year. [L1508.021]
- Consultancy and teaching services are often offered at a high fee. [L1214.056]

### Contracts and terms

- Use short-term contracts and require agencies to earn a contract extension by demonstrating results. This maintains accountability and gives both parties clean exit points. [L0217.030]
- Avoid fixed contracts that lock you in regardless of results. Reputable agencies work month to month. *(Older advice, 2021-22.)* [L1053.014]
- Watch for 3-month commitments and check who will work on the account before signing. [L1129.018]
- Put the communication protocol in writing before engagement begins. Include channel (Slack, email, phone), weekly meeting frequency, response time expectation (for example, 24 business hours) and decision-making cadence. Example: Monday standup, weekly Slack updates, monthly performance review. Avoid assumptions about availability. [L0217.049, L0217.022]
- For a PPC agency, use a benchmark of email replies within 2 to 3 hours during business hours. Put this expectation in writing before signing. [L1262.004]
- Communication channels must be established before engagement begins. Include response time expectations, meeting frequency, escalation procedures and backup contacts if the primary person is unavailable. [L0217.022]
- Define the escalation path and backup contacts in the contract: [L0217.050]
  1. Primary contact.
  2. Secondary contact (for example, team lead).
  3. Manager.
  
  Specify what happens when your primary contact is on vacation, sick or leaves the agency. This prevents service gaps and ensures someone owns your account at all times.
- Agree a realistic timeframe and a target uplift, such as 20 percent or 50 percent more ROAS, so results can be measured. [L1116.003]

### Onboarding roadmap

- Create a detailed 30-, 60- and 90-day roadmap with the agency before signing. Map specific deliverables to specific dates. Example: Day 1 (campaigns live), Week 2 (categorical search campaigns launched), Week 3 (YouTube testing begins). This prevents vague promises and gives clear weekly checkpoints for evaluation. [L0217.042]

### Performance review and benchmarks

#### Baseline targets

- A proven 13X return on ad spend can be used as an agency brief. A good brief keeps 13X while increasing spend from $1,000 to $2,000 per month, or raises return from 13X to 15X at the same spend. Without a baseline, an agency has no measurable target. [L0934.065]
- Keep Google Ads in-house until the account returns at least 10x ROAS. [L1116.001]
- Set a target uplift of 20 percent or 50 percent more ROAS, agreed with a realistic timeframe. [L1116.003]
- Compare an agency's period with the same months of the previous year. [L1508.021]

#### Reviewing an agency before firing it

- Review an agency or specialist before firing them: [L0963.144] *(Older advice, 2022.)*
  1. Ask what changes they made to scale, including budget increases and campaign type.
  2. If they switched to Smart Shopping, ask them to revert to manual campaigns.
  3. Decide afterward whether to keep or replace them.
- Do not fire a specialist on the first day of trouble. Previous manual work may have been what worked, so first restore the earlier setup. *(Older advice, 2022.)* [L0963.145]

#### Ongoing checks

- Judge the return on the agency only after its work has had time to improve results. [L0694.034]
- Monitor agency spend and results closely in the early phase, when there is little historic data. *(Older advice, 2021.)* [L0801.152]
- Verify the specialist's reports instead of trusting them blindly. [L1448.014]

### Testing agencies against in-house results

- When an agency, freelancer or new approach claims better results, test it in a few markets alongside the existing campaign and compare traced results. This settles the question without a commitment to the new party. [L1261.029]
- Test procedure: [L1261.030]
  1. Keep the current campaign running unchanged in most markets.
  2. Give the new agency or approach a few selected towns or segments.
  3. Track responses, cost per customer and sales in both groups using the same keying method.
  4. Compare the traced results and extend the winner only when its figures are clearly better.
- An outsourced agency or new hire must beat your own prior results from the same period, because outsourcing raises your break-even point. [L1508.021]
- Outsourcing Google Ads only makes sense with a stated timeframe and a commitment to beat current results. Without that baseline, a new agency's underperformance cannot be judged. [L1116.002]

## Access control and account security for helpers

### When to add a team member
- A new store starts with one user, the owner. Hire staff only once the business can afford to pay them and doing the work yourself is no longer the better option. [L1306.009]
- The risk of a virtual assistant (VA) stealing or copying is real, but it should not stop you from hiring. Reduce it by vetting thoroughly, starting small and building trust gradually, not by avoiding delegation altogether. [L245.055]

### Vetting and phasing in a new VA
1. Learn about the person through interviews and reference checks. [L245.054]
2. Start with a small, low-risk project to assess reliability and character. [L245.054]
3. Gradually increase access to different business systems and information over time. [L245.054]
4. Do not hand over all sensitive information immediately to someone you do not know. [L245.054]

**First access for a new VA:**
- Begin with limited access: sales, orders and the support mailbox only. This tests how the working relationship goes. [L1173.048]
- Extend access to other tools only after the assistant has delivered reliable work over a set period. The sources do not state how long that period is. [L1173.048]

### Account types and permission levels

#### Shopify staff accounts
- Under Settings > Users, staff accounts give team members such as marketers or designers access to the store. [L1306.008]
- Shopify Staff Accounts lets the owner invite team members with their own logins and permissions. Use it to give helpers access without sharing the main owner account. [L1095.010]
- A staff account added through the invite form has full permissions, so it can change the whole store. Only add people you trust with full access. [L1300.034]

**Adding a staff account (2021-22 plan, older):**
1. Open Settings and go to Plan and permissions, which is the Account page in the Shopify admin. [L1300.032]
2. In the Staff section, click Add staff. Check the staff count, which on this plan is limited to 2 (shown as Staff (0 of 2)). [L1300.032]
3. Enter the First Name and Last Name of the staff member and the email address from their welcome email. [L1300.032]
4. This staff account will have full permissions. [L1300.032]
5. Click Send invite. [L1300.032]
6. The staff member accepts the invitation and works on the store through their own Shopify Partners account. [L1300.032]

*Conflict on the navigation path:* The 2021-22 guide uses Settings > Plan and permissions (older). The later guide uses Settings > Users. Use whichever path matches your admin screen. [L1300.032, L1306.008]

#### Shopify collaborator accounts
- Collaborator accounts let a developer, designer or marketer enter your Shopify admin without taking one of your staff account slots. [L1300.033]
- Collaborators do not count toward the staff limit. [L1327.013]
- Use collaborator accounts for freelancers who upload products. Shopify's collaborator code feature grants limited upload access only, while protecting payment methods, store settings and account security. Never share your Shopify login with freelancers uploading products. [L0128.006]

**Giving an outside party access (supplier, 3PL, agency or support staff):**
1. Go to Settings > Users and open the organization account security page. [L1327.013]
2. Click Generate new code. [L1327.013]
3. Share the code together with the store's domain or store name identifier with the person who needs access. [L1327.013]
4. The outside party sends a collaborator request using that code. [L1327.013]
5. Review the request in your Shopify admin and approve it. [L1327.013]
6. Assign the appropriate role after approval. [L1327.013]

*Conflict on request type:* The 2021-22 guide describes two options in the Collaborator accounts section: allow any person to submit a collaborator request, or limit requests to people who hold a collaborator request code (older). The current procedure uses the code. Use the code option. [L1300.033, L1327.013]

#### Permission levels
| Access type | Default or rule | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Staff account via invite form | Full permissions | [L1300.034, L1300.032] |
| Collaborator | Smallest role that fits the job, such as Customer support, Online store editor or Limited permissions | [L1327.014] |
| Collaborator | Administrator is not the default choice | [L1327.014] |
| Apps and third-party agents (e.g., a China 3PL, bundle or review apps) | Also work through the roles system | [L1327.014] |

- Give each outside party the smallest role that fits their job, such as Customer support, Online store editor or Limited permissions, instead of Administrator. [L1327.014]
- Apps that need access, for example bundles or review apps, and third-party agents such as a China 3PL also work through this roles system. [L1327.014]

#### Shared logins
- Do not share one login across several people working at the same time. A second login on the same account logs the first user out with a message that another user has signed in from a different device. [L0892.034]
- For tools with no financial or customer data, a shared login can be used, such as a shared Canva account with extra seats for multiple users. [L0360.082]
- Individual seats are the safer option when more people need access. [L0360.082]

#### Google Workspace seats
| Option | Cost | Own login and inbox | Notes | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Additional user | $7 per month each | Yes | Only for employees who must sign in themselves | [L1197.024] |
| Alias address (e.g., returns@, firstname@) on the same domain | No extra cost | No | Accessed through the primary account's login | [L1197.024] |
| Annual plan user changes | Users can be added at any time |, | Users can only be removed when the contract renews | [L1197.024] |

### Tool access for VAs: conflicting guidance
Sources differ on how far VA access should go. Each rule applies to a different situation:

| Situation | Guidance | Source | Date |
|---|---|---|---|
| VA needs the tools for daily work | Give access to all tools they need, including system logins. Accepting this risk is necessary for the work to function. | [L1122.023] | Undated |
| VA must place orders or pay suppliers | Give system logins plus the relevant bank account details and credit cards. Without these they cannot complete ordering or payments. Misuse is considered uncommon in practice. | [L1122.031] | Undated |
| VA places orders and decides on supplier purchases | Give access to supplier pricing and ordering so they can decide and place orders on your behalf. Trust and information access are needed for them to work well. | [L1109.065] | 2018 (older) |
| Shopify store, financial data and customer data | Use a staff or collaborator account with limited, view-only access to the sections they need. Do not give admin login. Restrict access to financial and customer data. | [L0360.081] | 2024 |
| Supplier orders | VA gets passwords, supplier price lists, contracts and credit card details needed to place supplier orders. | [L1473.025] | Undated |
| Owner's bank or personal financial accounts | Not given to the VA. | [L1473.025] | Undated |
| Profit margin data | Not given to the VA, since it is not needed for daily tasks. | [L1473.025] | Undated |

*How the sources fit together:* The undated broad-access rules and the 2024 restriction meet at Shopify and financial or customer data. The 2024 restriction applies there. The broad rules apply to the other tools the job needs. The 2018 rule is older, and it matches the undated ordering rules. [L1122.023, L1122.031, L0360.081, L1109.065]

### Discount and decision limits for VAs
- The VA may grant discounts of up to 10% without asking the owner, since all suppliers accept that margin. [L1473.024]
- Any larger discount or other decision needs the owner's approval. [L1473.024]

### Protecting finances and sensitive data
- Restrict access to financial and customer data for every helper. [L0360.081]
- Billing history, meaning past payments to Shopify, is shown under Settings > Billing. [L1306.008]
- If the P&L is accessed by multiple team members or is sensitive internal data, create a separate restricted-access sheet. Share only the read-only output with the team, and keep transaction-level data isolated. [L0218.025]

**Maximum security for sensitive financial data used with AI tools:**
1. Use an old cleared laptop with a fresh OS. [L0218.013]
2. Create a brand new Google Drive account on that device. [L0218.013]
3. Create a separate Google Drive for this project only. [L0218.013]
4. Grant sheet access only to this new email address. [L0218.013]
5. Store this laptop offline except during AI sessions. [L0218.013]

### Reviewing who has access
- In Business settings, the Users list shows every person with access to the business portfolio. [L0651.024]
- The Last active column shows who has been working in the portfolio. [L0651.024]
- The Invite people button adds new users, and the Export button downloads the list. [L0651.024]
- Use this list to review access before adding or removing people. [L0651.024]

### Storing login details
- Keep the login details for every tool the team uses, such as the phone system Mighty Call and Shopify, on one card in the corporate information board. [L1396.065]
- Staff can then find access details without asking the owner, and forgotten passwords stop blocking work. [L1396.065]
- Limit who can view that board, because it holds credentials. [L1396.065]

### Freelancers and outside parties
- Giving freelancers direct access to the store is dangerous, because a single wrong click can damage the entire store. [L1396.050]
- A Fiverr freelancer was found to have copied a logo instead of creating one. [L1396.050]
- Limit store access to trusted staff. Use freelancers only for isolated tasks that do not need store access. [L1396.050]

### Fixing access problems

#### When a team member makes themselves a domain owner (2022, older)
1. Remove the meta tag they used to claim ownership. [L1281.082]
2. Message them calmly and ask them to remove themselves as an owner as soon as possible. [L1281.082]
3. Grant only the access they need, and ask them to request any future access in advance. [L1281.082]

**Message template:**
```text
Hi [name], I just got an email from Google saying you were added as an owner of [store domain]. If you need extra access, let me know and I will grant what is needed. Please remove yourself as an owner of the domain as soon as possible. In future, if you need more access to any tool, ask me in advance.
```
[L1281.083]

#### Keeping the response calm
- Most access problems from team members come from carelessness rather than intent. [L1281.084]
- Keep the response calm and fix the access, so the working relationship stays intact. [L1281.084]

#### Further details

- 1. When hiring a virtual assistant, learn about the person through interviews and reference checks; 2. Start with a small, low-risk project to assess reliability and character; 3. Gradually increase access to different business systems and information over time; 4. Do not hand over all sensitive information immediately to someone you do not know. [L0245.054]

- Risk of VA theft/copying is real but should not prevent hiring; mitigate by vetting thoroughly, starting small, and building trust gradually rather than avoiding delegation entirely. [L0245.055]

## Testing cadence and scale-or-kill decision rules

### Launch speed and testing rhythm

- Speed beats perfection in early product testing. Taking too long to pick a product, build a store or start ads is a common cause of failure, so set a time limit and start running ads quickly. [L0577.060]
- Testing with more money does not automatically bring success. Wealthy students who test products too fast and spend heavily without a valid strategy become impatient and see no results, so patience and a sound strategy matter more than budget size. [L0577.061]
- The most common scaling blocker for beginners is launching too slowly, because sales and results only become visible after a product, ad and store are live. Shortening the time from sourcing to launch is the first priority. [L1454.001]
- An experienced operator can take a product from sourcing through store and ads to a live launch in about one hour. A beginner should aim for no more than one day for the full cycle. [L1454.002]
- A beginner should be able to launch a new product at least every three days, and the target cadence is about one new launch per day. If cash flow is limited, the target is adjusted to about two launches per day. [L1454.003]
- The product, the ad campaign and the store should all go live on the same day. Launching them separately delays the first data and slows down testing. [L1454.004]
- Run a fixed testing system with two to three new product tests per week. Keeping this cadence consistent is what produces winners over time. [L1454.016]
- Running the full cycle from sourcing a product to a live store and ads, even once, builds a habit that makes the next launches faster. Once the habit is in place, launching one product a day becomes a routine rather than a project. [L1454.024]
- Speed comes from routine and preparation: a store template, products sourced in advance, and a product page kept ready let you switch products immediately. [L1171.046]
- Running three to five product campaigns per week at this spend level, and cutting quickly when no winner appears, is a workable test rhythm. Testing more products faster increases the chance of finding a winner. [L1194.019]
- Testing several products at once means separate campaigns, each with ad groups that hold different videos. Costs add up quickly because each asset needs a minimum of about $20 in spend. [L1194.018]
- Testing several products at once only makes sense if you have the capacity to maintain quality control. Testing too many products too fast tends to lower the quality of ads and product checks unless you have a team with experience. [L1215.016]
- Post every finished landing page change in the shared team channel so the mentor and other members can review it. Copy a page template to other products only after it has been reviewed and the add-to-cart ratio has been checked. [L1194.059]
- Set decision rules in advance. Many beginners launch ads without knowing what to do after launch, such as when to quit and when to scale. [L0363.071]
- Build a testing routine, because tests give proof for decisions instead of opinions. With a limited budget, put more money into scenarios that already make sales instead of spreading it across untested ideas. [L0644.026]
- Keep testing when a product is new and when it is proven. Testing is part of scaling, and some ideas will fail, so quickly adjust the product or offer when a test does not work. [L1153.057]
- Do not reject a hypothesis unless someone writes a full thesis explaining why it should not be tested. Test everything, because unexpected wins happen and the reasoning is obvious only after the fact. [L0067.043]
- Top-performing brands test and rotate offer structures monthly, switching between formats such as "buy one get one free", "buy two get one free", "buy any two get the third 50% off" and "bundle and save", to identify which drives the highest ROAS and CVR. [L0232.028]
- Winning audiences and campaigns typically fatigue after two weeks to two months, even when they were consistently profitable, because the creative wears out. Keep a separate testing campaign that tests new creatives and hooks on a weekly or biweekly rhythm, so fresh creative is ready when a winner dies. [L0398.005]
- If your testing window is short, such as three months, spending most of it learning a new skill leaves little time for testing. Use ready-made creative for early tests and build skills alongside them. [L0341.025]

### Testing order and what to test

- Follow this testing order for each new store:
  1. Build a creative that gets a high CTR. [L0388.041]
  2. Build an offer that turns clicks into sales. [L0388.041]
  3. Find the interest or audience that buys with a high conversion rate. [L0388.041]
  4. Turn on ad spend and scale the winning audience by spending more on it. [L0388.041]
- Scale by spending more on the audience that converts, since more spend brings more visitors and early sales. Scale an audience only after its data shows it converts at a break-even or better cost per purchase. [L0388.042]
- Testing should cover audiences, creatives and marketing angles, not only audiences. Ongoing angle testing keeps a winning product from dying out. [L1213.031]
- Change one variable at a time: creative, audience and offer are separate and each should be judged on its own data. While testing offers, keep running ads unchanged unless they are clearly failing. [L1345.080]
- Store conversion and ad creative are separate problems and can be optimised in parallel. Fix the offer when the store has about 200 visitors and weak add-to-cart rates, and replace creatives when CTR and CPC are poor. [L1345.081]
- Funnel data and tracking must be set up and checked before any price or offer test is started, so test results can be trusted. [L1288.013]
- Marketing and testing checklist order after the store is built:
  1. Define the target audience. [L1288.011]
  2. Produce creatives. [L1288.011]
  3. Set up Facebook ads. [L1288.011]
  4. Set location targeting. [L1288.011]
  5. Set up TikTok ads. [L1288.011]
  6. Set up Google Ads. [L1288.011]
  7. Set up Pinterest ads. [L1288.011]
  8. Set up the funnel data. [L1288.011]
  9. Check tracking. [L1288.011]
  10. Run the price A/B test in UK, Australia and US. [L1288.011]
  11. Run the offer test. [L1288.011]
  12. Set up bundles and upsells. [L1288.011]
  13. Set up email marketing funnels. [L1288.011]
  14. Build customer audiences. [L1288.011]
  15. Build lookalike audiences. [L1288.011]
- Use a process of elimination for performance drops: change one variable at a time and record the outcome before the next change. [L0950.075]
- Optimise by data thresholds, not by days alone: check ad spend, CTR and CPC against averages, and check store visitors and conversion rates against benchmarks before deciding. [L1345.097]
- Test one product at a time before moving to the next. This focuses energy and allows detailed analysis. Once profitability is proven, duplicate the page and product structure for other items, but do not split attention across multiple products during the initial testing phase. [L0321.030]
- Focus on one dropshipping product at a time per store, so that traffic, ad spend and data all relate to that product. This makes it clearer where to scale and where to stop. [L1347.027]
- Test one product at a time instead of running several simultaneously, because overlapping tests make results hard to read. Finish one test with clear data before starting the next product. [L1172.036]
- Testing many products at once with little data gives weak results. Focusing on one product per week lets you learn faster and make clearer decisions. [L1176.042]
- Under one domain, test products in sequence:
  1. Launch one product on its own product page with ads on the same day. [L1412.050]
  2. Check its performance the next day. [L1412.050]
  3. If it underperforms, create a new product page for another product, which the lesson says takes about 2 hours, and launch it. [L1412.050]
  4. Repeat until a winner is found. [L1412.050]
- After finding a winning product, either keep testing under the same store and dedicate it to the winner later, or create a one-product store for the winner. Use the approach that keeps the pixel data and ad setup working for the proven product. [L1412.051]
- The choice between one shared store and separate stores matters little compared with the product itself. Optimise the funnel and the time per test rather than the store structure. [L1412.052]
- Split products across more campaigns does not speed up learning when the total budget stays the same. A new store should start with one Alpha and one Beta campaign containing all products. Create additional campaigns later to scale proven best sellers with the highest margins, using separate budgets to control spend. [L0837.052]
- Existing campaigns that work should stay untouched while a new attribution setting runs next to them in a separate campaign. Shift spend only after the new campaign performs at a similar level to the working one. [L1022.019]
- If a store is already profitable on its current campaign structure, do not switch it off just to adopt a new one. Change structure gradually when results are not where you want them. [L0854.107]
- Spend a large share of the available budget on testing new ideas only if the core campaigns are proven. For a $1,000 budget, about 10% can go to a test. [L0854.106]
- Improvements come from continuous testing of plans on top of a campaign that already works, without interrupting the proven plan. Over several years, a series of tested changes can cut cost of sale dramatically. [L1261.027]
- Testing can answer questions such as product form, pricing, a claim, or whether a rival or agency will perform better, for a small amount of money. Let consumer response decide, instead of internal debates or taste. [L1261.025]

### Testing budgets and break-even spend

- Minimum testing budget: spend at least your break-even cost per purchase (your profit margin). Example: if your product has $10 profit, spend at least $10 per ad set before concluding it does not work. Recommended: 2-3 rounds of testing iterations (for example, $50/day for 3 days, twice = $300 total). [L0067.041]
- Budget a testing phase of about 2,000-2,500 USD to test 8-10 products. After a winner is found, add about 500-2,000 USD per month to scale slowly. [L0363.068]
- Start new ad tests with a small budget (for example about 100 to 200 dollars per product) and move on if the product does not work. Scale after the product has proven itself. [L0577.062]
- Launch new paid campaigns at a low starting daily budget, around $10 to $20/day, and judge them against a clear ROAS target of more than 10x before scaling spend. [L1509.013]
- Start with a small budget, collect data, and then scale up or down based on results. Never begin with a very large budget before the data shows what works. [L0551.059] (older, 2020)
- Accept a deliberate budget for experimentation that burns cash on tests, and manage it at a monthly or product-group level rather than by day. This keeps daily swings from driving decisions. [L0719.041] (older, 2021-2022)
- Test a new product on Facebook with about $200 to $300 of spend across 10 to 15 audiences, usually $200 if signs are very poor. Review the data every five to six hours during the test. [L1213.037]
- On TikTok, test seven to ten creatives for a product before switching to the next product if it does not work. [L1213.037]
- A small test campaign of this kind often costs about $3,000 to $5,000, and much of its spend is recovered through sales even when the product proves unpopular. Such a test is a far smaller risk than launching a full campaign on opinion. [L1261.024]
- Consumer test campaigns of about $1,000 each helped one advertiser avoid a costly product change and find a new form that consumers preferred by a large margin. Testing before launch can prevent expensive mistakes and reveal winners. [L1261.026]
- Over five years, one advertiser tried more than fifty plans and eventually found one that cut the cost of selling by 75 percent, making it four times more effective than the previous best. Systematic testing turned small improvements into a large cost advantage. [L1261.028]
- Testing budget sources at a glance:

| Source | Budget | Condition | Unit |
|---|---|---|---|
| Source C | At least break-even cost per purchase per ad set; 2-3 rounds (e.g. $50/day × 3 days, twice = $300) | Before concluding a test failed | L0067.041 |
| Source C | $100-200 per product | Starting a new product test | L0577.062 |
| Source C | $200-300 across 10-15 Facebook audiences; $200 if signs very poor | New product test | L1213.037 |
| Source C | About $20 minimum per asset | Several products tested at once | L1194.018 |
| Source C | $2,000-2,500 for 8-10 products; then $500-2,000/month | Testing phase, then scaling a winner | L0363.068 |
| Source A | $10-20/day | Launching new paid campaigns | L1509.013 |
| Source A (2022, older) | 10% of a $1,000 budget | Testing new ideas on a proven store | L0854.106 |
| Source C | $3,000-5,000 | Small market test campaign | L1261.024 |
| Source C | About $1,000 each | Consumer test campaigns | L1261.026 |

### Minimum data before scaling

- Do not scale an ad until it has produced consistent results: at least about six sales at a break-even or full ROAS. Do not raise the budget, duplicate or change the ad set after one or two sales, because early results may be luck. [L1315.020]
- Before scaling aggressively, have at least three to four weeks of consistent sales, data and profitability. Scaling without this base risks running out of cash. [L1213.028]
- Check the store's profitability and ad data for the last four days, then the same data for the last seven days. If either period shows inconsistency, do not scale; keep testing and try new creatives. If both the last four days and the last seven days are consistently profitable, launch the new campaign or ad set at a much higher budget. [L1213.029]
- Scale a campaign when the test over 5 to 7 days shows all metrics within target: good CPM, cost per click and cost per purchase, ROAS well above break-even, and a healthy website funnel. Do not scale on incomplete data. [L1354.021]
- When analysing ad performance for decisions, take a window of the last 4 days rather than the last day only, or 7 days if not confident, to avoid reacting to daily noise. [L1335.038]
- Scale a campaign only after it has shown a CPA below break-even over several days, and let the trend confirm the result first. The example scaled a campaign that had turned profitable, with daily revenue around $1,000. [L1124.015]
- Only increase budget after profit and loss results are consistent over several days. If metrics swing, cut or pause the test rather than scaling. [L1175.034]
- Analyse the data together before scaling, check the break-even point, and start the scaling process only once you have a product that clearly wins. [L1176.041]
- After a product gets good ad metrics, follow this decision procedure:
  1. Confirm the Meta ads show a CTR above 2% and a CPC below $1. [L1172.035]
  2. If clicks do not turn into sales, improve the product page: test prices, bundles, store layout, copy and a different marketing angle. [L1172.035]
  3. Once sales start and the conversion rate is good, test price changes to raise margin while keeping the conversion rate above 1.5%. [L1172.035]
  4. Keep testing different interests until one interest produces more than six sales. [L1172.035]
  5. After reaching this threshold, contact support for help with scaling each week. [L1172.035]
- Once a winning offer and product are found, scaling is fast, often within days. Spend the effort on finding what works rather than on the scaling step. [L1469.022]
- Do not scale ads aggressively at first. Identify the audiences and creatives that work, combine them, and only then scale from those proven assets. [L1469.010]
- Scaling a product follows a sales ladder: first $1,000, $2,000 and $3,000 in sales to confirm potential, then $10,000 and $100,000. Invest more only once each level shows the product has potential. [L1171.045]
- Do not kill a product based on spend without sales before the landing page is checked, because the page, offer, creative or product can each be the cause. Fix the controllable parts first. [L1436.052]

### Profit targets and ROAS thresholds

- Start with a modest profit target, such as 10%, when a product is new, then raise it toward 20% once the product is proven. Each profit target produces its own desired ROAS, so the threshold for aggressive scaling moves with it. [L0445.026]
- Start with a profit target of about 10% when the first ads are tested. Once the store is consistently profitable, raise the target step by step, for example to 15% and later to 20%, and recalculate the desired ROAS each time. [L0875.031]
- Scale aggressively only when the measured ROAS exceeds the desired ROAS for the profit target. If the campaign is below that value, keep the spend at the same level and test new things instead of increasing budget. [L0875.010]
- Example: if the break-even ROAS is 1.6 and the goal is 20% profit, a ROAS of about 2.0 is needed. A campaign reaching ROAS above 2.0 may be scaled up, while a campaign that only earns around 5% profit should not be scaled aggressively. [L0875.011]
- Minimum ROAS before scaling is around 2.5 (one operator's threshold is 2.53). Set this threshold against your own break-even ROAS. Scaling has not been successful for the speaker unless the ad had at least the full ROAS. [L1315.021]
- Increase ad spend only while the return on ad spend stays at least 10x. If performance drops below that, first try on-site optimisation, and if that fails, keep the budget at the highest level where 10x still holds. [L1287.007]
- A standard rule for scaling ads: keep raising ad spend only while return on ad spend stays at or above 10x. Once it drops below that, optimise the store first and, if that fails, hold budget at the highest level where 10x still holds. [L1287.068]
- Products that justify large ad budgets have a strong 2X MAP to wholesale spread and good conversion. Once they are profitable, ad spend can be increased on them as far as profit allows. [L0966.088]
- Note on thresholds: the units give different ROAS levels: about 2.0 to 2.5 for a 20% profit target (Source C, [L0875.011, L1315.021]) and more than or at least 10x (Source A, [L1509.013, L1287.007, L1287.068]). The units do not state the margin behind the 10x figure, so apply each threshold only to the business its source describes and recalculate break-even ROAS from your own margin. [L0875.011, L1315.021, L1509.013, L1287.007, L1287.068]

### Scaling step sizes and budget increases

- Increase ad spend by no more than 2x per step and never above 3x, because larger jumps can break the campaign. Moving from 100 to 300 to 900 USD in consecutive days is a failing example. [L0363.066]
- Increase ad spend gradually rather than jumping several times over in a single day. For example, a $30 daily budget should not become $150 overnight. [L0934.084, L0934.116]
- Required spend to reach a target revenue = current daily cost × (target revenue / current revenue), where daily cost is ad spend plus fulfilment cost per day. Example: 100 USD ad spend plus 100 USD fulfilment gives 200 USD cost and 400 USD revenue, so 800 USD revenue requires 400 USD cost. [L0363.067]
- Scale ad spend gradually: start with a small budget, spend more the next month only if sales were profitable, and keep increasing while the ROAS minimum holds. Avoid suddenly quadrupling the budget after a few good months. [L0808.036]
- Scaling ad spend once a store is profitable:
  1. Increase the budget gradually, e.g. from $2,500 to $3,000 per month, then $3,500, then $5,000. [L0854.103] (older, 2022)
  2. Keep each new spend level only if the return stays profitable. [L0854.103] (older, 2022)
  3. Work toward larger budgets step by step rather than doubling at once. [L0854.103] (older, 2022)
