- A common growth barrier is reluctance to spend large amounts on ads. Profitable stores should keep raising spend in steps to match their growth. [L0854.104] (older, 2022)
- Growing to large monthly revenue usually requires substantial ad budgets, which feel frightening at first. Scale spending on campaigns that the data confirms works rather than spending blindly. [L0469.108] (older, 2021)
- The most sensible scaling approach is to start small, find what works, scale what works, cut what does not, and keep repeating this cycle. [L0970.152]
- Scaling traffic is not linear. A small lift in what you can pay per subscriber can unlock a much larger increase in volume, because competitors stop bidding at their limits while you can continue. [L0378.093]
- Example: a business getting 1,000 subscribers per day at $1 each could spend $1.25 per subscriber if they were worth that much by day 14. The 25% increase in spend might add far more than 25% in volume, and at a $2 spend limit volume could rise to about 5,000 subscribers per day. Revenue scaled from about $1,000 to $10,000 per day with only a doubling of value per subscriber. [L0378.094]
- The traffic cliff is the point where you can outspend competitors because your economics support higher acquisition costs. Reaching it requires knowing your customer value precisely. [L0378.095]
- A higher AOV lets you spend less on ads per customer while keeping the same revenue per order, which makes scaling more profitable. Post-purchase upsells raise AOV without buying more traffic. [L1141.025]
- A single visitor can buy three to six units in one session when the funnel stacks add-ons, which multiplies order value without extra traffic. Stacked bundle offers are the main way to reach this. [L1141.026]

### Learning phase: keep hands off

- After launching traffic campaigns, do not constantly adjust settings, do not panic or overreact to early performance data, and do not restart or duplicate campaigns, because this disrupts Google's optimisation. [L0168.006]
- After launch, monitor for three specific issues only: campaign disapprovals, account warnings and technical errors. Escalate these to support, but do not adjust other metrics during the learning phase. [L0168.007]
- When a store has a long ad-time budget, leave it running. Sales from paid traffic should appear within 1 to 3 days of the click, so a long wait without sales signals a problem to fix. [L0964.148]
- If an ad is making sales but is not yet profitable, let it run until it either becomes consistently profitable or falls below break-even ROAS, then switch it off. Do not switch it off while it still performs well, and do not change the budget during the learning phase. [L1315.023]
- Do not customise a product whose original profit fluctuates a lot. Keep the original running at the same budget and treat the early phase as a learning phase. [L1030.009]

### Optimisation rules for scaling campaigns

- Optimisation rules, applied to yesterday's ROAS and the last 4 days (use 7 days if not confident):
  1. If yesterday's ROAS is above benchmark ROAS, increase the budget by 20%. [L1335.036]
  2. If yesterday's ROAS is roughly equal to break-even ROAS, make no change and monitor closely. [L1335.036]
  3. If the ROAS over the last 3 days plus today equals break-even ROAS, keep the same budget; if it is slightly below break-even, reduce the budget by 20%. [L1335.036]
  4. If the ROAS over the last 3 days plus today is below break-even ROAS, turn the ad set off (kill the loser). [L1335.036]
  5. If a campaign is unprofitable, check both ad-set and ad level; if one ad takes most of the spend (for example 90%) without a sale, remove that ad. [L1335.036]
  6. Consistently underperforming ad sets or creatives should be removed. [L1335.036]
- Budget example: an ad set at $50 with a 4-day ROAS of 1.4 and break-even ROAS of 1.5 is slightly below break-even, so its budget is reduced by 20% to $40. [L1335.037]
- Test one ad group at a time: Scale only from proven assets, not from unproven combinations. [L1469.010]

### Kill criteria

- Product kill decision: if you have spent your break-even cost per purchase and metrics are not promising (poor attention, poor interest, poor conversion rate despite good attention), stop testing and move to the next product. Do not burn budget on products with no signal. [L0067.042]
- Kill the product if results remain poor after the spend and audience test on Facebook. [L1213.037]
- Cutting a product after roughly one month without clear data, because results seem insufficient, makes it hard to know whether the product was really working. Decide on a product using its data rather than short-term impressions. [L1347.036]
- Decide when to cut on day 2 or day 3 by judgement: when soft metrics are weak early in the test and the product cannot realistically become a brand, cut it. The choice depends on the product and the operator's conviction. [L1030.014]
- Cut a product that shows thin margins and repeated red days without a consistent signal. When margins are very lean, one more red day can be enough to stop it. [L1030.015]
- Keep a product only while it has a clear chance to become a brand. Holding a mediocre product ties up budget that could test another product, so move on when opportunity cost outweighs the upside. [L1030.013]
- A product built for a quick pump-and-dump with no brand potential should be dropped rather than customised. [L1030.016]
- Reallocate ad budget across a supplier list with uneven margins:
  1. Cut ad spend on products and suppliers where the margin is too thin or no sales happen. [L1069.073] (older, 2019)
  2. Move the budget to the products and suppliers where margins are healthy. [L1069.073] (older, 2019)
  3. Return to the low-margin suppliers and negotiate better pricing before adding more ad spend to them. [L1069.073] (older, 2019)
- Decide with the data for each product and do not kill a product on a single weak signal. [L1213.037]

### Product customisation gates

- Test a book product first with an unchanged or minimally changed cover and content, then look for proof of validation, meaning real customers buying the book, before investing in full branding. Only build the full custom version once that proof exists. [L0333.015]
- Customising a book's cover and contents before knowing that it sells wastes time and possibly money if the product does not work. Test the product first. [L0333.014]
- A "pump and dump" approach uses fast tests with minimal customisation to check many products quickly. It suits speed-focused testing but skips the brand-building that comes after validation. [L0333.016]
- Once a brand consistently makes over $1k per day in profit, the owner can consider custom ads and custom covers as the next stage. Before that, focus on validating the concept. [L0351.047]
- Once a product is profitable, refine it rather than changing products. Mentors suggested focusing on one profitable product before testing more products. [L0577.063]
- Proof-of-concept gate before custom production: the product must hold a profit margin of US$75-100 per day consistently for 3-5 days. [L1019.046]
- Do not build the full testing, scaling and graveyard structure before the proof-of-concept gate is passed; save the structure for later. [L1019.048]
- The minimum signal to consider customising is consistent profit of about $100 or more per day over three days. Below that, customising wastes time and money on video credits. [L1030.010]
- A product that makes about $200 profit per day on the same budget for three to four consecutive days is a candidate for customisation. In the example, customisation started on day 8, after the profit held on the following days. [L1030.011]
- Customisation workflow for a proven product, in order:
  1. Keep the original product running in its CBO campaign. [L1030.012]
  2. Around day 5 or 6 (or day 8 if the $200 pattern applies), duplicate the CBO campaign. [L1030.012]
  3. Upload custom UGC ads to the duplicate. [L1030.012]
  4. Create a second product on the same store landing page with a custom cover photo, a different title and its own link. [L1030.012]
  5. Duplicate the campaign again and point it at the custom-cover product, with its own custom UGC ad. [L1030.012]
  6. Run the custom version together with the original until the custom version shows consistent profit. [L1030.012]
  7. Turn off the original product's ads once the custom version is clearly profitable. [L1030.012]
- Customisation gates at a glance:

| Gate | Signal | Unit |
|---|---|---|
| Proof-of-concept before custom production | US$75-100 profit/day, consistent for 3-5 days | L1019.046 |
| Minimum signal to consider customising | About $100+ profit/day over 3 days | L1030.010 |
| Candidate for customisation | About $200 profit/day, same budget, 3-4 consecutive days | L1030.011 |
| Custom ads and covers as next stage | Consistently over $1k/day profit | L0351.047 |
| Do not customise | Original profit fluctuates a lot | L1030.009 |

### Diagnosing bottlenecks

- Identify the bottleneck by checking the funnel in order and reading the colour of each metric. If the Meta side is red, fix ads; if the website side is red while Meta is green, fix the website; if both are red, fix both; a healthy Meta side with a healthy website means scale. [L1354.026]
- Troubleshooting a store with lots of traffic and few conversions:
  1. Cut outsourced traffic if it produces no sales. [L0664.065] (older, 2020)
  2. Reduce Facebook and Google budgets to about $5 per day. [L0664.065] (older, 2020)
  3. Pause Bing ads until Google Shopping is profitable. [L0664.065] (older, 2020)
  4. Focus on making Google Shopping profitable. [L0664.065] (older, 2020)
  5. Check the store look, traffic source, product pages and checkout process. [L0664.065] (older, 2020)
- When CPA is higher than target while the campaign is still profitable, the funnel has room to improve. The levers are the ads, the average order value and the landing page, not only the budget. [L0875.012]
- Optimise in this order once products are live: images, discount display, ETA, reviews, and tabbed pages. Feature-to-benefit rewriting and extra images follow as soon as possible. [L1130.032]
- If revenue stays flat, first check how much of the existing audience is used. Look at email database revenue per contact and remarketing audiences before adding new traffic. [L0854.105] (older, 2022)
- Use a test-and-measure approach for performance drops: change one variable at a time and record the outcome before the next change. [L0950.075]

#### Further details

1. Pick four or five representative towns or market segments for the test.
2. Start users quickly with a sample offer or a free package that carries your full claims.
3. Track whether the people who get the sample buy, how often they reorder, and how much they spend.
4. Calculate how long it takes for the profit from those buyers to repay the cost of selling to them.
5. Only expand to national or wider campaigns after the figures confirm the cost, sale and payback are acceptable.
[L1261.023]

## Scaling Google and Bing ad budgets

### Core principles of scaling Google ad budgets

- Scaling runs on three sequential levers: adding negative keywords, adjusting cost-per-click bids, and increasing daily budgets. Optimize each in sequence to keep profitability and control performance. [L0193.004]
- The three levers are negative keywords, max CPC per product, and daily budgets. Repeat negative keyword and CPC work before each budget increase. [L0750.008]
- Start with a small budget so Google can learn and non-converting search terms are filtered out sooner. Scale spend only once campaigns are profitable enough to reinvest. [L0750.007]
- Scale a campaign only while it stays profitable with positive return on ad spend. Growth comes from adding profitable spend, not from spending as much as possible at once. [L1203.007, L1341.007]
- Grow budgets in steps. The priority is moving from a profitable $500 per month to $1,000, then $1,500, and so on, with the goal of being profitable at twice the current size. [L1341.007, L0463.006]
- Absolute profit rises even as ROAS falls toward 10X. Maintain a sustainable ROAS (around 15X) and grow revenue through larger budgets, not by preserving the highest percentage return. [L0276.021]
- Plan for ROAS to drop when a product scales. Keep enough margin that the product stays profitable at the lower return. [L1508.015]
- Do not launch all Google Ads channels at once. Build and make one channel profitable before starting the next. [L1121.005]
- Do not raise budgets because Google recommends it. Google will always suggest a higher budget, so decide changes only from your own ROAS data. [L0972.085]
- Before raising ad budget, fully optimize negative keywords and campaign segmentation, check impression share, and improve store conversion so more visitors buy. *(Older, 2021.)* [L0968.103]

### ROAS thresholds

| Threshold | Value | When it applies | IDs |
|---|---|---|---|
| Floor for raising budgets or bids | At least 10X ROAS, meaning at least $10 of revenue for every $1 of ad spend. Never raise budgets or bids below 10X. | Any increase, Google Shopping and general paid search | L0750.004, L0972.086, L0359.098, L0193.018 |
| Stop line per campaign | The campaign must stay above its own minimum ROAS. | Each campaign; the exact minimum is confirmed at the start of the plan | L1203.007, L1203.035 |
| Working target while scaling | About 15X | Sustainable level while growing revenue; 2024 source | L0276.020, L0276.021 |
| Later-stage example | 12X at $8,000 spend *(older, 2021)* | Month 6 and beyond, as an example only | L0270.062 |
| Early-stage example | 20X at $900 spend *(older, 2021)*; 20X at $1K spend (2024) | First months, when the account is being built | L0270.062, L0276.020 |

- The 10X floor is the stop line for any increase. The 15X figure is the level to aim for while scaling. Both apply at the same time. [L0750.004, L0276.020, L0276.021]
- ROAS is judged as consistently above 10X over two weeks to a month before any raise. [L0972.086]
- Scale a channel only as far as its ROAS stays at 10X or above. When that limit is reached, put remaining budget into the next channel. [L0972.089]
- A ROAS drop at a larger budget is normal. Example: 15X at $3K, initial drop to about 10X at $5K, then optimize back to 15X at $5K. [L0276.020]

### The first month: learning phase

- Expect about 30 days of consistent optimization before most successful operators report meaningful momentum in Google Shopping campaigns. [L0193.016]
- The first month is spent filtering bad traffic with negative keywords, calibrating CPC bids, and training Google to find the campaign's most profitable keywords. [L0193.017]
- Reaching a profitable $1,000 per month from a profitable $500 per month often takes real time, even on large campaigns. This should be the top priority. [L0463.004]
- Time needed to manage paid search rises with spend. A store at $1,000 per month needs far fewer management hours than one at $10,000 or $50,000 per month. [L0463.020]

### Negative keywords and CPC levers

- Add negative keywords from the search terms report in every review, so budget is not spent on traffic that does not convert. [L0193.018, L0750.008]
- Raise max CPC bids in small steps, such as five cents at a time, to find the ROAS sweet spot. [L1283.025]
- Large CPC jumps can cause spending at an excessive rate. If an ad is unprofitable and overspending, cuts can be made quickly. [L1283.025]
- Pausing underperforming products and reducing bids on weak performers moves spend toward the best-converting products. Successful stores know which products drive profit and bid aggressively on them. [L0299.020]
- Pausing non-performers frees budget and inventory slots to test new products, so the catalogue improves over time. [L0299.022]

### Review routine

- Set a recurring weekly or bi-weekly reminder for scaling optimization. Make it a fixed owner task in the calendar, not a one-time setup. [L0193.018, L0750.010]
- Each review, in this order: [L0193.018]
  1. Review search terms.
  2. Add negative keywords.
  3. Adjust CPC bids.
  4. Increase the daily budget if ROAS is at least 10X.
- Once familiar with the routine, a full review of product-level max CPC changes in a Shopping campaign takes about 15 minutes. The first one or two reviews take longer. [L0750.030]
- Track these numbers regularly: quality score, CTR, CPC, conversion rate, and ROAS. [L0510.061]

### Review frequency by conversion rate

Do not optimize more often than these click intervals. Too little data produces noise rather than signal. [L0299.025]

| Conversion rate | Review bids every | IDs |
|---|---|---|
| 2% | 50 clicks | L0299.025 |
| 1.2% | 83 clicks | L0299.025 |
| 1% | 100 clicks (think in 100-click blocks) | L0299.025 |

### Budget progressions

Sources give different ladders. Use the ladder that matches the account's stage and ROAS, and never skip a step while ROAS is weak.

- **Google Shopping ladder, 2024 and 2025 sources:**
  - Example from one source: $10, $15, $20, $30, $50 per day, potentially reaching $500 to $2,000 per day if profitability is maintained. [L0193.003]
  - Alternative: a new account starts with a shared daily budget of $10. Steps follow $10, $15, $20, $30, $40, $50, each only after the previous level has held the target return. [L0750.003]
  - Reaching $500 to $2,000 per day can take about a year of scaling, if the return holds. [L0750.006]
  - At a $2,000 daily budget with a 10X return, daily revenue must be at least $20,000. [L0750.006]
  - Stop increasing once the next level cannot be reached while staying at 10X. That level is the breaking point. [L0750.005]
- **Later Google Shopping steps (2025):** from $30 to $40 or $50. Never jump from $30 to $120, because large jumps make Google relearn the campaign and waste spend. [L0972.087]
- **Shopping ladder with pauses (2024):** $50 to $70 per day, wait about two weeks and check ROAS, then $90 or $100. [L1285.050]
- **Large-budget ladder (2023):** $100 to $120 per day, let it settle for a few days, then $140. [L1283.024]
- **Monthly search budget ladder (general):** start with a budget that delivers at least 10X, for example $1,000 per month. Raise in steps, such as $1,000 to $2,000, then $3,000 to $4,000. Keep ROAS at or above 10X at each step before going further. [L0359.098]
- Only make larger jumps once the business has a stronger bankroll and accepts more risk. [L0359.098]
- **Google Ads progression with ROAS, 2021 *(older)*:**

  | Month | Spend | ROAS | IDs |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | 1 | $900 | 20X | L0270.062 |
  | 2 | $1,500 | 15X | L0270.062 |
  | 3 | $1,500 (maintain) | 18X | L0270.062 |
  | 4 | $2,500 | 15X | L0270.062 |
  | 5 | $3,000 | 15X+ | L0270.062 |
  | 6+ | $8,000 | 12X | L0270.062 |

  Always increase incrementally. Never jump aggressively if ROAS is dropping. [L0270.062]

- **Google Ads progression with revenue, 2024:**

  | Spend | Target ROAS | Revenue | IDs |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | $1K | 20X | $20K | L0276.020 |
  | $2K | about 15X | $30K | L0276.020 |
  | $3K | about 15X | $45K | L0276.020 |
  | $5K | initial drop to about 10X, then optimize back to 15X | $50K | L0276.020 |
  | $7K | 15X | $105K | L0276.020 |

- **Monthly revenue benchmarks:** a viable store should generate about $20,000 to $30,000 per month from the three core Google and Bing campaign types alone. [L1203.009]
- A $6,000 monthly budget at 5X to 10X ROAS produces $30,000 to $60,000 in monthly revenue. At 7X, it produces over $40,000. [L1203.036]

### Raising a daily budget step by step

**Procedure A, 2024 source:**

1. Click Shopping campaigns at the top, then Campaigns to see the daily budgets. Example: both campaigns at $15 per day as a shared budget. [L0750.025]
2. Confirm that ROAS has held at 10X or higher for about two weeks, and that negative keywords and product CPCs were adjusted in that period. [L0750.025]
3. Click the edit icon next to the budget and change the value. Example: $15 to $20. [L0750.025]
4. Let the new budget run for one to two weeks. [L0750.025]
5. Then add negative keywords from the search terms, adjust CPCs based on results, and check ROAS again. [L0750.025]
6. If ROAS is still at least 10X, increase the budget again and repeat the loop. [L0750.025]

- Example: after two weeks at a 15X return, with negative keywords and CPCs already adjusted, a $15 daily budget can be raised to $20 per day. [L0750.026]

**Procedure B, 2025 source:**

1. Check ROAS over two weeks to a month. [L0972.088]
2. Confirm ROAS is consistently above 10X. [L0972.088]
3. Raise the daily budget modestly, such as from $30 to $40 or $50. [L0972.088]
4. Watch ROAS for the next period before raising again. [L0972.088]
5. If you raised the budget too far, lower it back right away. [L0972.088]

### Scaling a large catalogue on a limited budget

**Procedure (2023):** the two sources below describe the same method and are merged here. [L0934.085, L0934.115]

1. Use historical sales data, such as about three years, to identify the top 1 to 500 products out of 5,000. [L0934.085, L0934.115]
2. Place those products into a Google Shopping campaign. [L0934.085, L0934.115]
3. Optimize their product pages first. [L0934.085, L0934.115]
4. Keep the remaining products on free organic traffic only. [L0934.085, L0934.115]
5. When the winners take off, optimize the ads further, raise spend, and add more products gradually. [L0934.085, L0934.115]

### Product-level focus and Shopping actions

- Shopping action items: [L0510.061]
  1. Place customer search terms at the front of Shopping product titles.
  2. Run Facebook dynamic product ads for visitors who viewed products.
  3. Add an incentive opt-in on the site to capture emails and earn the email revenue lift.
  4. Review the numbers regularly: quality score, CTR, CPC, conversion rate, and ROAS.
- A strong brand can stay in one campaign while the other brands get dedicated budgets. Separating the strong brand's ads from the rest protects its performance. *(Older, 2021.)* [L0853.071]

### Ceiling of Google Shopping

- Profitable Google Shopping traffic is limited. The ceiling varies with niche, product, brand, and market. In the lesson's examples, it ranged from tens of thousands to hundreds of thousands of dollars in sales per month. *(Older, 2021-2022.)* [L0744.050]
- Narrow niches have smaller search pools and a lower Google Shopping ceiling. A seller of beginner-only products hits the limit much sooner than one covering all skill levels. *(Older, 2021-2022.)* [L0744.051]

### Adding other channels

**When to start a channel**

- Start Google ads only after the first two suppliers are approved and have their products uploaded. Keep recruiting suppliers and uploading products while the ads run. [L0972.084]
- Build and profit from one channel before starting the next. [L1121.005]
- Add other channels such as SEO, social, content, and email once paid search runs profitably, since they tend to perform better on top of that base. [L0463.021]
- A profitable Google Shopping campaign can be a retargeting channel. Run it for a while before judging, since it can pick up the slack in profit. [L0950.064]

**Bing**

- Early-phase split (2024): focus most of the budget on Google, for example $500 of $1,000. Move $100 to $150 into Bing and reserve about $100 for Meta, growing Bing later. [L0856.105]
- Earlier rule (2019 *(older)*): Bing Shopping ads should start only after Google Shopping product listing ads are profitable and tuned. Starting Bing early will rarely double income. [L1275.054]
- Apply both: the 2024 split works for a small early budget, while the 2019 rule governs when Bing shopping should start. Bing is never a substitute for a tuned Google Shopping campaign. [L0856.105, L1275.054]

**Six-month sequence (2024)**

1. Before October is out, confirm the store's numbers and the exact minimum ROAS. [L1203.035]
2. In months two and three, roll out product listing ads, search text ads on PLA terms, and search retargeting (the three-pack), plus retargeting for product listing ads. [L1203.035]
3. In month four, launch dynamic product ads on Facebook. [L1203.035]
4. In month five, start cold lookalike audiences that feed Facebook pre-sales engagement content, which takes time to build. [L1203.035]
5. Roll out each piece in sequence, not everything at once. [L1203.035]

### Management time and handover

- Plan the owner's or manager's time to grow with the budget. The workload is lightest during the initial setup and learning phase. [L0463.020]
- Grow a paid search channel in this order: [L0463.021]
  1. While spend is small, invest your own hours to set up campaigns, learn the mechanics, and understand the account.
  2. Prove that the campaigns are viable and profitable.
  3. Once viability is proven, hand day-to-day management to a professional, or bring in a consultant for targeted review.
  4. With paid search running profitably, add other channels such as SEO, social, content, and email.
- A campaign profitable at $500 per month can realistically be scaled to $3,000 per month profitably with ongoing work. [L0463.006]

## Scaling Meta ads

### Scaling directions and principles

- Horizontal scaling means finding more audiences: while a product stays profitable, launch 2 to 3 new interests every day, keep testing for as many audiences as possible that work, and reuse them with new creatives. Turn off underperforming ad sets. [L0375.013]
- Vertical scaling means raising the budget of existing campaigns or ad sets, or duplicating them. Use either method or both to scale aggressively. [L0375.018]
- Test whether raising budgets or duplicating works better in your own account, because the best method depends on the account. [L0375.015]
- Budget increase methods differ per ad account. Test doubling the budget, a 25 percent increase, or duplicating the ad set on one campaign first, and roll a method out to other campaigns only if it works there. [L0645.010]
- Every ad account behaves differently, so test everything instead of copying what worked for another account. Test a structure with a small budget before committing to it. [L0376.024]
- There is no single strategy that works for every product or ad account. Keep testing broad and interest approaches and scale whichever one proves profitable. [L1161.030]
- No single formula fits every account. Choose the structure based on what is profitable, and keep the cost per acquisition below your break-even. [L1195.048]
- Keep scaling campaigns running for the best audiences and creatives, using CBOs where they work. If CBOs do not work in the account, use ABO campaigns that only scale the best audiences and creatives. [L0375.016, L0376.023]
- Always keep scaling campaigns running. Use CBO for scaling the best audiences with the best creatives, and switch to an ABO campaign dedicated to scaling if CBO does not work for your account. [L0376.023]
- The testing campaign is a starting structure. Once purchases show which interests and creatives win, move to CBO or other campaign types to scale. [L0647.034]
- Scale a winning product only once the pixel shows real purchase data. Until then, keep testing. [L1195.046]

### Readiness to scale: proof thresholds

Sources disagree on the sales threshold. Use the values below according to the situation:

| Source value | Threshold | Context |
|---|---|---|
| Interest or ad set in ABO testing | At least 3 purchases with ROAS above break-even | Starting a CBO scaling campaign with a $100 budget [L0645.009] |
| Ad set with consistent sales | At least 4 purchases | Raising the budget by 20 percent every 24 hours [L0375.014, L0376.017] |
| Interest proven profitable | At least 6 profitable sales | Choosing a scaling method [L0639.014] |
| Audience or ad set | About 6 to 7 sales; 5 is too few | Most common rule, applied before scaling [L0646.001, L0692.019, L0773.056] |
| Winning interest | About 7 sales | Raising its budget or starting a new $50 CBO without interests [L0692.031] |

- Before scaling a Meta ad set, wait for roughly six to seven profitable sales in a consistent pattern. Four or five sales is too little data. A single unusually strong day can distort the picture, so look for steady sales across several days. [L0646.001]
- Consider scaling an audience only after it has about 6 to 7 sales, since five sales are too few to be confident. Early on, use a small budget rather than a high one. [L0692.019]
- Do not scale a Facebook ad set until it delivers more than six or seven sales. Before that point, keep collecting data at the current budget. [L0773.056]
- Once a winning interest reaches about seven sales, you can raise its budget, or start a new $50 CBO without interests to test further. Both options should follow the same ROAS and sales thresholds. [L0692.031]
- A campaign needs a minimum daily spend of about 10 or 50 pounds per day to be easily scalable. Below that level, scaling is considered difficult. [L0646.003]
- Once the pixel has 1,000 or more purchases, you can also target a broad audience with no interests. [L0376.025]
- Do not scale an audience with a ROAS below 1.7 when the break-even ROAS is 1.5. A higher ROAS gives more confidence, and 1.7 is the minimum for scaling. [L0692.020]
- Advertisers disagree on how far ROAS should sit above break-even before scaling. Some use break-even plus 0.3 to 0.5. A more conservative rule with a break-even ROAS of 1.5 requires a minimum of 1.7, about 0.2 above. Use the margin your own account supports. [L0692.048, L0692.020, L0375.022]
- Set your own benchmark ROAS and adjust the formula to it, because a 20 percent margin is not every seller's target. [L0375.022]
- Test any new scaling method on only one winning campaign first. Do not test on your sole profitable campaign. [L0298.014]
- At low budgets, around $10 per asset, daily sales are very unstable. Even ad sets at about $300 can swing from a 2 ROAS to under 1, though higher budgets tend to stabilize results somewhat. [L1107.009]
- Scaling is not a day-one activity: segment and scale by profit only after the account has data and standard optimizations are done. [L0224.002]

### Testing structure and creative flow

- Testing campaign structure:
  1. Interest-testing campaign: take winning creatives and test new interests.
  2. Creative-testing campaign: take the winning interest and test new creative variations.
  3. Once winners are identified, combine all winning creatives into one audience targeting for scaling. [L0067.040]
- Start each new product with $5 per ad set across five to six ad sets, about $25 to $30 per day. Once a test ad set shows a good CTR and consistent sales, choose how to scale: raise the budget on the winning ad sets, or add spend to test ad sets so the winning audience is found faster. [L1184.070]
- Testing procedure for a new audience:
  1. Test the audience for two to three days.
  2. Remove creatives that perform badly in a consistent way.
  3. Wait until the audience has 6 to 7 sales.
  4. Confirm a ROAS of at least 1.7 when break-even ROAS is 1.5.
  5. Start with a CBO budget of about $50.
  6. Once it is proven, scale using the three-campaign structure below. [L0692.027]
- A CBO campaign with a daily budget of about $50 is an acceptable starting budget for testing a scaling candidate. [L0692.021]
- Do not double a budget abruptly. A product that converted above 3 percent in testing dropped to zero add-to-carts the day after the budget was doubled. [L0692.026]
- Move the winning creative into its own campaign, then duplicate the same creative into multiple ad sets to scale it:
  1. Find the winning creative among the test ad sets.
  2. Move that creative into its own campaign.
  3. Duplicate the same creative into multiple ad sets to scale it. [L1194.017]
- Relying on a single ad to scale is risky, even if that ad has good metrics. Build more ad variants so the campaign does not depend on one creative. [L1107.005]

### Account layout

| Campaign | Purpose | Type | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| Audience testing campaign | Test all audiences, including LLAs | Testing | Every audience gets tried |
| Creative testing campaign | Test new creatives with your best audiences | Testing | New ads run against the best audiences |
| Scaling campaigns | Scale the best audiences with the best creatives | CBO, or ABO if CBO does not work for the account | Use CBO first; switch to ABO if it performs better in your account |
| Retargeting campaign | Retarget warm visitors | ABO or CBO | Run both on a small budget to confirm which works better |

[L0375.017, L0376.022]

- The more audiences an account has, the easier scaling becomes. Launch 3 to 5 or more new audiences (single interest or LLAs) every day or every week, and calculate the budget needed for these new audiences before scaling. [L0375.019, L0376.014]
- Once the account has several winning audiences, scale with 3 to 5 or more new audiences per day, using single interests or lookalike audiences (LLAs). [L0376.014]

Source D 2026 stage table, for a lead-to-scale loop:

| Stage | Campaign type | Bid strategy | Structure | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Testing | ABO | Cost cap (tROAS / CPA) | 1 campaign per product; 1 ad set per creative batch; 3-5 creatives per ad set; top-performing countries | Per creative: 3 primary text variations, 3 headline variations, 1 description. Cut losers early. |
| Scaling | CBO | Bid cap | Inflated budget; 1 campaign per region; monthly winner ad sets split by landing page | Winners graduate here once the pixel has real purchase data. |
| Graveyard | CBO | Bid cap | All markets | Ads with good signals but not yet scalable, plus past winners that have faded. |

[L1195.045]

- Graduate from the testing structure to the scaling structure only after the pixel holds real purchase data, roughly 500-1,000 website purchases. Until then, keep testing. [L1195.046]
- Revived creatives from the graveyard go back to ABO testing with cost cap, forming a repeating loop: testing, then scaling, then graveyard, then testing again. [L1195.047]
- A big-cap CBO can be limited to the best-performing countries, for example the top four countries plus New Zealand, while Singapore and all other markets are left out. Set the country list from results rather than keeping every market. [L1195.058]

### CBO and ABO

- ABO is used for testing interests and CBO for scaling in many accounts. CBO does not work for every account, and ABO can perform better, so the right choice depends on testing the setup. [L0349.020]
- CBO scaling setup: put a proven interest into a CBO campaign with a budget of 100 to 200 USD. Include 4 to 5 ad sets with the same audience, with about four creatives in each. [L0648.044]
- Conflict on creative count per CBO ad set. Use about four creatives per ad set in a proven CBO scaling campaign [L0648.044]. Use about three creatives per ad set when a campaign starts with 4 to 5 ad sets [L1107.015]. Use three creatives per ad set in the three-campaign structure [L0692.028]. Use two to three creatives rather than one when building the first scaling CBO [L0645.009].
- In a CBO, Meta assigns the budget to the ad sets that are reaching the best pockets of the audience, then searches for similar people to grow those pockets. This is why CBO works for scaling once there is a budget of about 100 to 200 USD. [L0648.045]
- A campaign-level budget (CBO) moves money toward the best-performing ad sets, but it can sometimes allocate spend to a weaker one based on early engagement. Review the allocation regularly and turn off the weak ad sets after enough data has accumulated. [L0639.015]
- A high-budget CBO does not spend evenly across creatives. On one day one creative gets most of the budget, and on another day a different one does. A scaling campaign therefore needs several working creatives, not one. [L1107.014]
- Before building a scaling CBO, keep ads that work in other test ad sets even if they fail in one ad set. Remove an ad from the CBO only if it performs badly everywhere, and keep looking for more good creatives. [L1107.013]
- A CBO scaling campaign starts with 4 to 5 ad sets. Each ad set contains about 3 creatives. [L1107.015]
- If CBO does not work in an account, scale with ABO instead. Duplicate the winning ad set three times at 100 USD each in one ABO campaign, or create three separate ABO scaling campaigns with 100 USD each. [L0648.046]
- CBO campaigns with several ads do not work for every account. In the case described, they gave zero sales and rising CPA compared with testing (testing CPA about $10 to $50, CBO CPA about $20). Switching off underperforming ads inside a CBO can disrupt the optimization, so ABO was preferred for scaling in this case. [L0995.026]
- Move a winning product from an ABO test into its own CBO:
  1. After the ABO test, shortlist the products whose ad sets show strong results.
  2. For each winning product, create a new CBO campaign with three creatives.
  3. Wait until the new CBO is live and running.
  4. Only then turn off the individual ABO ad set for that product. [L0348.034]
- Once the best cost cap is found, move it into its own individual CBO campaign. Use that cap across other winning audiences and broad campaigns as you scale. [L0977.024]
- Create three CBO campaigns using the winning interest, then increase budgets there when the data supports it. [L1161.029]

### Horizontal scaling procedures

- Horizontal scaling procedure for a winning ad set:
  1. Move the winning ad set into its own scaling campaign.
  2. Duplicate it, for example 10 times, as ABO campaigns with about $20 in each.
  3. After a short spend, switch off the copies that have no sales; for example, kill an ad after a day without sales.
  4. Keep the copies that perform well and scale them further. [L0995.025]
- Horizontal scaling means duplicating a winning ad set into a new campaign, often about 10 copies, then keeping the best performer and turning off the rest. It is used when the ROAS stays profitable but no longer supports a large budget jump. [L0646.007]
- Budget testing with a small ad set: run three copies at 10, 15 and 20 units of budget to find the best budget for the ad, then scale the winner horizontally by duplicating it again. [L0646.008]
- You can duplicate your top 3 to 5 winning audiences or interests into a CBO campaign with a $150 to $200 budget. Use this when a single ad set's budget increases stop working. [L0376.018, L0375.014]
- Once a campaign is unprofitable, check it at ad set and ad level first. A single audience or creative can drag down the whole campaign, so kill that bad ad set or creative. [L0375.023]
- Keeping underperforming ad sets running alongside the winners drains budget, so turn them off consistently. [L0375.033]
- If an interest or ad set stops working across several days, switch it off and try something else. An interest that stopped can sometimes work again after days or weeks, so a stop does not mean it is dead for good. [L1107.010]

### Vertical scaling: budget steps and rules

Merged from the table and procedure with the same content:

| Condition | Action |
|---|---|
| Yesterday ROAS is at or above the benchmark ROAS (BE ROAS + 20% margin) | Increase budget by 20% (vertical scaling). Or duplicate the ad set 2 to 3 times at higher budgets. Or duplicate the campaign at a higher budget. |
| Yesterday ROAS is about equal to BE ROAS | No action needed. |
| Last 3 days + today ROAS is about equal to BE ROAS | Reduce budget by 20% to stabilize. |
| Last 3 days + today ROAS is below BE ROAS | Turn off the ad sets (kill losers). |

[L0375.020, L0376.015]

Vertical scaling by duplicating, from the table and the related procedure:

| Condition | Action |
|---|---|
| Yesterday ROAS is at or above the benchmark ROAS (BE ROAS + 20% margin) | Duplicate the ad set 3 to 4 times at higher budgets. Or duplicate the campaign at a higher budget. |
| Yesterday ROAS is about equal to BE ROAS | No action needed. |
| Last 3 days + today ROAS is about equal to BE ROAS | Pause the ad sets, then duplicate and refresh them. |
| Last 3 days + today ROAS is below BE ROAS | Kill the ad sets. |

[L0375.021, L0376.016]

- Scaling an ad set with a 20 percent step: when an ad set consistently produces sales and has at least 4 purchases, raise its budget by 20 percent of the initial budget every 24 hours, as long as it performs 20 percent above BE ROAS. Check whether budget increases or duplication work better for your account. [L0375.014, L0376.017]
- Scaling by a 20 percent step on a moderate ad: when an ad has only a moderate result but is still profitable, duplicate it at a 20 percent budget step. Run it a few days, and if it holds, raise the budget again by 20 percent. This avoids risking large budgets on an ad whose ROAS may drop. [L0646.009]
- Scaling a profitable Advantage+ campaign:
  1. Start with a budget such as $100.
  2. Raise the budget by 20% every two to three days while the campaign stays profitable.
  3. If results drop after a few days, return to the previous budget level.
  4. If the campaign is not profitable after about a week, close it and scale a winning interest instead. [L1161.028]
- Scaling a winning Facebook ad with a slow, step-by-step increase:
  1. Keep the winning ad in an ABO campaign with its own budget.
  2. Raise the budget by about $20 on the same ad, since it already has engagement and Facebook has learned from it.
  3. Run it for a few days.
  4. If the ad holds, add another $20 after about three more days.
  5. Repeat and raise the budget slowly while the return stays acceptable.
  6. Once the ad is strong at a higher budget (e.g. $100), test it against a more aggressive budget, then move to $200 and $400 if it holds. [L0995.024]
- Scaling an ad that has proven itself:
  1. Keep the original ad set running; do not touch it.
  2. Duplicate the ad set three times.
  3. Put more budget into each copy, for example three ad sets at about $40 per day.
  4. Test under the bigger budgets and see which one holds. [L1315.022]
- Scale a CBO campaign by raising the budget by 25 percent. If profit margin falls after a 25 percent increase in budget, reduce the budget again (descale). [L0348.013]
- Scaling by a fixed step on a CBO, with a 100-dollar starting point:
  1. Find an interest in ABO testing that gives at least 3 purchases with a ROAS above break-even.
  2. Launch a CBO campaign with a $100 budget.
  3. Create three duplicate ad sets, each with the same winning interest.
  4. Add the winning ads (creatives) to the ad sets, using two to three creatives rather than one.
  5. If the campaign runs for about 12 hours with a ROAS of 3 or more and at least 3 purchases (2 purchases can be enough), raise the budget by $100, for example from $100 to $200.
  6. Do not raise the budget again on the same day. Check the next day and repeat the +$100 step (to $300, then $400) only while the ROAS holds.
  7. If the CBO is not profitable on day one, cut it. If it is profitable on day one but not on day two, let it run to day three; cut it if day three is also unprofitable.
  8. Keep testing new interests with 15 to 20 percent of the budget so the next winners can be moved into CBO. [L0645.009]
- Aggressive scaling method: double the CBO budget repeatedly until reaching 500 dollars; thereafter increase budget by only 20% per adjustment. [L0298.008]
- Budget scaling rule: increase daily ad spend by a maximum of 2x (e.g., $50 per day to $100-150 per day), and only after observing 2 to 3 weeks of consistent profitability. Aggressive scaling without proven consistency leads to losses. [L0321.028]
- When results are strong, scale aggressively by nearly doubling the budget, for example from 50 to 80 pounds per day, and keep it if performance holds for a few days before the next step. Sustained performance at the higher level then justifies moving to the next budget step, such as 100 pounds per day. [L0646.004]
- Return on ad spend typically drops sharply after the first aggressive budget increase, then falls by smaller amounts with each further increase. The largest drop comes from the initial jump, so a strategy must allow for that first decline. [L0646.005]
- Aggressive scaling is safe only if the ROAS after the jump stays about 0.8 above break-even. If it does, the budget can be doubled again and the ad set moved into its own scaling campaign. [L0646.006]
- A Meta campaign that performs well can be scaled in steps, for example from about $100 per day to $500 per day, and later to the thousands or tens of thousands per day. Each step should follow only a stable result with a strong audience and targeting. Jumping directly to high budgets is a common way to trigger restrictions. [L0637.062]
- Scaling a test from 5 dollars per day to 100 dollars per day in one jump led to an ad account block. Scale in gradual steps after the product shows profit. [L1131.045]
- Do not double a budget on one day, and do not copy a 300 dollar CBO to a new 300 dollar campaign. Neither carries a guarantee, and either can break a working setup. Increase in steps across days and watch the ad account's response before duplicating. [L0645.047]
- Do not change every campaign at once when scaling, because a bad budget change can hurt all campaigns in the account. Apply a change to one CBO campaign, watch the results, and then extend it to others. [L0645.011]
- Scale a winning campaign by duplicating it at a higher budget only when ROAS is well above break-even. If results are only acceptable, raise the budget of the existing campaign instead. [L0692.029]
- Example: a $50 CBO campaign with a strong ROAS can be duplicated at $100 while the original stays at $50, bringing total daily spend to $150. If the $100 copy fails, retry the duplicate at $50 or increase the original budget by 20 percent. [L0692.030]
- On Facebook, smaller daily ad set budgets of roughly £10 to £30 often deliver better and cheaper impressions than large budgets. Scale across several small ad sets first, and raise budgets in steps rather than doubling a single ad set, because big jumps make delivery search harder. [L1426.048]
- Short event campaigns such as Black Friday or Christmas may be scaled up and down quickly, but evergreen campaigns need a steady 7-day window before any budget change. [L1016.060]
- Facebook budget steps for a campaign started at $50 per day:
  1. Start the Facebook campaign at $50 per day and make sure the creatives and page are solid.
  2. Let it run for 7 days.
  3. If all target metrics are hitting, raise the budget by 15%.
  4. Let it breathe for about 3 days.
  5. If it still performs well, raise the budget another 15% (a 15-20% step stays safe for the learning phase).
  6. If traffic arrives but there are no sales after 7 days, turn the ads off and refine the website before spending again. [L1016.057]

### Duplication methods

- Triple duplication for a CBO with a $300 total start:
  1. Launch three campaigns with the same ad set at the same budget each.
  2. Start with a total of about $300, for example $100 per campaign, and check that setup is correct.
  3. Optionally start at $200 per scaling campaign.
  4. Add an automatic rule that turns a campaign off when it spends its break-even cost per purchase without producing a sale.
  5. If it fails, relaunch after a couple of weeks at a different budget. [L0349.018]
- Triple duplication with high budgets, using 200 USD per CBO as the example:
  1. Launch three identical CBO campaigns with the same ad sets and creatives, each on a high budget (the example used 200 USD per CBO).
  2. Let all three run until about 10 to 20 USD of spend.
  3. Turn off any CBO with very high CPCs and no sales.
  4. If one CBO has a sale in this phase, keep it running and turn off the other two.
  5. If no CBO has a sale after about 15 to 20 USD of spend (the break-even), turn off the weakest one and let the other two spend another 15 USD.
  6. If one of the two still has no sale after that, turn off both. [L0648.047]
- Use triple duplication only after roughly two to three months, once the product works and the budget is high. For the start, a single CBO campaign is enough. [L0648.048]
- Audience overlap above 15 to 20 percent is poor. More than three duplicated CBOs on the same audiences is likely to add competition with yourself, raise CPM and waste spend, so treat it as an untested risk. [L0648.049]
- Duplicate the ad set five times into a CBO campaign, creating three CBOs, if the budget increase steps stop working. Keep the one that performs, close the others, then scale the budget of the winning CBO. [L0773.032]
- Three-campaign scaling structure:
  1. For each winning interest, create one CBO campaign with four ad sets.
  2. Put three creatives in each ad set.
  3. Duplicate that campaign so you have three campaigns.
  4. Launch all three together.
  5. Set an automatic rule that turns off campaigns with no sale after roughly eight to twelve hours or after spend equal to the break-even cost per purchase.
  6. Keep the best-performing campaign and turn off the other two. [L0692.028]
- Duplicating with one variable changed, used when budget changes and duplication do not work:
  1. Duplicate the ad set 3 to 4 times at the same budget.
  2. Change one thing per ad set, keeping the interest the same. For example, use men only or women only (depending on the product).
  3. Narrow the ad set to the best country.
  4. Narrow the ad set by age.
  5. Narrow the ad set to the best placement and platform. [L0375.024, L0376.019]
- Ad set variants for the one-variable method:
  1. Ad set 1: men only or women only (depends on the product).
  2. Ad set 2: narrow down to the best country.
  3. Ad set 3: narrow down the age range.
  4. Ad set 4: narrow down to the best placement and platform. [L0375.024]
- Duplicating an ad set in Ads Manager:
  1. Open the campaign, ad set or ad that should be reused.
  2. Press Duplicate and then Duplicate again to create a copy inside the same level.
  3. For a new ad, rename the copy named copy and swap in the new creative.
  4. For a new ad set, duplicate the ad set and then change the name and the ads.
  5. For a new campaign, duplicate the campaign because it carries over all ad sets and ads inside it. [L0865.055]
- Copying ads between ad sets:
  1. Select the ads to copy with a click on the first one and Shift-click on the last one.
  2. Press Command C on a Mac to copy them.
  3. Open the target ad set and press Command V to paste the ads.
  4. While the pasted ads are still selected, remove the word copy at the end of their names.
  5. Use Edit > Find and replace, type dash copy in the find field, leave the replace field empty, press Replace and then save the draft. [L0865.056]
- Budget testing and duplication: a case where the first sales were about 1,700 in revenue (currency not stated). The budget was raised three times within 10 days, and each time Facebook cut it back the next day, while support staff could not explain the cap. Horizontal scaling with separate interest-based ad sets was used as a workaround. [L0644.016]

### Automated rules and loss control

- For campaigns with a 100,000+ dollar budget, implement automated rules to prevent losses at each scaling stage. [L0298.007]
- Set an automatic rule that switches off a scaling campaign once its spend reaches the break-even cost per purchase without a single sale. Automatic rules matter because campaigns may launch overnight and run unattended. [L0349.019]
- Set an automatic rule that turns off campaigns with no sale after roughly eight to twelve hours, or after spend equal to the break-even cost per purchase. [L0692.028]
- Losses on individual days are normal under about $1,000 daily spend, so a profitable day can absorb one or two losing days. Consistent results usually appear once a campaign has spent above $500, and a bigger budget can show inconsistent CBO days that the other campaigns offset. [L0645.046]
- Review the allocation of a campaign regularly and turn off the weak ad sets after enough data has accumulated. [L0639.015]
- Scaling a winning interest: confirm the interest has at least 6 profitable sales, then choose a scaling method:
  1. Raise the ad set budget (example: from $20 to $40 per day), or duplicate the winning interest into a CBO campaign.
  2. For a CBO duplication, use a $100 campaign budget with the winning interest copied 4 times, so each copy reaches a different pocket.
  3. Test both methods, since one may work better than the other in a given account.
  4. Check which ad sets receive budget and cut underperformers once you have enough data. [L0639.014]

### Self-scaling, events and refresh

- Use self-scaling (automated budget increases every 2 to 4 hours) only during specific high-intent events: Halloween, BFCM, Cyber Monday, or with very aggressive offers (60% off vs. typical 40%). [L0298.012]
- Self-scaling works during events because people have high purchase intent and expect sales. During normal days, frequent budget increases hurt performance. [L0298.013]
- Winning Facebook campaigns often fade after about one to two months. Plan to refresh by changing the creative, the hook or the audience, or by duplicating into a new campaign before results drop. [L1213.034]

### Creative testing allocation and budget split

- Allocate 20% of daily ad budget to creative testing. If the daily budget is 500 dollars, dedicate 100 dollars to testing. [L0298.047]
- Keep spending 15 to 20 percent of the budget on testing new audiences and creatives, even for products that have worked for months. Keep a separate audience testing sandbox and creative testing sandbox to do this. [L0648.051]
- Split a daily ad budget into 20% for testing, 10% for retargeting and 70% for existing winning audiences and scaling campaigns. Example: on $100 per day, allocate $20 to testing, $10 to retargeting and $70 to scaling. [L1213.030]
- Keep testing new interests with 15 to 20 percent of the budget so the next winners can be moved into CBO. [L0645.009]
- Testing across formats, such as bottom-of-funnel, UGC, natives and whitelist pages, maximizes the chance that something works. A format that fails still gives information, and a winner in one format is still a win. [L1476.054]
- Aggressive scaling to very high monthly revenue needs winning audiences, winning creatives, strong email and SMS marketing, and Google Ads in place. Without these assets, scaling spend usually causes losses. [L1213.035]

### Advantage+ and campaign build

- Advantage+ campaign setup under the Sales objective:
  1. Click Create and choose the Sales objective.
  2. Keep Buying type on Auction and click Continue.
  3. Enter the campaign name with product, budget type, creative type and date or year.
  4. Under Budget, choose Advantage+ campaign budget (CBO) and enter the daily budget.
  5. Set Campaign bid strategy to Highest volume or value.
  6. Name the ad set after its creative.
  7. Under Conversion, set Conversion location to Website, Performance goal to Maximize number of conversions, Dataset to the default pixel and Conversion event to Purchase.
  8. Under Show more settings, open Attribution setting, choose Standard attribution and set the selections to 7-day click only, with engaged-view and view-through set to None.
  9. Check the start date, end date and time zone.
  10. Set ad set spending limits only if needed.
  11. Under Audience, keep Broad, 18 to 65+ and all genders, and keep Advantage+ audience on.
  12. Keep Placements on Automatic with Advantage+ placements on.
  13. Name the ad.
  14. Under Identity, select the Facebook Page and check the Instagram account.
  15. Under Ad setup, choose Create ad, Manual upload and Single image or video.
  16. Enter the destination URL to a collection or product page and keep Use shop default off.
  17. Under Creative, leave Source URL empty, turn off the catalog if it is not optimized and crop the vertical 9:16 version.
  18. Enter primary text, a headline if wanted and choose Shop Now.
  19. Skip Meta AI options and turn off Enhancements except Show products when the catalog is optimized.
  20. Set URL parameters, check the preview, then press Publish. [L0865.054]
- There is no single strategy that works for every product or ad account. Keep testing broad and interest approaches and scale whichever one proves profitable. [L1161.030]

### Bottom-of-funnel scaling

- BOF has a hard scaling ceiling due to a limited warm audience size. For solution-aware products, BOF remains profitable indefinitely but cannot scale beyond the fixed daily impression supply. BOF harvests warm conversions rather than generating the volume needed for $1M+ monthly revenue. [L0315.013]
- Attempting to scale BOF to large monthly revenue targets is a mistake. The warm audience pool is inherently small, imposing a strict ceiling on impressions and spend. Though BOF delivers high ROAS on available inventory, the limited scale makes it unsuitable as a primary growth driver. [L0315.014]
- Introduce new bottom-of-funnel concepts weekly (e.g., new testimonials, new benefits angles, new social proof). Rotate fatigued ad sets out. Do not constantly refresh if your video campaign is working well, because the inflating warm audience keeps ROAS high even on existing ads. [L0316.019]
- Scale bottom-of-funnel budget only until ROAS drops to your comfort threshold. If your breakeven ROAS is 1.5X, scale until you reach 2.5X ROAS, then hold budget steady. Do not overscale. Monitor ROAS and reduce if it drops below your target. [L0316.020]
- Set bottom-of-funnel budget at roughly 30% of what your video ads are spending. This is a guideline, not a fixed rule. Adjust based on ROAS and available inventory. If your video campaign is still strong, the warm pool keeps inflating, and you can maintain steady bottom-of-funnel budget without constant increases. [L0316.021]
- Adding new ads is better than reducing budget. If an account is underperforming, introduce new ad concepts and variations to give the algorithm more material to work with, rather than cutting spend. Reducing spend only shrinks the business; growing requires adding new creative constantly. [L0316.036]

### Targeting, demographics and ceilings

- Choose a single winning gender and age from the breakdown rather than mixing all demographics. For a product aimed at both genders, check whether men or women perform better and focus budget on the stronger group. [L0773.028]
- Facebook groups audience targeting at the ad account level, so ads in different campaigns can still reach the same people. Separate campaigns per format are still worth it to control budget and scale each format on its own. [L1476.037]
- Every ad has a scaling ceiling where spend stops being profitable. Example from the lesson: one ad can take about $2,000 a day, another dies at about $100 a day, images may cap near $200 a day, and strong videos can scale to about $3,000 a day. Separate formats let each one run at its own ceiling. [L1476.038]
- Facebook's campaign budget optimization shifts budget by percentage and can push about 95 percent of spend into one favored ad, whatever the total budget. Putting different formats into one campaign lets a favored video starve the other ads. [L1476.039]
- If an ad in a campaign gets no spend, move it to its own separate campaign to give it a fair test. The point of a new campaign is to isolate the variable. [L1476.040]
- The copy-analysis method for finding winning ads is most useful once you are scaling. Apply it when you are moving beyond testing. [L0772.035]
- Scale a CBO campaign with a low-volume test before extending it: a budget step only follows a stable result with a strong audience and targeting. [L0637.062]

### Team, time and staffing

- After switching to the Power Five approach, a whole team needs about 5 hours per week for Facebook ads, down from more than 50 hours per week with three people. [L0649.005]
- If lead generation is not running yet, focus first on hot dynamic, hot static, customers goodwill and promotions. Lead generation comes after these are working. [L0649.051]
- Quick wins come first: set up dynamic remarketing with varied ads, add a short video ad for hot traffic, run a goodwill video, promote to warm audiences and match email promotions. Lead magnets, opt-in pages and designed assets can wait. [L0649.052]
- At the business-owner stage, a trained virtual assistant can set up and manage Facebook ad campaigns while the founder reviews them, analyzes results and makes the final spending and offer decisions. Hire an ad manager only if they are trained on your account or show a strong CV with past client results. [L1432.039]

### Segmentation by profit margin

- Segment alpha-beta campaigns by profit margin only after campaigns have generated enough historical data to identify which products drive the most sales with the highest profit margins. This requires completing all standard optimizations (negative keywords, site conversion optimization) and is not a day-one activity for new stores. [L0224.002]

#### Further details

- A product went from launch on 13 to 14 June to about $100k in 40 days. The first CBO on 18 June started at $100, went to $200 after about 12 hours at a ROAS above 3, then to $300 with ROAS 3.1 and spend of about $285. Daily ad spend reached about $1,400 to $1,500 and one campaign generated about $4,000 in revenue, with later daily spend of about $1,300 at a ROAS of 2.45 and return of about $3,221. [L0645.048]

## Scaling TikTok, Pinterest and other channels

### Deciding When to Add a Second Channel

- Keep Google Ads as the main traffic source until the store reaches roughly $30,000 to $50,000 in monthly revenue before adding other channels. This is older advice from 2019. [L0663.019]
- At monthly sales of about $5,000 to $20,000, it is not worth taking time away from Google Ads to build other channels, even if Google seems not to be scaling. This is older advice from 2019. [L0663.021]
- Channels like Pinterest work but take time to master, because they require learning a new skill. Adding them too early takes focus away from the core Google setup. This is older advice from 2019. [L0663.020]
- Move to other channels when Google growth stalls, meaning budget increases no longer add sales. Then test Pinterest properly and try Facebook and Instagram ads to cold audiences. This is older advice from 2019. [L0663.022]
- When the profitable Google Shopping limit is reached, more traffic has to come from new sources such as organic search and social media. Pushing harder inside Google Shopping does not solve it. This is advice from Source B, 2021 to 2022. [L0744.052]
- Once Google Shopping cannot be scaled further at a 10X return, it is time to consider adding other advertising platforms. [L0750.009]
- Scale a winning product on Facebook until its audience is exhausted, then test TikTok ads for it. Most products in this approach performed best on Facebook. [L1017.017]
- When a store is profitable but not scaling, open another channel such as Google to turn the business into an ecosystem with more touch points. [L0950.063]
- Build an ecosystem of channels (Facebook, TikTok, Google) so that each channel covers gaps the others leave, especially as Q4 approaches. [L0950.066]
- Scaling is limited by payment setup and cash flow rather than by the number of TikTok accounts. Fix the setup, systems and rules before scaling aggressively. [L0363.070]
- Direct-response TV and radio campaigns are a scaling option to consider once social ads are working. They require significant spending and carry financial risk if they do not work, so test before committing large budgets. [L0334.052]

### Fallback When a Main Ad Account Is Suspended

- While a main Facebook ad account is suspended, the seller can keep testing products on other channels such as Snapchat, Pinterest, TikTok and Twitter, then move to Google Ads and return to Facebook once it is restored. [L0562.040]
- Running several channels in parallel reduces dependence on one blocked account. [L0562.040]

### Small Budgets and the Five-Month Checklist (Older Advice, 2019)

- On a small budget, growth can come from a unique angle or a viral post rather than outbidding competitors on ads. [L0357.060]
- A post that gets comments, shares and reactions can be boosted with about $5 using targeting, which is cheaper than trying to win a bidding war. [L0357.060]
- Five-month marketing checklist when Google Ads alone is not enough:
  1. Run Bing Shopping. [L0357.059]
  2. Use influencer marketing. [L0357.059]
  3. Use blog banner advertising. [L0357.059]
  4. Raise search engine optimization to the highest priority. [L0357.059]
  5. Run paid social traffic campaigns. [L0357.059]
  6. Use referral marketing. [L0357.059]
  7. Run email marketing. [L0357.059]

### TikTok Organic: Posting and Momentum

- Standard upload frequency is 3 videos per day, 4 on a day when content goes viral. A target of 4 to 6 videos per day is suggested for accounts that have the time to produce them. [L0764.030]
- After a video goes viral, post more and do not hesitate to make similar videos. Repeating a proven format with small changes in sound, wording, color or movement can keep the momentum going. [L0764.039]
- Creators often stop posting after one viral hit because they fear damaging the account. This wastes the momentum, since a viral moment should be used by increasing output. [L0764.040]
- Inconsistent uploading, such as one upload a day with long gaps, lets viral momentum fade. Keep uploading regularly to make the most of any breakout video. [L0764.041]
- Once organic videos prove they sell, fund paid ads from the profit those videos already generated rather than from your own money. [L0820.048]
- Keep the sequence: organic content first, then paid ads to maximize sales, then brand building once the product is proven. [L0820.048]

### TikTok Accounts: Number and Setup

- You can operate 2 to 3 or more TikTok accounts simultaneously on the same phone. No additional devices need to be purchased; log in and out as needed. [L0309.038]
- Use separate email addresses for each account. [L0309.038]
- Assign each account a purpose: testing and research, product promotion, or personal use. [L0309.038]
- Sources differ on the limit per phone. One source says no more than three accounts per phone, ideally two, with about seven mentioned as a maximum. A ban on one account can hurt the others on the same device. [L1444.057]
- Use separate phones for separate products and keep successful accounts apart from experimental ones. This protects the working account from risk when new tests are added. [L1444.058]
- Do not log into one TikTok account from two phones at the same time. Share finished video files through a cloud folder and let one person upload to the account. [L1444.059]
- Split team roles so one person builds the store while the other creates content. This allows the content creator to test products faster. [L1444.060]

### TikTok Account Structure and Prospecting Setup

- TikTok ad account structure should be simple and broad. There is no need for dozens of campaigns or many ad sets per campaign, because the platform performs best with broad targeting. [L1374.009]
- Start with no more than five ad groups per campaign, and three is a good starting number. A typical start includes broad and recommended interest ad sets, plus one hashtag ad set that often underperforms over time. [L1374.029]
- Set up a prospecting campaign with three ad sets:
  1. Ad set 1 is broad targeting with both genders, all ages except 13-17 excluded, and no interests added. [L1374.010]
  2. Ad set 2 uses TikTok's Recommended Interests by clicking the use these button. [L1374.010]
  3. Ad set 3 uses hashtag targeting with one ad set per hashtag. [L1374.010]
- Start a new TikTok prospecting campaign with Lower Cost (maximized conversions), not Value Optimization. TikTok needs customer data for value optimization to work, and performance is poor without data. [L1374.011]
- TikTok Recommended Interests is the best interest-targeting option. In the speaker's tests it outperformed manually entered interests in 9.5 out of 10 tests. Use the recommended interests as-is, even though some may look odd. [L1374.012]
- For hashtag targeting, use about three hashtags, each in its own ad set so you can see which one produces results. Hashtag targeting is not available in every ad account yet, and hashtags that are brand-associated or product-specific may not show up as options. [L1374.013]

### TikTok Budget Structure: ABO and CBO

- For testing, split the TikTok ad budget by ad set (ABO) rather than by campaign (CBO), because CBO can send most of the budget to one ad set and make the test unfair. [L1374.014]
- The speaker prefers ABO at all stages, but notes CBO may improve as TikTok's algorithm matures, as Facebook's did. [L1374.014]
- Ad set budget optimization (ABO): set the budget at ad set level, for example 10 dollars each for three ad sets. [L1374.015]
- Campaign budget optimization (CBO): set a total campaign budget, for example 30 dollars, which the algorithm distributes across ad sets. [L1374.015]
- Under CBO, ad sets can train each other through shared campaign-level optimization. Data from a well-performing ad set can help a broad ad set find buyers. With ABO, ad sets optimize separately and do not share data. [L1374.016]
- Example: with a 30 dollar campaign budget under CBO, the algorithm can give about 25 dollars per day to one ad set and only about 2.50 dollars per day to the other two. This is why ABO gives each ad set an equal budget and makes a split test fair. [L1374.038]
- Source disagreement: in the operator's experience, CBO performed poorly on TikTok, in the same way it did on Facebook when it was introduced, so budgets were set manually per campaign. [L0819.021]
- Source disagreement: later units use CBO campaigns for scaling and for the staged budget ladder, once a creative and audience are proven. See the scaling sections below. [L1436.055, L1382.046, L1375.013]

### TikTok Scaling: Horizontal and Vertical

- Scaling procedure for TikTok ads:
  1. Run one campaign dedicated to scaling and one dedicated to testing. [L0819.020]
  2. Judge performance by comparing Google Analytics data with TikTok ad results using UTMs. [L0819.020]
  3. If a campaign performs well, duplicate it three times. [L0819.020]
  4. Double the budget on the duplicates. [L0819.020]
  5. Run a separate testing campaign for new creatives continuously, and move creatives that beat the benchmark into a scaling campaign. [L0819.020]
- Vertical scaling versus horizontal scaling on TikTok:
  1. Horizontal scaling: duplicate a winning ad set without changing anything, so 25 dollars per day becomes 50 dollars across two copies. [L1374.020]
  2. Vertical scaling: increase the budget of the winning ad set step by step, for example 25, 50, 75, 100 dollars. [L1374.020]
  3. Use horizontal scaling at lower budgets (around 25 dollars per day), because the percentage increase from vertical scaling is large and can trigger re-optimization. [L1374.020]
  4. Use vertical scaling at higher budgets (around 500 dollars per day), because a 500 to 700 increase is a small percentage change. [L1374.020]
  5. Split test both methods and keep the one that works best. [L1374.020]
- A simple account with broad targeting and few campaigns is reported to have scaled smoothly to about 5,000 dollars per day, and it is claimed to scale to about 10,000 dollars per day. Scaling happens by raising the budget of ad sets with a positive return on ad spend. [L1374.032]
- Duplicating an ad set that already has enough data can move the copy automatically toward value optimization. Duplicating into value optimization is only useful after data exists, because early value optimization without data can perform very badly. [L1374.033]
- Value optimization transition on TikTok:
  1. After the ad set has enough data, duplicate the best-performing ad set. [L1374.030]
  2. Switch the copy from Lower Cost to Value Optimization. [L1374.030]
  3. Scale that copy by raising its budget. [L1374.030]
  4. Do not switch to Value Optimization early when there is no data, because results can go badly wrong. [L1374.030]

### TikTok Staged Budget Ladder (CBO)

- Staged scaling procedure for TikTok CBO campaigns:
  1. Confirm that CPC is below $1, CPM is below $10, CTR is above 1.5%, and the store conversion rate is good. [L1375.010]
  2. Start from the initial CBO with a daily budget of $50. [L1375.010]
  3. Double the budget to $100 and monitor results for three hours. [L1375.010]
  4. If results stay consistent or improve, raise the budget to $150 after three hours. [L1375.010]
  5. If results still hold, raise it to $200 after another three hours. [L1375.010]
  6. If results still hold, raise it to $250 after another three hours. [L1375.010]
  7. At the end of the day, reset the budget to $50 so the next day starts from scratch, then repeat steps 3 to 6. [L1375.010]
- Each increase waits three hours. The budget reaches $250 nine hours after the first move to $100, and the method then ends with the end-of-day reset to $50. [L1375.015]

| Step | Budget | Time after first move to $100 |
|---|---|---|
| Start | $50 | before scaling |
| 1 | $100 | 0 hours |
| 2 | $150 | 3 hours |
| 3 | $200 | 6 hours |
| 4 | $250 | 9 hours |
| End of day | reset to $50 | end of day [L1375.010, L1375.015] |

- If results get worse after a budget increase, reset the budget to $50 and retry the scaling sequence the next day. [L1375.011]
- Higher budgets bring more reach, more traffic to the store, and more sales, so budget increases should be staged and checked for consistency rather than made all at once. [L1375.012]
- When several CBOs perform well, increase the budget on all of them and apply the same staged scaling method. [L1375.013]

### TikTok Creative Testing and Break-Even

- Break-even test per creative: let each creative spend up to the product profit. For example, $20 profit allows $20 spend per creative. Keep the creative running only if it produces a purchase; kill it otherwise. [L1376.009]
- TikTok ads work like a casino in their first two days, when they deliver the best traffic. A creative with no sales in those two days is unlikely to start selling later and should be stopped. [L1376.010]
- Starting a testing phase: begin with one ad group per creative. With 10 creatives this means 10 ad groups at $20 per day each, with no interest targeting. [L1376.014]
- Vertical scaling on TikTok with ad groups:
  1. Find more winning creatives first; with only three winners, scaling to large numbers is unrealistic. [L1376.011]
  2. Create variants of the best performing creatives by changing small elements such as music, the first two seconds or the hook. [L1376.011]
  3. When several creatives perform, raise each ad group budget from $20 to $100 per day. [L1376.011]
  4. Stack the top three best performing creatives into one ad group instead of one creative per ad group. [L1376.011]
  5. Each day at about 11:30 to 11:45 pm (before midnight), increase the budget of these ad groups by $20, for example $100 to $120, then $140, and so on. [L1376.011]
- Judge creatives by CTR and CPC, and cut ad groups that reached the break-even cost without sales. [L1382.035]
- In the testing stage, the goal is to learn which ad group, audience and creative work, not to get immediate sales from every test. If the landing page is good and add-to-cart and checkout rates are healthy, an ad group that spent the break-even cost without a sale can be turned off. [L1382.038]
- The purpose of the testing stage is not an immediate sale but finding out which audience, which creative and which ad group works, judged by CPC and CTR. [L1436.051]
- When sales are unclear, judge the creative by CTR. An ad showing a CTR of 1 percent or more across ad groups was the strongest candidate in the test. [L1436.053]
- A high CPM on some TikTok ad accounts can distort the results, so a poor result is not always caused by the product or creative. Check the account before blaming the test. [L1436.086]

### TikTok Manual Bidding Scaling

- Manual bidding scaling on TikTok:
  1. Create a new manual bidding campaign once you have several winning creatives and winning ad groups. [L1376.012]
  2. Set the daily budget per ad group between $100 and $300, depending on total budget. [L1376.012]
  3. Create five ad groups with different target cost-per-purchase values, starting from the average cost per purchase. Example: average $12, then $11.50, $11, $10.50 and $10. [L1376.012]
  4. Lower-target ad groups may perform well and buy purchases at a lower cost. [L1376.012]
  5. Do not raise budgets linearly. Duplicate a well-performing ad group and double its budget, for example $100 to $200, then $400, then a $300 to $400 range as used. [L1376.012]
  6. Keep watching the ad groups closely, because manual bidding can overspend suddenly. [L1376.012]
- Manual bidding on TikTok can suddenly overspend, for example spending $100 to $200 per day without matching purchases even when the budget is set to $300 and earlier spend was low. Monitor these ad groups daily. [L1376.013]
- Scaling a manual bidding ad group on TikTok: do not raise its budget linearly. Duplicate the ad group that performed well and set the duplicate's budget to double the original, for example $100 to $200, then $300 to $400 with the next duplicate. Keep checking each duplicate because overspend can happen suddenly. [L1376.029]

### TikTok Ad Group Scaling and Replacement

- Scale by duplicating winning ad groups, and consider switching to cost cap bidding once a campaign has about 50 or more purchases. [L1377.055]
- Replace underperforming ad groups with new creatives rather than letting them run unchanged. [L1377.055]
- If a test campaign is in the last four to five hours of the day, launch new ad groups the next day with fresh creatives instead of changing it mid-day. [L1377.056]
- Compare the next day's results across creatives and identify which part of the funnel fails. [L1377.056]

### TikTok Test Campaign Structures

- Test structure: one campaign with five ad groups (the original plus four duplicates), each holding two to four video ads. [L1378.023]
- Let the test campaign run for two days before evaluating results. [L1378.024]
- Testing and scaling in order, with one campaign of three to four ad groups:
  1. Launch one campaign with three to four ad groups, for example open audience, stacked interest, hashtags, and another open or stacked interest audience. [L1382.035]
  2. Set each ad group budget to about $25 to $30 and let it run the first day. [L1382.035]
  3. Put in the three best creatives per ad group, with one ad copy using an offer line. [L1382.035]
  4. Judge creatives by CTR and CPC, and cut ad groups that reached the break-even cost without sales. [L1382.035]
  5. Once one creative and audience are winning, build CBO campaigns (see the aggressive scaling procedure below). [L1382.035]
- Testing structure for a new product on TikTok:
  1. Create one campaign with three to four ad groups, each with a different audience, for example open targeting, a stacked interest audience, a hashtag audience, and one more open or stacked audience. [L1436.050]
  2. Set each ad group budget to $25 to $30. A $20 ad group budget caused spending problems on new accounts. [L1436.050]
  3. Add the three best creatives to each ad group (add 1, add 2, add 3), with the same ad copy in each group. [L1436.050]
  4. Use one ad copy with an offer line such as 70% off, get yours today or ending soon. [L1436.050]
