Actual result: 5X ROAS, below the expected 10X, so the test was iterated instead of kept as is. [L0410.004]

Example of an iteration logged as a new row: after the first YouTube remarketing test returned 5X ROAS, the video ads in the campaign were swapped and the test was logged again as a new experiment with the expected result of over 10X ROAS. The note in the new row referenced the 5X result of the original test, so the link between the two experiments stayed visible. [L0410.005]

#### Deciding after a test and before repeating an idea

- When a test fails to reach its expected ROAS, decide between two options: turn the campaign off, or change its structure, targeting, or ads and run it again as a new logged experiment. Either way, record the decision so the same idea is not retested without knowing the earlier outcome. [L0410.006]

Use the changelog in team meetings before a new idea is accepted:
1. When someone proposes a change, search the log for earlier tests of the same element or platform. [L0410.014]
2. If a matching test exists, read its Expected Results, Result Date and Results. [L0410.014]
3. Only propose the idea again if the earlier outcome is recorded as a different setup or was never run, and log the new attempt. [L0410.014]

### CRO test boards (older advice, 2021-22)

- Trello boards can track every CRO test in one place, with columns for future tests, current tests, completed tests, insights and notes. Trello is easy to share with virtual assistants and team members, who often already know it. [L1364.039]
- The tool does not decide success, so a spreadsheet with the same columns or another project tool such as Asana or Basecamp works equally well. Pick the tool that keeps all tracking in one place and is quick to use. [L1364.044]

Structure of a CRO test tracking board:

| Column | Purpose |
|---|---|
| Future Tests | Test ideas planned but not started |
| Current Tests | Tests running now |
| Completed Tests | Finished tests, with sub-lists Winning Tests and Failed or Inconclusive Tests |
| Insights | Ideas for further tests drawn from results |
| Notes | Loose ideas and reminders not related to a current test |

[L1364.040]

Contents of each test card:

| Field | Content |
|---|---|
| Description | What is being tested and the exact change |
| Duration | How long the test will run |
| Notes | Specific data points or other points worth recording |
| Attachments | Exports from Google Analytics, Google Ads or Shopify with data linked to the test |

[L1364.041]

Test card workflow:
1. Create a card for each planned test in Future Tests with its description, duration and attachments. [L1364.042]
2. When the test starts, move the card to Current Tests. [L1364.042]
3. Set a calendar reminder for the end date and review running tests in a weekly to-do session. [L1364.042]
4. When the test ends, move the card to Completed Tests and update it with results, comments and any additional attachments. [L1364.042]
5. Decide whether the test is winning or failed or inconclusive. [L1364.042]
6. Copy the card into the matching winning or failed sub-list. [L1364.042]
7. If the test produced insights, add a card to Insights describing them, to feed future test ideas. [L1364.042]

### P&L and bookkeeping

#### Starting the P&L with Claude Code (Source E 2025-26)

Procedure:
1. Open Claude Code project and type "execute this project". [L0218.009]
2. Answer onboarding questions in one message: brand name, accounting software (Xero/QuickBooks/MYOB/spreadsheets), monthly revenue ballpark, and primary priority (e.g., margin visibility, ad ROI, cash forecasting). [L0218.009]
3. Review and approve the generated CFO_DECISION_LOG.md, MEMORY.md, and DATA_INVENTORY.md files. [L0218.009]
4. Drag Xero P&L export into folder. [L0218.009]
5. Ask iterative questions to improve formatting or analysis. [L0218.009]

- Claude Code is preferable to Claude Cowork for initial P&L projects. Pass the Xero transaction export and classification prompt directly to Claude Code rather than Cowork; it handles larger datasets and maintains better file organization. [L0218.019]

#### Operating expenses in the P&L

- Operating expenses are entered on a 30-day (monthly) basis, then multiplied by three to create a 90-day total that aligns with the 90-day revenue figures. [L0222.017]
- Include only fixed operating expenses, meaning costs that remain constant regardless of revenue level. Examples: employee salaries, rent, warehouse costs, subscriptions to Shopify, Klaviyo, Gorgias, and other software. Exclude variable expenses that fluctuate with sales volume. [L0222.018]
- Example monthly operating expenses: $25,000, which equals $75,000 for a 90-day period ($25,000 × 3). [L0222.019]

#### Scaling and fixed costs

- Scaling example: selling 50 products per month at 1,000 USD each gives 50,000 USD in revenue. Fixed costs stay roughly the same at about 30 USD (Shopify plus domain), so they barely increase as volume grows. [L0759.027]

#### When to upgrade bookkeeping

- Older advice from 2021-22: start with the main goal of making sales and more sales; bookkeeping should not be perfected before the business has revenue to account for. Only move to proper accounting once questions about cash flow or money in the bank become important, or once you can afford to pay someone or have the time to set it up yourself. [L1516.001]
- A practical threshold for upgrading from a DIY bookkeeping setup to professional accounting is roughly 10,000 to 20,000 in monthly revenue. The figure is in the course's currency; the German course copy states it in euros. Above that level the business is considered real and should be run more seriously. [L1516.002]
- Scaling a dropshipping business means taking on bigger problems, which need better tools and different, more advanced help than the starter setup provides. The starter tools are not wrong, they are simply not suited to a business that is just getting started. [L1516.003]
- A spreadsheet is recommended while the store has low order volume. When revenue grows, you have a bookkeeper or accountant, and the extra cost is justified, switch to a paid accounting tool such as Xero.com, which the author describes as the one used once the business had an accountant. [L1202.021]

Decide when to upgrade from a basic bookkeeping setup to proper accounting (older advice, 2021-22):
1. Upgrade once questions about where the money goes or why the bank balance does not match the P&L keep coming up. [L1516.020]
2. Upgrade when cash flow becomes important to you. [L1516.020]
3. Upgrade when you have the money to hire someone for bookkeeping, or the time to set it up yourself. [L1516.020]
4. If you are unsure, upgrade when monthly revenue reaches 10,000 to 20,000. [L1516.020]
5. Upgrade when the business moves into a growth phase with full financial systems. [L1516.020]

Until one of these applies, put the focus on getting sales. [L1516.020]

#### Who does the books (older advice, 2021-22)

- When a store grows, a fractional CFO or a better accountant can provide a finance plan and budget, and can advise on financial issues that do not matter at the starter stage. [L1516.009]
- Hiring a cheap bookkeeper from freelance marketplaces such as Upwork, Fiverr or general online job boards is risky, because poor work is often not visible for many months. A business owner without accounting skills usually cannot inspect the work, and a clean-up by experienced professionals later costs far more than good work would have. [L1516.010]
- When hiring an external bookkeeper, choose someone who has proven experience with e-commerce businesses, ideally with dropshippers, rather than an unknown freelancer. Specialists in e-commerce accounting exist, and a business owner who has run an e-commerce store themselves may offer this service. [L1516.011]
- Professional e-commerce bookkeeping typically costs at least a few hundred dollars per month, which is the minimum a reputable bookkeeping company charges for this type of work. [L1516.012]
- If the owner dislikes doing the books or lacks the time to do them, a done-for-you e-commerce bookkeeping service is a reasonable option once the business has grown. Choose a provider that specializes in e-commerce and can show it works with many dropshippers. [L1516.019]
- Bookkeeping is needed for the owner to understand performance and solve problems. The main goal of the books is clarity about what is happening in the business, not a formal record for its own sake. [L1516.015]
- Outsourcing bookkeeping to a trained VA is allowed and encouraged when the owner purchases a course, as long as the course is used in the business for the assistant's training. [L1516.014]
- Once manual tracking is established, you can delegate order profit entry to a virtual assistant. Full automation with Zapier-style automations is possible but is an advanced setup; if you are not comfortable with such tools, hire someone to build it. [L1201.003]

Budget-conscious alternative to hiring an agency or freelancer (presented as far better than hiring an unknown cheap freelancer):
1. Hire a virtual assistant who already has some bookkeeping experience. [L1516.013]
2. Give the assistant access to a DIY e-commerce bookkeeping course that the business owner has purchased or has free access to. [L1516.013]
3. Have the assistant learn the bookkeeping process from the course and apply it to the business. [L1516.013]

Options compared:

| Option | Who does the books | Cost named | Main risk named |
|---|---|---|---|
| Done-for-you e-commerce bookkeeping service | Specialist firm | At least a few hundred dollars per month | Low when the firm has proven e-commerce experience |
| Cheap freelancer from Upwork, Fiverr or online job boards | Unknown freelancer | Low | Poor work often shows only months later, and the clean-up by professionals costs a lot |
| Virtual assistant with some bookkeeping experience, trained with a DIY e-commerce bookkeeping course | Virtual assistant under the owner's supervision | Not stated | Depends on the assistant's skill and training |

[L1516.021]

#### Financial review cadence (older advice, 2021-22)

- Review the financial reports every month, because the monthly picture is what allows decisions before problems compound. Reports should be reviewed alongside the cash position, not only the sales figures. [L1046.045]
- An outside bookkeeper or advisor who knows ecommerce can translate the reports into clear actions. Where an advisor is not affordable, a self-taught bookkeeping course can also provide the same reports. [L1046.046]
- Financial analysis is built in layers: first understand the profit and loss, balance sheet and cash flow, then address cash flow, supplier and profit problems in turn. Each analysis answers some questions and raises new ones. [L1046.047]

### Profit allocation

#### Reinvest before taking profit

- Early profits from a drop shipping store should be reinvested, not taken out, so that you can work through the full cycle of finding a winner, testing, scaling, creating unique content, and eventually moving toward a private label or bulk-buying brand. Take profit out only once you have a second store running and enough experience to do so without taking a personal financial hit. [L1215.025]

#### 90-10 rule for budget and time (Source A 2024)

- Follow the 90-10 rule monthly: allocate 90% of time and budget to proven, repeatable methods that drive revenue. Allocate 10% to experimental channels or strategies. If an experiment works, move it to the proven 90%; use the freed 10% for new tests. [L0253.015]

#### Paying yourself (older advice, 2021)

Procedure:
1. Calculate your personal monthly expenses. [L0801.150]
2. Pay yourself a fixed salary from the business account, for example 500 dollars per week, once the business is running. [L0801.150]
3. At the end of each month or quarter, check the net profit left in the business. [L0801.150]
4. Decide a distribution based on goals and the profit available, for example distributing 150,000 dollars at the end of a quarter when net profit passes 200,000 dollars. [L0801.150]
5. Keep some profit in the business and avoid drawing the entire balance. [L0801.150]

- Set distributions from goals, not from what is left in the account. Tie a quarterly or monthly distribution to a target net profit so you do not overdraw the business. [L0801.151]

#### Planning sheet (older advice, 2021-22)

- A two-tab spreadsheet works for planning: one tab holds the Profit First allocations from revenue, and the other holds the strategic planning with current team, staff pay, remaining budget, and max and target capacity. The lesson used a spreadsheet for this, and the owner should update it with real numbers. [L1334.025]

### Hiring thresholds tied to revenue (older advice, 2021-22)

- A new expense can be funded by raising revenue or by raising that expense's allocation percentage. This forces a check on whether the expense, such as a software tool or a hire, is necessary. [L1200.034]
- Tie each hire or large expense to a revenue threshold, so growth is paid for before it is committed. For example, a new staff member should be added only once real revenue reaches a set level that covers their cost. [L1200.035]
- Staff share benchmarks differ by source:
  - About 35% of real revenue: observed in one established dropshipping company that used this method. [L1200.037]
  - 30%: the figure used in the lesson's example. [L1200.037]
  - Use 35% or 30% only as a reference point. The right staff percentage depends on the business's actual payroll and should be set from its own numbers. [L1200.037]

### Reviewing metrics

- Every e-commerce store should have someone who asks each month what revenue can be reached next, comparing the latest month and the upcoming quarter, so growth opportunities are not missed. [L0463.005]
- Review the changelog regularly so past experiments stay available for comparison. [L0410.001]
- Compare each change against its logged baseline and expected result, using the 90-day trailing figure. [L1509.003, L1509.004]
- Review the monthly financial reports alongside the cash position. [L1046.045]

## Preparing a store for sale or shutdown

### Making a store sellable without the owner

- A buyer acquires a business, not a job. The store must run on its own without your daily involvement, so document all critical processes in standard operating procedures and remove yourself from essential functions. [L0231.010]
- Most buyers do not want a 40-hour-a-week job. Hire a team and step out of daily operations before selling. The team also shows which tasks still need documentation. [L0554.031]
- A store that runs without the seller, with documented SOPs, training documents, and processes, is worth more, because the buyer can take over the daily work without you. [L0554.030]
- Services that depend on the seller reduce valuation. A service tied to the product can add value if a team runs it, but some buyers dislike the extra complexity. [L0554.033]
- Portfolio investors want passive income, so virtual assistants (VAs) who run the site without the owner matter a lot to them. Buyers who want to tinker will prefer a more hands-on setup. [L1468.038]
- A store can be sold while it is still early. List it with its key details and either a price or a "best offer" note in a for-sale channel so buyers can make offers. [L0360.083]

### Documentation and SOPs

- Document all processes as SOPs before listing. Clear documentation lets a buyer take over with confidence. Missing documentation makes buyers hold back money or discount the price. [L1468.037]
- SOPs and a documented, trained team (for example VAs) raise a store's attractiveness, especially to newer buyers. They can decide between a completed sale and no sale. [L0553.020]
- Document critical processes in SOPs so the buyer can run them without you. [L0231.010]

### Building and handing over the team

**Procedure: prepare a store team for sale** [L0553.022]

1. Hire and train new VAs about 3 to 6 months before selling so they can run the operations.
2. Document SOPs.
3. Hand over the trained team and processes to the buyer as part of the sale. Do not try to train after the sale closes.

- Using VAs to run day-to-day operations makes a store more attractive, because the owner does not need to be involved. This raises buyer interest and can improve the multiple. [L0553.023]
- VAs usually transfer with the business. If they do not, you must train replacement hires before closing. Buyers will contractually require you to handle the handover and may hold back part of the payment until training is complete. [L0553.021]
- You can write a sale-linked bonus into each VA's contract: each gets 1% of the profit when a site is sold. This aligns the team with the long-term value of the business. [L1437.031]

**Case: owner-dependent production** [L1468.039]

A seller's wine-glass store made about $1,000 per month in net profit. The owner said it needed about two hours per week. However, the product was hand-made by a family member working 30 to 40 hours per week. This made the business dependent on one unpaid producer who might not transfer, so a buyer would discount it.

### Contracts with staff and suppliers

- Formal written contracts with VAs and suppliers are not required to sell a business. They are helpful because they define expectations and protect both parties during the transition. [L0231.026, L0231.030]
- Contracts with staff and suppliers are not required for a sale, but they add buyer confidence. A contract may be needed before a kickback arrangement with a supplier counts as profit. [L0554.032]

### Notifying suppliers and staff

- Tell your suppliers that you are selling. Clarify what terms the new owner expects, so they understand the changes before the handover. [L0231.019]
- Inform your staff of the pending sale as early as your circumstances allow. Most buyers retain existing employees, which eases continuity concerns. [L0231.020]

### Post-signing handover

- After the asset purchase agreement is signed, the broker's team can migrate the accounts and run the handover. The level of help depends on what the buyer and seller each want. [L0553.044]
- Some sellers prefer that the broker does not touch the accounts. Less experienced sellers are guided step by step. [L0553.044]
- Cross-border handovers, such as a buyer in another country who needs a local company, are handled case by case and can take months. [L0553.044]

### Valuation factors

- A store's valuation is based on profit and success. More suppliers, more revenue, and better profit mean a higher sale price. *(Older advice, 2021.)* [L0962.136]
- A buyer will not trust a P&L that is only typed into a spreadsheet. The seller must prove the figures with proper accounting records, so accounting must be upgraded well before an exit. The higher the expected sale price, the more important this proof becomes. *(Older advice, 2021-22.)* [L1516.007]
- Dropshipping businesses can be sold for six, seven, or even eight figures, so a buyer will expect audited, verifiable financial records before paying that kind of price. *(Older advice, 2021-22.)* [L1516.008]

### Portfolio buyers

- Portfolio buyers typically set up a separate LLC for each acquisition. This compartmentalizes liability and lets them manage tax reporting independently. [L0231.025]
- Portfolio buyers want stores that keep running without the seller. They often keep the existing team and systems for a while after purchase rather than shutting everything off at once. Continuity in the first months after the sale is part of what the buyer pays for. [L0554.056]

### Sale channels and brokers

- Store sale options depend on age and profit. *(Older advice, 2021.)* [L0962.135]

| Store profile | Route |
|---|---|
| Under one year old, modest net profit | Member marketplace listing [L0962.135] |
| Over one year old, significant net profit | Offer to a broker such as Empire Flippers [L0962.135] |

- Selling a store means transferring its ownership inside Shopify. The store can be sold with or without a full supplier set. *(Older advice, 2021.)* [L0962.137]

### Closing a store instead of selling

- If a store cannot be actively managed for a long period, such as 18 months, it is better to sell it and restart later than to let it sit idle. *(Older advice, 2019.)* [L1069.074]

**Procedure: shut down a store you no longer need** [L0804.102]

1. Export all products and data with Rewind backups or the product CSV export.
2. Stop paying for the store once the export is saved.
3. Contact any supplier you approved with, thank them, and explain that the store is closing.

## Other details

### Owner role and team growth

- *Older advice (2021-22):* Growing the team multiplies positive impact by improving team members' lives through meaningful work, enhancing customer service, and creating broader community benefit. [L0107.007]
- *Older advice (2021-22):* Sustainable growth comes from repeatedly identifying and reducing weaknesses over time, not from a single fix. [L0443.007]
- *Older advice (2021-22):* Assign someone on the team to monitor the online spaces your customers use. They are a steady source of pain points, desires and market research for your avatars. [L0907.018]
- Running a store passively through a virtual assistant is not permanent. The assistant may quit or stop answering, and competitors may add bundles or redesign their sites, so the store must keep being monitored and improved. [L0826.017]
- Case: a dropshipping store started in 2014 made its first sale within a few weeks of opening, by Christmas. [L1473.036]
  - It ran as a side business while the owner worked as a bookkeeper, and it helped pay off about 70,000 in debt built up with a husband from buying a house, in just under two years. [L1473.036]
  - After the owner quit the bookkeeping job, the store tripled its revenue in the first full-time year. [L1473.036]
  - The owner still worked about 50 hours a week on customer service, orders and fires, and only removed themselves from business operations in 2018, about four years after starting. [L1473.036]
  - The turning point was a pregnancy in 2017. The owner built the business system and found a virtual assistant within about three months. [L1473.036]
  - Training lasted two weeks, after which customer service and order management were handed over. The owner later could travel for months while the business ran, working on the business rather than in it. [L1473.036]
  - The owner later hired a second assistant and raised pay after one year. [L1473.036]

### Delegation and done-for-you services

- Done-for-you services are listed at the bottom of the members area page and can be booked at any time. [L1496.014]
  - Most of them carry an extra fee and are optional. [L1496.014]
  - They suit those who want faster progress or who dislike tasks such as website design, product uploads or market verification. [L1496.014]
- Done-for-you store building typically takes two to five business days, depending on team workload. Ask for the team's current turnaround before committing. [L0806.095]

### Working setup and tools

- Use a screen split or external monitor for supplier and product research so you do not switch between windows constantly, which saves time. [L0665.110]
- Do not open a normal chat or create a new Cowork task without first selecting the project. Work started outside the project does not get the project's files, instructions or memory. [L1506.013]
- Downloaded course files belong in one dedicated folder inside the project location. You can then drag them into a chat to install skills or to reference them. [L1506.014]

### Business identity and accounts

- Use the exact same email address for the Google Merchant Center account, Google Analytics, Google Ads and the other Google products. This lets Google link them and confirm domain ownership automatically. [L0728.001]
- Set the same time zone across all Google products so data shows consistently everywhere. [L0728.001]
- *Older advice (2021):* Use an existing store's customer support email, at the store's own domain, for business email. Avoid email from another business domain. [L0834.107]

### Store build and technical checks

- *Older advice (2021-22):* When implementing image optimization, start with the high-priority pages and products you want to rank most, then gradually work through lower-priority pages. This staged approach makes the task manageable without needing to optimize everything at once. [L0301.031]
- *Older advice (2021-22):* Clicking through every link on a page by hand is too time consuming on pages with dozens of outgoing links. Use a link checker extension or outsource the scan instead. [L0332.022]
- Build single-product stores, add relatable products at the bottom of the page, and use upsells in the cart as a way to grow average order value. [L0577.064]

### Offers and average order value

- Quantity discounts (buy one, buy two, buy three) can outperform a complex bundle for a first test. [L0773.041]
  - They are simple to set up. [L0773.041]
  - They lift average order value without needing to pick related products. [L0773.041]
- *Older advice (2021-22):* Consistency drives upsell results. Keep offering the upsells across all three channels every time, rather than trying once and stopping. [L1409.027]
- *Older advice (2021-22):* Upsell action items: [L1409.029]
  1. Decide which on-site upsell positions to use (product page, cart page, post-purchase).
  2. If no post-purchase upsell exists yet, add one; keep existing product-page upsells and add later upsells in the sequence.
  3. Add an upsell email sequence for each core product from the planner list into your email system.
  4. Customize the email templates with your specific products.
  5. Pass the script and instructions to the customer service team and discuss the planner list with them, so they offer the related upsell when a customer calls about a core product.

### Product research and validation

- Find someone already achieving the results you want, then model their process instead of reinventing it. Starting from a proven model can compress years of trial and error into a short period. [L0523.044]
  - Test and improve the model only after it makes money. [L0523.044]
- Product review workflow used by the team: [L0530.027]
  1. Watch the TikTok video and judge whether it is good.
  2. Read the comments for buying intent.
  3. Check the seller's account for upload dates and posting consistency.
  4. Open the store on mobile and desktop and review structure, product page and payment options.
  5. Estimate cost and selling price.
  6. Score the product and decide whether to test it.
- Spending more does not beat a competitor's higher budget if your targeting is sharper. [L0664.066]
  - *Older advice (2020):* A focused store with a few thousand dollars a month can outperform a competitor spending tens of thousands by cutting weak products. [L0664.066]
- Roughly 80% of sales often come from about 20% of products. Focus effort on identifying and adding the few suppliers whose products drive most of the revenue. [L0835.125]
- Validation and profit timing:
  - A store that passes all validation tests can usually become profitable. [L0806.092]
  - How large it grows (hundreds of thousands, millions or more in annual sales) is learned over the first year or two. [L0806.092]
  - Profit does not appear in the first week or the first six months. [L0806.092]

### Competitor signals

- When a competitor switches off a large number of active ads, for example 22 to 30 ads after about three weeks of running, this can signal a stock problem around the holiday rather than a failed product. [L0694.014]
  - If the product meets your criteria, the wait for stock may be worth it. [L0694.014]

### Product range and rebranding

- Launch a new product regularly to give existing customers a reason to buy again. [L0790.083]
  - The target was one new product per month, though one per quarter may be enough in some niches. [L0790.083]
- Keep a stable store with the same products over time. A single winner can keep a business running, and the goal is to keep the store alive by reselling and upgrading the best product, not chasing one-off hits. [L1440.052]
- Rebrand a store with a mixed product line: [L0806.094]
  1. Remove the products that do not fit the target niche.
  2. Change the domain name and logo in Shopify.
  3. Keep the remaining products and rename the store to match the niche (for example a carport store).
  4. Keep the removed products only for a future second store if there are enough suppliers to justify it.

### Sub-niches and multi-niche stores

- *Older advice (2022):* Use the same workflow for multiple sub-niches. Build keyword silos for each sub-niche in one store rather than separate stores. [L0665.107]
  - One store is easier to rank with one keyword silo. [L0665.107]

### Launch sequence and growth

- *Older advice (2021):* Start paid traffic and suppliers right away instead of waiting to build content first. Make money from paid traffic, then reinvest that money into content and SEO. [L0801.158]
- *Older advice (2022):* A store can grow to seven-figure revenue if it stays focused on one niche, becomes a leading store there, and keeps scaling ad budgets. [L0803.074]
  - The main limit is how far the owner pushes, not the product. [L0803.074]
- *Older advice (2021-22):* A customer multiplier funnel consists of about six to seven main actions plus some smaller supporting actions. [L0482.004]
  - The main actions work best when they all run together, one after another, to move customers toward a second purchase or toward new customers. [L0482.004]
- *Older advice (2021-22):* Follow-up is the practice of keeping prospects who visited but did not buy, and customers who already bought, engaged so they move toward a second or third purchase. [L1497.001]
  - Its value builds over time as traffic sources bring in many top- and middle-of-funnel visitors. [L1497.001]
- *Older advice (2021-22):* High-ticket stores need far fewer orders than low-ticket stores for the same profit. [L0771.052]
  - Five saunas a day can be fulfilled by one owner in a few hours. [L0771.052]
  - Equal profit from 40 USD items needs hundreds of orders and a team. [L0771.052]

### Copy, direct mail and product arguments

- *Older advice... no year given:* When one argument works for one buyer group, apply it to every group with the same need. For example, use it for all gas companies within several hundred miles, then for all stove makers within 500 miles. [L1369.107]
  - Expanding a proven argument to new segments is faster than inventing a new one. [L1369.107]
- Test changes to the product itself, such as the screening of coal, the cooking time of coke or the size of oven charges, to stay ahead of competitors. [L1369.109]
  - Improving the product keeps the argument for it strong over time. [L1369.109]
- Study proven direct mail and letter-writing references and apply their principles to your own mailings on a regular basis. [L1369.110]
  - A systematic study routine produces better results than relying on instinct alone. [L1369.110]

### Store updates and course materials

- A major update to the outsourcing and automation module was in preparation, replacing all existing trainings with new strategies and tools. [L0373.015]
  - Existing material still works. [L0373.015]
  - Users who want the newest tools should wait about one week before going through that module. [L0373.015]
- Store updates in the training are published in one place, so the member login always shows the latest version. [L1283.026]
  - Announce significant updates in the team announcement channel with a link to the changed lesson. [L1283.026]

### Owner income and reinvestment

- An online business can be scaled far beyond a salary because revenue is not tied to hours worked, and it can run from any location. [L0863.009]
  - This makes it a stronger base for reinvesting income into other assets than a fixed-salary job. [L0863.009]
- A large share of business income can be reinvested. The lesson cites 80% of earnings invested from 2013 until COVID. [L0863.011]
  - The reinvestment should be spread across diversified assets such as index funds and stable stocks, with only a small part in speculative assets. [L0863.011]

### Income beyond the own stores

- Monthly income for a freelancer who serves multiple dropshipping stores = monthly fee per client × number of clients. Example: 150 dollars × 10 clients = 1,500 dollars per month. [L0910.054]
- *Older advice (2021-22):* Skills gained from running your own store, such as paid traffic management, can be sold as a service to other store owners who will pay for them. [L1014.010]
  - Early client work of this kind can grow into a separate income stream alongside the own stores. [L1014.010]
- *Older advice (2021-22):* When the same questions and problems keep coming from clients, package the answers into a structured course or program instead of repeating one-on-one advice. [L1014.011]
  - Courses can deliver the same knowledge to more people in less time, and one-on-one coaching can continue alongside. [L1014.011]

### Personal environment and idea generation

- Being in a different environment or culture, even within your own country, shifts how your brain works and generates new ideas. Visit other places, meet people who do things differently, and bring back what you learn. [L1343.033]

### Case studies: store design and launch

- Case: a jewelry store was the sixth product tested after several earlier products without a winner, and it was launched when buyers wanted to show identity. [L1131.057]
  - Ads pointed to a collection page with about 20 designs of an African map necklace, priced at 30 with a buy-one-get-second-free offer, so two cost 30 instead of 60. [L1131.057]
  - A later collection of African country necklaces let buyers choose their country free with the map necklace. [L1131.057]
  - The store made about 60,000 in revenue within two months. [L1131.057]
  - The store was closed after two months because the operator lacked ads experience. Money went into unprofitable ads, the daily budget was raised from 5 to 100 dollars in one step, which got the ad account blocked, and negative comments were left unanswered, which led to the Facebook page being blocked. [L1131.057]
- Case: a student's store sold a mix of unrelated products, including hair wax, pet grooming gloves, men's jewelry, pet accessories, hats, car accessories and a bathroom apron. Its traffic came from a Facebook ad for a baby hammock sleeping bed. [L1346.034]
  - The homepage showed unrelated lifestyle photos. The product pages used default featured images and had no reviews, no urgency, no estimated delivery dates, no live chat link and no social media accounts. [L1346.034]
  - The reviewer judged the store to look like a burner store. [L1346.034]
  - Recommended changes: choose one product type, theme the site around it, replace the images, add a review system, scarcity counts, urgency offers, estimated delivery, a live chat link, tabbed product pages, a fuller footer and active social media accounts. [L1346.034]
  - The lesson gives no sales figures or results for the store. [L1346.034]
